Chain level loop bracket and pseudo-holomorphic disks
Kei Irie

Abstract
. Let L \displaystyle L be a Lagrangian submanifold in a symplectic vector space which is closed, oriented and spin. Using virtual fundamental chains of moduli spaces of nonconstant pseudo-holomorphic disks with boundaries on L \displaystyle L , one can define a Maurer-Cartan element of a Lie bracket operation in string topology (the loop bracket) defined at chain level. This observation is due to Fukaya, who also pointed out its important consequences in symplectic topology. The goal of this paper is to work out details of this observation. Our argument is based on a string topology chain model previously introduced by the author, and the theory of Kuranishi structures on moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic disks, which has been developed by Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono.



Link to an erratum for the published version

Kei Irie

1.
Introduction

The study of Lagrangian submanifolds is one of central topics in symplectic topology.
In the monumental paper
[
11
]
,
Gromov proved that the first Betti number of
a closed Lagrangian submanifold in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
(with the standard symplectic structure) is nonzero,
using moduli spaces of (perturbed) pseudo-holomorphic disks with boundaries on the Lagrangian submanifold.
On the other hand, string topology is the study of algebraic structures on (homology of) loop spaces,
introduced by Chas-Sullivan
[
1
]
.

In this paper we discuss an application of
chain level
string topology operations to the pseudo-holomorphic curve theory in symplectic topology.
This idea is due to Fukaya
[
6
]
, who also pointed out its important consequences,
including a proof of Audin’s conjecture for (closed, oriented and spin) aspherical Lagrangian submanifolds in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
,
and a complete classification of
orientable, closed, prime three-manifolds admitting Lagrangian embeddings into
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
.

Let us briefly sketch the key argument in
[
6
]
.
Let
\displaystyle L
be a closed, oriented and spin Lagrangian submanifold in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}L:=C^{\infty}(S^{1},L)
be the space of free loops on
\displaystyle L
.
Let
\displaystyle D:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid|z|\leq 1\}
,
and let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}
denote the (compactified) moduli space of nonconstant holomorphic maps

\displaystyle(D,\partial D)\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
modulo
\displaystyle\text{Aut}(D,1)
.
Then one can define a map
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{{\mathcal{M}}}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{L}}L
by
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{{\mathcal{M}}}(u):=u|_{\partial D}

(strictly speaking, this “definition” has an ambiguity up to parametrizations of loops, however we omit this issue for the moment).
Considering virtual fundamental chain of the moduli space
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}
,
the pair
\displaystyle({\mathcal{M}},\text{\rm ev}\,_{{\mathcal{M}}})
defines a “chain”
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{C}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}L)
.
Here
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}L)
denotes the complex of “chains” on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}L
,
on which the
loop bracket
is defined and makes
\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}L)
a dg Lie algebra.
Since the codimension
\displaystyle 1
boundary of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}
consists of configurations of two disks glued at a point, one sees that
the chain
\displaystyle x
satisfies the
Maurer-Cartan equation

(1)

\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0

where
\displaystyle[\,,\,]
denotes the loop bracket defined at
chain level
.
Nextly, we take a time-dependent Hamiltonian
\displaystyle H
on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
which displaces
\displaystyle L
,
and define a moduli space
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}
which consists of solutions of the Cauchy-Riemann equation perturbed by
\displaystyle H
.
Then, the associated chain
\displaystyle y:=({\mathcal{N}},\text{\rm ev}\,_{{\mathcal{N}}})\in{\mathcal{C}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}L)
satisfies

(2)

\partial y-[x,y]=z

where
\displaystyle z
is another chain whose “symplectic area zero part”
is a cycle representing the fundamental class of
\displaystyle L
.
Once we obtain chains
\displaystyle x
,
\displaystyle y
,
\displaystyle z
satisfying equations (
1
) and (
2
),
using the homotopy transfer theorem for
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras,
one can formulate an equivalent result on homology of the free loop space (Theorem
3.2
).
This result has the following remarkable consequences:

(i):

If
\displaystyle L
is aspherical, then
there exists
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
with Maslov index
\displaystyle 2
and positive symplectic action
(Corollary
3.5
).

(ii):

If
\displaystyle n=3
and
\displaystyle L
is prime as a three-manifold, then
\displaystyle L
is diffeomorphic to
\displaystyle S^{1}
times a closed surface
(Corollary
3.6
).

(i) in particular confirms Audin’s conjecture for Lagrangian tori in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
,
and (ii) gives
a complete classification of
orientable, closed, prime three-manifolds admitting Lagrangian embeddings into
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
;
see Section 3 for details and previous related works.

In the above argument,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}L)

denotes the complex of “chains” on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}L
,
on which the loop bracket is defined and makes it a dg Lie algebra.
It is a highly nontrivial technical problem to define such chain models of the free loop space, in particular those compatible with virtual techniques in the pseudo-holomorphic curve theory.
Partly due to this issue,
in spite of the importance of its consequences,
full details of the above argument have not been available so far.

In
[
12
]
, the author developed foundations for part of chain level algebraic structures (specifically, Batalin-Vilkovisky structure) in string topology,
using de Rham chains on spaces of Moore loops with arbitrarily many marked points.
The goal of this paper is to combine techniques from
[
12
]
with the theory of Kuranishi structures
[
10
]

to work out details of the argument sketched above.

Now we describe the structure of this paper.
The goal of the first part (Sections 2–6) is to state the main result and introduce our setup in string topology.
Section 2 explains some preliminaries on
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras, in particular the homotopy transfer theorem.
Section 3 states the main result (Theorem
3.2
) on homology of the free loop space.
We also recall a few applications in symplectic topology from
[
6
]
.
In Sections 4–6, we introduce our setup in string topology, following
[
12
]
with minor modifications,
and reduce Theorem
3.2
to a chain-level statement (Theorem
6.1
).
Further details will be explained in the last paragraph of Section 3.

The goal of the second part (Sections 7–10) is to prove Theorem
6.1
.
The plan of the proof will be explained at the beginning of Section 7.
Our proof uses the theory of Kuranishi structures on moduli spaces of (perturbed) pseudo-holomorphic disks.
In particular, our arguments heavily rely on
[
10
]
by Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono.
Section 10 very briefly explains some notions in the theory of Kuranishi structures, mainly to fix notations.

Conventions.

Throughout this paper all manifolds are assumed to be of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
.
All vector spaces are over
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}
, unless otherwise specified.

Acknowledgements.

The author appreciates Kenji Fukaya for sharing his time and insights into virtual techniques in
the pseudo-holomorphic curve theory,
and his comments on an early version of this paper.
The author also appreciates the Simons Center for Geometry and Physics for a great work environment.
This work is supported by JSPS Postdoctoral Fellowship for Research Abroad.

2.
Preliminaries on
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras

We briefly recall basics of
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras, partially following
[
14
]
.

2.1.
Bar construction

Let
\displaystyle C=\bigoplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}C_{i}
be a
\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}\,
-graded vector space.
For every integer
\displaystyle k\geq 1
,
let
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}\,_{k}
denote the
\displaystyle k
-th symmetric group,
and
let us define an
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}\,_{k}
-action on
\displaystyle C^{\otimes k}
by

\rho\cdot(c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{k}):=\varepsilon(\rho:c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\cdot c_{\rho(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{\rho(k)}

where
\displaystyle\varepsilon(\rho:c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}):=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i<j\\
\rho(i)>\rho(j)\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|c_{i}||c_{j}|}
.
Let
\displaystyle S^{k}C
denote the quotient of
\displaystyle C^{\otimes k}
by the
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}\,_{k}
-action,
and set
\displaystyle SC:=\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq\infty}S^{k}C
.

We define a coproduct
\displaystyle\Delta:SC\to SC^{\otimes 2}
by

\Delta(c_{1}\ldots c_{k}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
\rho\in\mathbb{S}\,_{k}\end{subarray}}\frac{\varepsilon(\rho:c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})}{k_{1}!k_{2}!}\cdot c_{\rho(1)}\cdots c_{\rho(k_{1})}\otimes c_{\rho(k_{1}+1)}\cdots c_{\rho(k)}.

Then
\displaystyle\Delta
is coassociative, namely
\displaystyle(1\otimes\Delta)\circ\Delta=(\Delta\otimes 1)\circ\Delta
.
We denote
\displaystyle\Delta
by
\displaystyle\Delta_{C}
when we need to specify
\displaystyle C
.

2.2.
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras and
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-homomorphisms

For any
\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vector space
\displaystyle C
and
\displaystyle n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
we define a shifted complex
\displaystyle C[n]
by

\displaystyle C[n]_{d}:=C_{n+d}\,(\forall d\in{\mathbb{Z}})
.

Definition 2.1
.

(i):

An
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebra is a pair of a graded vector space
\displaystyle C
and
a linear map
\displaystyle l:S(C[-1])\to S(C[-1])
such that

•

\displaystyle|l|=-1
.

•

\displaystyle l
is a coderivation;
\displaystyle\Delta\circ l=(l\otimes 1+1\otimes l)\circ\Delta
.

•

\displaystyle l^{2}=0
.

For each integer
\displaystyle k\geq 1
, we define
\displaystyle l_{k}:S^{k}(C[-1])\to C[-1]
by

\displaystyle l_{k}:=\text{\rm pr}_{1}\circ l|_{S^{k}(C[-1])}
, where

\displaystyle\text{\rm pr}_{1}:S(C[-1])\to S^{1}(C[-1])\cong C[-1]

denotes the projection.

(ii):

Let
\displaystyle(C,l)
and
\displaystyle(C^{\prime},l^{\prime})
be
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras.
An
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-homomorphism from
\displaystyle(C,l)
to
\displaystyle(C^{\prime},l^{\prime})
is a linear map

\displaystyle f:S(C[-1])\to S(C^{\prime}[-1])
such that

•

\displaystyle|f|=0
.

•

\displaystyle f
is a coalgebra map;
\displaystyle\Delta_{C^{\prime}[-1]}\circ f=(f\otimes f)\circ\Delta_{C[-1]}
.

•

\displaystyle\l^{\prime}\circ f=f\circ l
.

For each integer
\displaystyle k\geq 1
, we define
\displaystyle f_{k}:S^{k}(C[-1])\to C^{\prime}[-1]
by

\displaystyle f_{k}:=\text{\rm pr}_{1}\circ f|_{S^{k}(C[-1])}
.

Here is another definition of
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras and
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-homomorphisms using exterior products.
For each integer
\displaystyle k\geq 1
, let
\displaystyle\Lambda^{k}C
denote the quotient of
\displaystyle C^{\otimes k}
by the
\displaystyle\mathbb{S}\,_{k}
-action defined by

\rho\cdot(c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{k}):=\text{\rm sgn}(\rho)\cdot\varepsilon(\rho:c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})\cdot c_{\rho(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{\rho(k)}.

Then there exists a natural isomorphism

\sigma_{k}:(\Lambda^{k}C)[-k]\to S^{k}(C[-1]);\quad c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{k}\mapsto(-1)^{\sum_{i}(k-i)|c_{i}|}c_{1}\cdots c_{k}.

We define
\displaystyle\lambda_{k}:\Lambda^{k}C\to C
by
\displaystyle\lambda_{k}:=\sigma_{1}^{-1}\circ l_{k}\circ\sigma_{k}
.

Then one can define an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-structure on
\displaystyle C
as a sequence
\displaystyle(\lambda_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
each
\displaystyle\lambda_{k}:\Lambda^{k}C\to C
is of degree
\displaystyle k-2
and satisfies the equation

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
\rho\in\mathbb{S}\,_{k}\end{subarray}}\pm\frac{1}{k_{1}!(k-k_{1})!}\lambda_{k_{2}}(\lambda_{k_{1}}(c_{\rho(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{\rho(k_{1})})\wedge c_{\rho(k_{1}+1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{\rho(k)})=0

where
\displaystyle\pm
stands for appropriate signs.
Similarly, one can define an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-homomorphism from
\displaystyle(C,(\lambda_{k})_{k})
to
\displaystyle(C^{\prime},(\lambda^{\prime}_{k})_{k})
as a sequence
\displaystyle(\varphi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that
each
\displaystyle\varphi_{k}:\Lambda^{k}C\to C^{\prime}
is of degree
\displaystyle k-1
and satisfies the equation

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
\rho\in\mathbb{S}\,_{k}\end{subarray}}\pm\frac{1}{k_{2}!(k_{1}-1)!}\cdot\varphi_{k_{1}}(\lambda_{k_{2}}(c_{\rho(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{\rho(k_{2})})\wedge c_{\rho(k_{2}+1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{\rho(k)})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+\cdots+k_{r}=k\\
\rho\in\mathbb{S}\,_{k}\end{subarray}}\pm\frac{1}{r!k_{1}!\cdots k_{r}!}\cdot\lambda^{\prime}_{r}(\varphi_{k_{1}}(c_{\rho(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{\rho(k_{1})})\wedge\cdots\wedge\varphi_{k_{r}}(c_{\rho(k-k_{r}+1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{\rho(k)}))

where
\displaystyle\pm
stands for appropriate signs.

Remark 2.2
.

For later purposes we need to specify signs for dg Lie algebras.
A dg Lie algebra is an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebra such that
\displaystyle\lambda_{k}=0
for every
\displaystyle k\geq 3
.
Setting
\displaystyle\partial x:=\lambda_{1}(x)
and
\displaystyle[x,y]:=\lambda_{2}(x,y)
, there holds

\displaystyle\displaystyle[x,y]+(-1)^{|x||y|}[y,x]=0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial[x,y]=[\partial x,y]+(-1)^{|x|}[x,\partial y],

\displaystyle\displaystyle[x,[y,z]]+(-1)^{|x|(|y|+|z|)}[y,[z,x]]+(-1)^{|z|(|x|+|y|)}[z,[x,y]]=0.

We also introduce the following notions for later purposes.

Definition 2.3
.

(i):

Let
\displaystyle V
be a vector space and
\displaystyle A
be a commutative semigroup.
A decomposition of
\displaystyle V
over
\displaystyle A
is a decomposition
\displaystyle V=\bigoplus_{a\in A}V(a)
,
where each
\displaystyle V(a)
is a subspace of
\displaystyle V
,
and
\displaystyle V(a)+V(a^{\prime})\subset V(a+a^{\prime})
for every
\displaystyle a,a^{\prime}\in A
.

(ii):

Let
\displaystyle V
and
\displaystyle W
be vector spaces with decompositions over
\displaystyle A
.
A linear map
\displaystyle f:V^{\otimes k}\to W
respects these decompositions if

f(V(a_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes V(a_{k}))\subset W(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k})

for every
\displaystyle a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}\in A
.

(iii):

An
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebra structure

\displaystyle l=(l_{k})_{k}
on
\displaystyle V
respects the decomposition of
\displaystyle V

if
\displaystyle l_{k}
respects the decomposition for every
\displaystyle k\geq 1
.

2.3.
Homotopy transfer theorem

Finally we state the homotopy transfer theorem for
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebras.
The proof is only sketched since it is now standard
(perhaps goes back to the paper by Kadeishvili
[
13
]
on
\displaystyle A_{\infty}
-algebras).

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\displaystyle(C,l)
be an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebra, and

\displaystyle H(C):=\text{\rm Ker}\,l_{1}/\text{\rm Im}\,l_{1}
.
Suppose that there are linear maps

\iota:H_{*}(C)\to C_{*},\quad\pi:C_{*}\to H_{*}(C),\quad\kappa:C_{*}\to C_{*+1}

such that

l_{1}\circ\iota=0,\quad\pi\circ l_{1}=0,\quad\pi\circ\iota=\text{\rm id}_{H(C)},\quad\text{\rm id}_{C}-\iota\circ\pi=l_{1}\circ\kappa+\kappa\circ l_{1}.

Then there exist
an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebra structure
\displaystyle l^{H}
on
\displaystyle H(C)

and an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-homomorphism
\displaystyle p:(C,l)\to(H(C),l^{H})
,
such that
\displaystyle l^{H}_{1}=0
and
\displaystyle p_{1}=\pi
.

When
\displaystyle C
has a decomposition over a commutative semigroup
\displaystyle A

(thus
\displaystyle H(C)
also has a decomposition over
\displaystyle A
),
and linear maps
\displaystyle\iota
,
\displaystyle\pi
and
\displaystyle\kappa
respect these decompositions,
then one can take
\displaystyle l^{H}
and
\displaystyle p
so that they respect the decompositions over
\displaystyle A
.

Proof
.

It is sufficient to define sequences
\displaystyle(l^{H}_{\leq k})_{k\geq 1}
and
\displaystyle(p_{\leq k})_{k\geq 1}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\displaystyle p_{\leq k}
is a coalgebra map of degree
\displaystyle 0
from
\displaystyle S(C[-1])
to
\displaystyle S(H[-1])
which respects decompositions over
\displaystyle A
.

•

\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k}
is a coderivation of degree
\displaystyle-1
from
\displaystyle S(H[-1])
to
\displaystyle S(H[-1])
which respects decompositions over
\displaystyle A
.

•

\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k}\circ l^{H}_{\leq k}=0
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k+1}(H[-1]):=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq k+1}S^{i}(H[-1])
.

•

\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k}\circ p_{\leq k}-p_{\leq k}\circ l=0
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k}(C[-1]):=\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq k}S^{i}(C[-1])
.

•

\displaystyle p_{\leq k}=p_{\leq k+1}
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k}(C[-1])
.

•

\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k}=l^{H}_{\leq k+1}
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k}(H[-1])
.

•

\displaystyle p_{\leq 1}=\pi
,
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq 1}=0
.

Once we obtain these sequences, the limits

\displaystyle p:=\lim_{k\to\infty}p_{\leq k}
and

\displaystyle l^{H}:=\lim_{k\to\infty}l^{H}_{\leq k}

satisfy the conditions in the theorem.

We can define
\displaystyle(l^{H}_{\leq k})_{k\geq 1}
and
\displaystyle(p_{\leq k})_{k\geq 1}
by upward induction on
\displaystyle k
.
For
\displaystyle k=1
,
\displaystyle p_{\leq 1}
and
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq 1}
are defined by the last condition.
We assume that we have defined
\displaystyle p_{\leq k}
,
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k}
and
are going to define

\displaystyle p_{\leq k+1}
,
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k+1}
.
Let
\displaystyle\text{\rm Hom}_{A}(S^{k+1}C,H)
denote the space of linear maps from
\displaystyle S^{k+1}C
to
\displaystyle H
preserving decompositions over
\displaystyle A
. Namely:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm Hom}_{A}(S^{k+1}C,H)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\{f\in\text{\rm Hom}(S^{k+1}C,H)\mid

\displaystyle\displaystyle f(C(a_{1})\cdots C(a_{k+1}))\subset H(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k+1})\,(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k+1}\in A)\}.

We define a boundary operator
\displaystyle\partial
on
\displaystyle\text{\rm Hom}(S^{k+1}C,H)
by
\displaystyle\partial f:=f\circ l_{1}
,
then the homology is isomorphic to
\displaystyle\text{\rm Hom}_{A}(S^{k+1}H,H)
.
Our induction assumption shows that
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k}\circ p_{\leq k}-p_{\leq k}\circ l
is a cycle in
\displaystyle\text{\rm Hom}_{A}(S^{k+1}C,H)
, thus one can define

l^{H}_{k+1}:=[p_{\leq k}\circ l-l^{H}_{\leq k}\circ p_{\leq k}]\in\text{\rm Hom}_{A}(S^{k+1}H,H).

Then

\displaystyle l^{H}_{k+1}\circ p_{\leq 1}+l^{H}_{\leq k}\circ p_{\leq k}-p_{\leq k}\circ l

is a null-homologous cycle, thus there exists

\displaystyle p_{k+1}\in\text{\rm Hom}_{A}(S^{k+1}C,H)
such that

p_{k+1}\circ l_{1}=l^{H}_{k+1}\circ p_{\leq 1}+l^{H}_{\leq k}\circ p_{\leq k}-p_{\leq k}\circ l.

Then we define a coderivation
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k+1}
so that
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k+1}=l^{H}_{\leq k}
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k}H
, and

l^{H}_{\leq k+1}|_{S^{i}H\to H}=\begin{cases}l^{H}_{k+1}&(i=k+1)\\
0&(i>k+1).\end{cases}

Similarly, we define a coalgebra map
\displaystyle p_{\leq k+1}
so that
\displaystyle p_{\leq k+1}=p_{\leq k}
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k}C
, and

p_{\leq k+1}|_{S^{i}C\to H}=\begin{cases}p_{k+1}&(i=k+1)\\
0&(i>k+1).\end{cases}

It is easy to check
\displaystyle l^{H}_{\leq k+1}\circ l^{H}_{\leq k+1}=0
on
\displaystyle S^{\leq k+2}H
.
∎

3.
Main result

We state the main result (Theorem
3.2
) and
explain a few applications to symplectic topology of Lagrangian submanifolds.
Let
\displaystyle\omega_{n}
denote the standard symplectic form on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
, namely
\displaystyle\omega_{n}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}dx_{j}\wedge dy_{j}
,
and
\displaystyle L
be a Lagrangian submanifold in
\displaystyle({\mathbb{C}}^{n},\omega_{n})
.
We assume that
\displaystyle L
is closed (compact and
\displaystyle\partial L=\emptyset
),
connected, oriented and spin.
Let
\displaystyle\mu\in H^{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
denote the Maslov class.
Since
\displaystyle L
is oriented,
\displaystyle\mu(H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}}))\subset 2{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Let us explicitly define the Maslov class
\displaystyle\mu
as follows.
Let
\displaystyle\Lambda(n):=U(n)/O(n)
denote the unoriented Lagrangian Grassmannian,
and consider maps

\tau:L\to\Lambda(n);\,x\mapsto T_{x}L,\qquad{\det}^{2}:\Lambda(n)=U(n)/O(n)\to U(1).

Then we define
\displaystyle\mu:=(\text{det}^{2}\circ\tau)^{*}[U(1)]
,
where
\displaystyle U(1)
is identified with
\displaystyle\{e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta}|\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}\}
,
and
\displaystyle[U(1)]
is defined as
\displaystyle[U(1)]:=[d\theta]/2\pi
.

Let
\displaystyle S^{1}:={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}L:=C^{\infty}(S^{1},L)
.
We will often abbreviate
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}L
by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}
.
For every
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
, we set

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(a):=\{\gamma\in{\mathcal{L}}\mid[\gamma]=a\}
.
Obviously
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}=\bigsqcup_{a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})}{\mathcal{L}}(a)
.
For each
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
,
we consider the
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-topology on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(a)
and set

H^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)_{*}:=H^{\text{\rm sing}}_{*+n+\mu(a)-1}({\mathcal{L}}(a):{\mathbb{R}}),

where the RHS is the singular homology with respect to the
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-topology on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(a)

(in the following we often omit the superscript “
sing
” ).
Now we consider the direct product

H^{\mathcal{L}}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})}H^{\mathcal{L}}(a)_{*}

equipped with the energy filtration;
for each
\displaystyle E\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we set

F^{E}H^{\mathcal{L}}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})>E}H^{\mathcal{L}}(a)_{*}

where
\displaystyle\bar{a}
denotes the unique element in
\displaystyle H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
satisfying
\displaystyle\partial\bar{a}=a
.
Finally
\displaystyle\widehat{H}^{\mathcal{L}}_{*}
denotes the completion by the energy filtration:

\widehat{H}^{\mathcal{L}}_{*}:=\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}H^{\mathcal{L}}_{*}/F^{E}H^{\mathcal{L}}_{*}.

Now let us state the main result of this paper:

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\displaystyle L
be a Lagrangian submanifold in
\displaystyle({\mathbb{C}}^{n},\omega_{n})

which is closed, oriented and spin.
Then, there exist
an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-structure
\displaystyle(l^{H}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
on
\displaystyle H^{\mathcal{L}}
and

\displaystyle X\in\widehat{H}^{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
,

\displaystyle Y\in\widehat{H}^{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
,
satisfying the following conditions:

(i):

\displaystyle l^{H}_{1}=0
.

(ii):

The
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-structure
\displaystyle(l^{H}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
respects the decomposition of
\displaystyle H^{\mathcal{L}}
over
\displaystyle H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
.
In particular, the
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-structure extends to the completion
\displaystyle\widehat{H}^{\mathcal{L}}
.

(iii):

There exists
\displaystyle c>0
such that
\displaystyle X\in F^{c}\widehat{H}^{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
.

(iv):

\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle Y
satisfy the following equations:

(3)

\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{1}{k!}l^{H}_{k}(X,\ldots,X)=0,

(4)

\biggl(\sum_{k\geq 2}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}l^{H}_{k}(Y,X,\ldots,X)\biggr)_{a=0}=(-1)^{n+1}[L].

Note that infinite sums in the LHS make sense by the condition (iii).

\displaystyle[L]
in the RHS of (
4
) denotes the image of the fundamental class
\displaystyle[L]\in H_{n}(L:{\mathbb{R}})

by the embedding map
\displaystyle H_{*}(L:{\mathbb{R}})\to H_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(0):{\mathbb{R}})

which is induced by

L\to{\mathcal{L}}(0);\quad x\mapsto\text{constant loop at $\displaystyle x$}.

Remark 3.3
.

It will be possible to show that
\displaystyle l^{H}_{2}
coincides (up to sign) with the Chas-Sullivan loop bracket
[
1
]
,
and the full
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-algebra structure is homotopy equivalent to
the dg Lie algebra defined by the chain level loop bracket in
[
12
]
.
However, we do not give complete proofs of these claims in this paper.

Remark 3.4
.

If
\displaystyle l^{H}_{k}=0
for
\displaystyle k\geq 3
, assuming that
\displaystyle l^{H}_{2}
coincides with the loop bracket up to sign,
(
4
) implies
\displaystyle[Y(-a),X(a)]\neq 0
for some
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
.
When
\displaystyle L
is diffeomorphic to
\displaystyle S^{1}\times S^{2}
, this equation implies nonvanishing of the Maslov class
\displaystyle\mu
,
contradicting a result by Ekholm-Eliashberg-Murphy-Smith (Corollary 1.6 in
[
4
]
).
Therefore, if
\displaystyle L
is diffeomorphic to
\displaystyle S^{1}\times S^{2}
, there exists at least one nonvanishing higher term in the LHS of (
4
).

Let us quickly recall two applications of Theorem
3.2
from
[
6
]
.
For further results, consult the original paper
[
6
]
.

Corollary 3.5
.

Suppose that
\displaystyle L
is aspherical. Then there exists
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
such that
\displaystyle\mu(a)=2
,

\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\bar{a})>0
and
\displaystyle H_{n}({\mathcal{L}}(a):{\mathbb{R}})\neq 0
.

Proof
.

By the equation (
4
),
there exist
\displaystyle a_{1},\ldots,a_{k-1}\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
such that

l^{H}_{k}(Y(-(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k-1})),X(a_{1}),\ldots,X(a_{k-1}))\neq 0.

On the other hand, the assumption that
\displaystyle L
is aspherical implies that

\displaystyle H_{i}({\mathcal{L}})\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle 0\leq i\leq n
(Lemma 12.11 in
[
6
]
).
Therefore we obtain

1\leq\mu(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k-1})\leq n+1,\qquad 2-n\leq\mu(a_{j})\leq 2\quad(1\leq\forall j\leq k-1).

Since
\displaystyle\mu(a_{1}+\cdots+a_{k-1})>0
, there exists
\displaystyle j
such that
\displaystyle\mu(a_{j})>0
.
Since
\displaystyle\mu
takes values in
\displaystyle 2{\mathbb{Z}}
, we obtain
\displaystyle\mu(a_{j})=2
.
Since
\displaystyle X(a_{j})\neq 0
we obtain
\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\bar{a_{j}})>0
and
\displaystyle H_{n}({\mathcal{L}}(a_{j}):{\mathbb{R}})\neq 0
.
∎

Corollary
3.5
in particular confirms Audin’s conjecture:
every Lagrangian torus in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
bounds a disk with positive symplectic area and Maslov index
\displaystyle 2
.
Note that Cieliebak-Mohnke
[
2
]

proved Audin’s conjecture
by an approach different from ours.
For other previous results on this conjecture see
[
2
]
.

Another important application is
a complete classification of
orientable, closed, prime three-manifolds admitting Lagrangian embeddings into
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
:

Corollary 3.6
.

A closed, connected, orientable and prime three-manifold
\displaystyle M
admits a Lagrangian embedding into
\displaystyle({\mathbb{C}}^{3},\omega_{3})

if and only if
\displaystyle M
is diffeomorphic to
\displaystyle S^{1}\times\Sigma

where
\displaystyle\Sigma
is a closed orientable two-manifold.

The “if” part in Corollary
3.6
is elementary and classically known.
The “only if” part follows from Corollary
3.5
and some classical results in three-dimensional topology.
See
[
6
]
Section 11 or
[
14
]
Section 5 for details.
Note that Evans-Kȩdra
[
5
]
and Damian
[
3
]
proved the same conclusion for
monotone
Lagrangian submanifolds in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{3}
which are orientable but not necessarily prime.

The proof of Theorem
3.2
occupies the rest of this paper.
In Sections 4–6 we reduce Theorem
3.2
to Theorem
6.1
(see Section 6),
which will be proved in Sections 7–9 using the pseudo-holomorphic curve theory.
In Section 4 we introduce the space of Moore loops with marked points, and the notion of de Rham chains on these spaces,
following
[
12
]
with minor modifications.
Then we define the chain complex of de Rham chains and study its basic properties.
In Section 5, we reduce Theorem
3.2
to Theorem
5.1
, which asserts the existence of a solution of (
3
), (
4
) at chain level.
In Section 6, we reduce Theorem
5.1
to Theorem
6.1
,
which asserts the existence of a sequence of approximate solutions connected by “gauge equivalences”.

4.
de Rham chains on the space of loops with marked points

In Section 4.1, we introduce the space of Moore loops with
\displaystyle k+1
marked points (where
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
) which we denote by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.
In Section 4.2 we fix our conventions on signs.
In Section 4.3, we define the chain complex
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})
which consists of “de Rham chains” on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.
In Section 4.4, we introduce a chain model of
\displaystyle[-1,1]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.
In Section 4.5, we introduce a natural dg Lie algebra by taking direct products of de Rham chain complexes introduced in Sections 4.3 and 4.4.

4.1.
Space of Moore loops with marked points

First we consider the space of Moore paths

\Pi:=\{(T,\gamma)\mid T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0},\,\gamma\in C^{\infty}([0,T],L),\,\partial_{t}^{m}\gamma(0)=\partial_{t}^{m}\gamma(T)=0\,(\forall m\geq 1)\}.

We define evaluation maps
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0},\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}:\Pi\to L
by

\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(T,\gamma):=\gamma(0),\qquad\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(T,\gamma):=\gamma(T)

and a concatenation map

\Pi\,_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}}\Pi\to\Pi;\quad(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})\mapsto\Gamma_{0}*\Gamma_{1}

by

(T_{0},\gamma_{0})*(T_{1},\gamma_{1}):=(T_{0}+T_{1},\gamma_{0}*\gamma_{1})

where

(\gamma_{0}*\gamma_{1})(t):=\begin{cases}\gamma_{0}(t)&(0\leq t\leq T_{0}),\\
\gamma_{1}(t-T_{0})&(T_{0}\leq t\leq T_{0}+T_{1}).\end{cases}

Next we consider the space of Moore loops with marked points.
For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
we define the space
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

which consists of
\displaystyle(T,\gamma,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
such that

•

\displaystyle T>0
and
\displaystyle\gamma\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}},L)
.

•

\displaystyle 0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}<T
. We set
\displaystyle t_{0}:=0=T\in{\mathbb{R}}/T{\mathbb{Z}}
.

•

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{m}\gamma(t_{j})=0
for every
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
and
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.

For every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we define
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}:{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}\to L
by

\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}(T,\gamma,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}):=\gamma(t_{j}).

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}
will be abbreviated as
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}
when there is no risk of confusion.

For
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
\displaystyle k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
,
we define a concatenation map

\text{\rm con}\,_{j}:{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}\,{}_{\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k^{\prime}+1}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+k^{\prime}}

as follows.
Notice that one can identify
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
with

\{(\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{k})\in\Pi^{k+1}\mid\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(\Gamma_{i})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(\Gamma_{i+1})\,(0\leq i\leq k-1),\,\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(\Gamma_{k})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(\Gamma_{0})\}.

Then we define
\displaystyle\text{\rm con}\,_{j}
by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm con}\,_{j}((\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{k}),(\Gamma^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{j-2},\Gamma_{j-1}*\Gamma^{\prime}_{0},\Gamma^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}-1},\Gamma^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}*\Gamma_{j},\Gamma_{j+1},\ldots,\Gamma_{k})&(k^{\prime}\geq 1)\\
(\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{j-2},\Gamma_{j-1}*\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}*\Gamma_{j},\Gamma_{j+1},\ldots,\Gamma_{k})&(k^{\prime}=0).\end{cases}

For every
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
,
let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
denote the subset of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

which consists of
\displaystyle(T,\gamma,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
such that

\displaystyle[\gamma]=a
.
Obviously
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}=\bigsqcup_{a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})}{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
,
and the concatenation map
\displaystyle\text{\rm con}\,_{j}
satisfies

\text{\rm con}\,_{j}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)\,_{\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime}))\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{k+k^{\prime}}(a+a^{\prime}).

4.2.
Signs

Here we summarize some conventions on signs.
Our sign conventions for direct/fiber products follow
[
8
]
Section 8.2,
and for pushout of differntial forms we follow
[
10
]
Section 7.1.
Note that these conventions are different from those in
[
12
]
.

Direct and fiber products of manifolds

Let
\displaystyle X_{1}
and
\displaystyle X_{2}
be oriented manifolds.
Their direct product
\displaystyle X_{1}\times X_{2}
is oriented so that

T(X_{1}\times X_{2})\cong TX_{1}\oplus TX_{2}

preserves orientations.

Next we consider fiber product.
Let
\displaystyle M
be an oriented manifold and

\displaystyle\pi_{i}:X_{i}\to M\,(i=1,2)
be
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-maps.
We assume that
\displaystyle\pi_{2}
is a submersion.

\displaystyle\ker d\pi_{2}
is oriented so that the isomorphism

TX_{2}\cong TM\oplus\ker d\pi_{2}

preserves orientations. Then we orient
\displaystyle X_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}X_{2}
so that

T(X_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}X_{2})\cong TX_{1}\oplus\ker d\pi_{2}

preserves orientations.

Direct and fiber products of K-spaces

For later use we also fix sign conventions for direct and fiber products of K-spaces
(see Section 10 for basic notions in the theory of Kuranishi structures).
Let
\displaystyle X_{1}
,
\displaystyle X_{2}
be topological spaces with K-structures,
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}=(U_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},s_{i},\psi_{i})
be a K-chart on
\displaystyle X_{i}
, for each
\displaystyle i=1,2
.

Then the direct product
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
,
which is a K-chart of
\displaystyle X_{1}\times X_{2}
,
is oriented by

{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{U}}_{2}:=(-1)^{\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(\text{\rm dim}\,U_{1}-\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{1})}(U_{1}\times U_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{2},s_{1}\times s_{2},\psi_{1}\times\psi_{2}),

where
\displaystyle U_{1}\times U_{2}
is oriented as before, and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{2}

is oriented so that the isomorphism

({\mathcal{E}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{2})_{(x_{1},x_{2})}\cong({\mathcal{E}}_{1})_{x_{1}}\oplus({\mathcal{E}}_{2})_{x_{2}}\qquad(x_{1}\in U_{1},\,x_{2}\in U_{2})

preserves orientations.

Next we consider the fiber product.
Let
\displaystyle M
be an oriented
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold and

\displaystyle\pi_{i}:X_{i}\to M\,(i=1,2)
be strongly smooth maps.
We assume that
\displaystyle\pi_{2}
is weakly submersive.
Then the fiber product

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
,
which is a K-chart of

\displaystyle X\,_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}X_{2}
, is oriented by

{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}{\mathcal{U}}_{2}:=(-1)^{\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{2}(\text{\rm dim}\,U_{1}-\text{\rm dim}\,M-\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{1})}(U_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}U_{2},{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{E}}_{2},s_{1}\times s_{2},\psi_{1}\times\psi_{2}),

where
\displaystyle U_{1}\,{}_{\pi_{1}}\!\times_{\pi_{2}}U_{2}
is oriented as before.

Pushout of differential forms

For any manifold
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle j\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{j}(X)
denote the space of degree
\displaystyle j
differential forms on
\displaystyle X
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{j}_{c}(X)
denote its subspace which consists of compactly supported differential forms.
We set
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{j}(X)=0
when
\displaystyle j<0
or
\displaystyle j>\text{\rm dim}\,X
.

Suppose
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle Y
are oriented manifolds
and
\displaystyle\pi:X\to Y
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-submersion.
We define a pushout (or integration along fibers)

\pi_{!}:{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(X)\to{\mathcal{A}}^{*-\text{\rm dim}\,\pi}_{c}(Y)

(here
\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,\pi:=\text{\rm dim}\,X-\text{\rm dim}\,Y
) so that the formula

\int_{Y}\pi_{!}\omega\wedge\eta=\int_{X}\omega\wedge\pi^{*}\eta

holds for any
\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(X)
and
\displaystyle\eta\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(Y)
.
Simple computations show

\displaystyle\displaystyle d(\pi_{!}\omega)=(-1)^{\text{\rm dim}\,\pi}\pi_{!}(d\omega)\qquad\qquad(\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(X)),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\pi_{!}(\omega\wedge\pi^{*}\omega^{\prime})=\pi_{!}\omega\wedge\omega^{\prime}\qquad\qquad\,(\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(X),\,\omega^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}(Y)).

4.3.
de Rham chain complex of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

Let us define the “de Rham chain complex” of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
.
First we need the following definition.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\displaystyle U
be a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold and

\displaystyle\varphi:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.
We set

\varphi(u)=(T(u),\gamma(u),t_{1}(u),\ldots,t_{k}(u)).

We say that
\displaystyle\varphi
is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
, if the map

U\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k+1};\,u\mapsto(T(u),t_{1}(u),\ldots,t_{k}(u))

is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
and

\{(u,t)\mid u\in U,\,0\leq t\leq T(u)\}\to L;\,(u,t)\mapsto\gamma(u)(t)

is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
, namely it extends to a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-map from an open neighborhood of the LHS in
\displaystyle U\times{\mathbb{R}}
to
\displaystyle L
.
We say that
\displaystyle\varphi
is
smooth
, if
\displaystyle\varphi
is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
and

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\varphi:U\to L
is a submersion.

For every
\displaystyle N\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
, let
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{U}}_{N}
denote the set of oriented submanifolds in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, and let
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{U}}:=\bigsqcup_{N\geq 1}{\mathfrak{U}}_{N}
.
Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
denote the set of pairs
\displaystyle(U,\varphi)
such that

\displaystyle U\in{\mathfrak{U}}
and
\displaystyle\varphi:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
is a smooth map.

For every
\displaystyle N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let us consider the vector space

(5)

\bigoplus_{(U,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))}{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\rm dim}\,U-N}_{c}(U).

For any
\displaystyle(U,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
and
\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\rm dim}\,U-N}_{c}(U)
,
let
\displaystyle(U,\varphi,\omega)
denote the vector in (
5
)
such that its
\displaystyle(U,\varphi)
-component is
\displaystyle\omega
and the other components are
\displaystyle 0
.

Let
\displaystyle Z_{N}
denote the subspace of (
5
) which is generated by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{(U,\varphi,\pi_{!}\omega)-(U^{\prime},\varphi\circ\pi,\omega)\mid(U,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)),\quad U^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{U}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}_{c}^{\text{\rm dim}\,U^{\prime}-N}(U^{\prime}),\quad\pi:U^{\prime}\to U\,\text{is a $\displaystyle C^{\infty}$-submersion}\}.

Then we define

C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{N}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)):=\biggl(\bigoplus_{(U,\varphi)\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))}{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\rm dim}\,U-N}_{c}(U)\biggr)/Z_{N}.

We often abbreviate
\displaystyle[(U,\varphi,\omega)]\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{N}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
by
\displaystyle(U,\varphi,\omega)
.

We define a boundary operator

\displaystyle\partial:C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\to C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
by

(6)

\partial(U,\varphi,\omega):=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}(U,\varphi,d\omega).

It is easy to check that
\displaystyle\partial
is well-defined and
\displaystyle\partial^{2}=0
.
We call this chain complex the
de Rham chain complex
of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
,
and denote its homology by
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
.

Remark 4.2
.

Here are slight differences between the presentation in this section and that in
[
12
]
.

•

The sign for the boundary operator in (
6
) is different from that in
[
12
]
.

•

In the definition of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
we only require that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\varphi:U\to L
is a submersion.
On the other hand,
in Section 7.2 in
[
12
]
,
we consider maps
\displaystyle\varphi:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\varphi:U\to L
are submersions for all
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
However the resulting chain complexes are quasi-isomorphic.

Lemma 4.3
.

(i):

The forgetting map

{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(a);\qquad(T,\gamma,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\mapsto(T,\gamma)

induces an isomorphism
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\cong H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(a))
.
In particular
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
does not depend on
\displaystyle k
.

(ii):

\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(a))\cong H^{\text{\rm sing}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(a):{\mathbb{R}})
, where the RHS denotes the singular homology with respect to the
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-topology on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(a)
.

Proof
.

For each
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, let
\displaystyle\Delta^{k}
denote the
\displaystyle k
-dimensional simplex:

\Delta^{k}:=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{R}}^{0}&(k=0),\\
\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\mid 0\leq t_{1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{k}\leq 1\}&(k\geq 1).\end{cases}

Then we define
\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\times\Delta^{k})
to be the set
which consists of
\displaystyle(U,\varphi)
such that

\displaystyle U\in{\mathfrak{U}}
and
\displaystyle\varphi:U\to{\mathcal{L}}(a)\times\Delta^{k}
is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
(i.e. projections to each components are of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
).
Then we can define a chain complex
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\times\Delta^{k})
in exactly the same manner as
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
.
Moreover, there exists a zig-zag of quasi-isomorphisms connecting
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
and
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\times\Delta^{k})

(see the last part of Section 7.2 in
[
12
]
, where
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
is denoted by

\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{L}}}^{a}_{k,\text{reg}})
).
Then, to prove (i) it is sufficient to show that the map

(7)

{\mathcal{L}}(a)\times\Delta^{k}\to{\mathcal{L}}(a);\qquad(\gamma,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\mapsto\gamma

induces an isomorphism on
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}
,
which follows from homotopy invariance of
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}
(see Proposition 4.7 in
[
12
]
).
(ii) follows from
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(a))\cong H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(a))

(apply the zig-zag mentioned above for
\displaystyle k=0
),
and
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(a))\cong H^{\text{\rm sing}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}(a):{\mathbb{R}})
,
which follows from Theorem 6.1 in
[
12
]
.
∎

Next we define the fiber product on de Rham chain complexes.
For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
\displaystyle k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
\displaystyle a,a^{\prime}\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
,
we define a linear map

(8)

\circ_{j}:C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\otimes C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime}))\to C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+k^{\prime}}(a+a^{\prime}));\,x\otimes y\mapsto x\circ_{j}y

in the following way.
Setting

x:=(U,\varphi,\omega),\qquad y:=(U^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})

let

\displaystyle\varphi_{j}:=\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\varphi
and

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{0}:=\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\varphi^{\prime}
.
Then we define

x\circ_{j}y:=(-1)^{(\text{\rm dim}\,U-|\omega|-n)|\omega^{\prime}|}(U\,_{\varphi_{j}}\!\times_{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}U^{\prime},\text{\rm con}\,_{j}\circ(\varphi_{j}\times\varphi^{\prime}_{0}),\omega\times\omega^{\prime}),

where
the fiber product
\displaystyle U\,_{\varphi_{j}}\!\times_{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}U^{\prime}
is oriented as in Section 4.2, and

\displaystyle\text{\rm con}\,_{j}
denotes the concatenation map defined in Section 4.1.
It is straightforward to check that the fiber product (
8
) is a chain map,
i.e. it satisfies the Leibniz rule

\partial(x\circ_{j}y)=\partial x\circ_{j}y+(-1)^{d}x\circ_{j}\partial y.

It is also easy to check the associativity: given
\displaystyle x_{i}\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d_{i}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1}(a_{i}))\,(i=1,2,3)
,
there holds

\displaystyle\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2})\circ_{k_{2}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(-1)^{d_{2}d_{3}}(x_{1}\circ_{i_{2}}x_{3})\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2}\quad\,(1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq k_{1}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle(x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}x_{2})\circ_{i_{1}+i_{2}-1}x_{3}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=x_{1}\circ_{i_{1}}(x_{2}\circ_{i_{2}}x_{3})\qquad\qquad\qquad(1\leq i_{1}\leq k_{1},\,1\leq i_{2}\leq k_{2}).

On homology level,
the fiber product corresponds to the Chas-Sullivan loop product,
which was originally defined in
[
1
]
:

Lemma 4.4
.

The fiber product (
8
) induces a linear map

H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\otimes H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime}))\to H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+k^{\prime}}(a+a^{\prime})).

Via isomorphisms in Lemma
4.3
, this map corresponds to the loop product

H_{n+d}({\mathcal{L}}(a))\otimes H_{n+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}(a^{\prime}))\to H_{n+d+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}(a+a^{\prime})).

Proof
.

By Lemma
4.3
(i), it is sufficient to prove the case
\displaystyle k=1
and
\displaystyle k^{\prime}=0
,
which follows from Proposition 8.7 in
[
12
]
.
∎

4.4.
Chain model of
\displaystyle[-1,1]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

In this subsection, we define another chain complex
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
.
Roughly speaking, it consists of chains on
\displaystyle[-1,1]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
relative to
\displaystyle\{-1,1\}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
.
In Section 6, we use this chain complex to define “gauge-equivalence” of (approximate) solutions of the Maurer-Cartan equation of loop bracket.

Let
\displaystyle\bar{{\mathcal{P}}}
denote the set consists of tuple
\displaystyle(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-})
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\displaystyle U\in{\mathfrak{U}}
and
\displaystyle\varphi:U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
. We denote
\displaystyle\varphi:=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}},\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}})
,
and for every interval
\displaystyle I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
we denote
\displaystyle U_{I}:=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}})^{-1}(I)
.

•

\displaystyle\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}
and
\displaystyle\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}
are of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times L;\,u\mapsto(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u),\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}(u))
is a submersion.

•

\displaystyle\tau_{+}:U_{\geq 1}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\times U_{1}
is a diffeomorphism (
\displaystyle U_{\geq 1}
is an abbreviation of
\displaystyle U_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}}
) such that

\varphi|_{U_{\geq 1}}=(i_{\geq 1}\times\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}|_{U_{1}})\circ\tau_{+}

where
\displaystyle i_{\geq 1}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the inclusion map.

•

\displaystyle\tau_{-}:U_{\leq-1}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}\times U_{-1}
is a diffeomorphism
\displaystyle(U_{\leq-1}
is an abbreviation of
\displaystyle U_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}}
) such that

\varphi|_{U_{\leq-1}}=(i_{\leq-1}\times\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}|_{U_{-1}})\circ\tau_{-}

where
\displaystyle i_{\leq-1}:{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the inclusion map.

Remark 4.5
.

\displaystyle U_{\geq 1}
and
\displaystyle U_{\leq-1}
may be the empty set.

For any
\displaystyle(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-})\in\bar{{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\displaystyle N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}^{N}(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-})

denote the vector space which consists of
\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{N}(U)
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\displaystyle\omega|_{U_{[-1,1]}}
is compactly supported.

•

\displaystyle\omega|_{U_{\geq 1}}=(\tau_{+})^{*}(1\times\omega|_{U_{1}})
.

•

\displaystyle\omega|_{U_{\leq-1}}=(\tau_{-})^{*}(1\times\omega|_{U_{-1}})
.

For
\displaystyle N\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, let us define

\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{N}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)):=\biggl(\bigoplus_{(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-})\in\bar{{\mathcal{P}}}}{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\rm dim}\,U-N-1}(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-})\biggr)/Z_{N}

where
\displaystyle Z_{N}
is a subspace generated by vectors

(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega)-(U^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}_{+},\tau^{\prime}_{-},\omega^{\prime})

such that there exists a submersion
\displaystyle\pi:U^{\prime}\to U
satisfying

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\varphi\circ\pi,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\pi_{!}\omega^{\prime},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau_{+}\circ\pi|_{U^{\prime}_{\geq 1}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\text{\rm id}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}}\times\pi|_{U^{\prime}_{1}})\circ\tau^{\prime}_{+},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau_{-}\circ\pi|_{U^{\prime}_{\leq-1}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\text{\rm id}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}}\times\pi|_{U^{\prime}_{-1}})\circ\tau^{\prime}_{-}.

Let us define
\displaystyle\partial:\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\to\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))

by

\partial(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega):=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},d\omega).

It is easy to check that
\displaystyle\partial
is well-defined and
\displaystyle\partial^{2}=0
, thus we obtain a chain complex.

In the following argument of this subsection,
we abbreviate
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
and
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))
by

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{*}
and
\displaystyle C_{*}
, respectively.
Let us define
\displaystyle i:C_{*}\to\bar{C}_{*}
by

i(U,\varphi,\omega):=(-1)^{\text{\rm dim}\,U}({\mathbb{R}}\times U,\text{\rm id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},1\times\omega)

where
\displaystyle\tau_{+}
and
\displaystyle\tau_{-}
are defined in the obvious way.
Also, we define

\displaystyle e_{+}:\bar{C}_{*}\to C_{*}
and
\displaystyle e_{-}:\bar{C}_{*}\to C_{*}
by

\displaystyle\displaystyle e_{+}(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=(-1)^{\text{\rm dim}\,U-1}(U_{1},\varphi|_{U_{1}},\omega|_{U_{1}}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle e_{-}(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=(-1)^{\text{\rm dim}\,U-1}(U_{-1},\varphi|_{U_{-1}},\omega|_{U_{-1}}),

where
\displaystyle U_{1}
(resp.
\displaystyle U_{-1}
) is oriented so that

\displaystyle\tau_{+}:U_{\geq 1}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\times U_{1}

(resp.
\displaystyle\tau_{-}:U_{\leq-1}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}\times U_{-1}
)
is orientation-preserving, where

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
(resp.
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}
)
is oriented so that

\displaystyle\partial/\partial t

is of positive direction
(
\displaystyle t
denotes the standard coordinate on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}
).
Then it is easy to see that
\displaystyle i
,
\displaystyle e_{+}
,
\displaystyle e_{-}
are well-defined chain maps,
and there holds
\displaystyle e_{+}\circ i=e_{-}\circ i=\text{\rm id}_{C}
.

Lemma 4.6
.

\displaystyle(e_{+},e_{-}):\bar{C}_{*}\to C_{*}\oplus C_{*}
is surjective.

Proof
.

Let us take
\displaystyle\chi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},[0,1])
so that

\displaystyle\chi(t)=1
for every
\displaystyle t\geq 1
and
\displaystyle\chi(t)=0
for every
\displaystyle t\leq-1
.
Given
\displaystyle x=(U,\varphi,\omega)\in C_{*}
, let

\bar{x}:=(-1)^{\text{\rm dim}\,U}({\mathbb{R}}\times U,\text{\rm id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\chi\times\omega),

where
\displaystyle\tau_{+}
and
\displaystyle\tau_{-}
are defined in the obvious manner.
Then
\displaystyle e_{+}(\bar{x})=x
and
\displaystyle e_{-}(\bar{x})=0
, thus we have proved
that the image of
\displaystyle(e_{+},e_{-})
contains
\displaystyle C_{*}\oplus 0
.
A similar argument shows that the image contains
\displaystyle 0\oplus C_{*}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

\displaystyle i\circ e_{+}
and
\displaystyle i\circ e_{-}
are chain homotopic to
\displaystyle\text{\rm id}_{\bar{C}}
.

Proof
.

We only prove that
\displaystyle i\circ e_{+}
is chain homotopic to
\displaystyle\text{\rm id}_{\bar{C}}

by explicitly defining a linear map
\displaystyle K:\bar{C}_{*}\to\bar{C}_{*+1}
which satisfies

K\partial+\partial K=\text{\rm id}_{\bar{C}}-i\circ e_{+}.

The proof for
\displaystyle e_{-}
is completely parallel.

Step 1.

Let us take
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-functions
\displaystyle\alpha:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\displaystyle\chi:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to[0,1]

so that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\displaystyle x\leq 0\implies\alpha(x,y)=y
.

•

\displaystyle x\geq 1,\,y\geq-1\implies\alpha(x,y)=-x
.

•

\displaystyle\nabla\alpha(x,y)\neq 0
for every
\displaystyle(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

•

\displaystyle\nu_{\alpha}:=\nabla\alpha/|\nabla\alpha|
is proper.
Namely, for any
\displaystyle p\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, there exists
\displaystyle c:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that

\displaystyle c(0)=p
and
\displaystyle\dot{c}(t)=\nu_{\alpha}(c(t))
for any
\displaystyle t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

\displaystyle\{\alpha\geq 1\}\subset\{y\geq 1\}
.

•

\displaystyle\{\alpha\leq-1\}\subset\{y\leq-1\}\cup\{x\geq 1\}
.

•

\displaystyle\chi\equiv 1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle\{x=0\}\cup\{x\geq 0,y\leq 1\}
.

•

\displaystyle d\chi(\nabla\alpha)=0
on
\displaystyle\{\alpha\geq 1\}\cup\{\alpha\leq-1\}
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\chi\cap\{-1\leq\alpha\leq 1\}
is compact.

Step 2.

We define a linear map
\displaystyle K:\bar{C}_{*}\to\bar{C}_{*+1}
by

K(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega):=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}({\mathbb{R}}\times U,\bar{\varphi},\bar{\tau}_{+},\bar{\tau}_{-},\bar{\omega})

where
\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}
,
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{\pm}
and
\displaystyle\bar{\omega}
are defined as follows.

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{*}
is defined by taking a quotient, however well-definedness of
\displaystyle K
is easy to check.

•

Let us denote
\displaystyle\varphi:U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
by
\displaystyle\varphi=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}},\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}})
.
We define

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}:{\mathbb{R}}\times U\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}:{\mathbb{R}}\times U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a)
by

\tilde{\alpha}(r,u):=\alpha(r,\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u)),\qquad\bar{\varphi}(r,u):=(\tilde{\alpha}(r,u),\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}(u)).

We have to check that

{\mathbb{R}}\times U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times L;\quad(r,u)\mapsto(\tilde{\alpha}(r,u),\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}(u))

is a submersion.
This is because

\displaystyle U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times L;\,u\mapsto(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u),\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}(u))
is a submersion,
and
\displaystyle d\alpha(x,y)\neq 0
for every
\displaystyle(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

•

To define
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{+}
and
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{-}
,
we first define a vector field

V\in{\mathfrak{X}}({\mathbb{R}}\times(U_{\geq 1}\cup U_{\leq-1})\cup{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\times U_{[-1,1]})

as follows (recall
\displaystyle\nu_{\alpha}:=\nabla\alpha/|\nabla\alpha|
from Step 1):

–

On
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}\times U_{\geq 1}\cong{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\times U_{1}
, we set
\displaystyle V(x,y,u):=(\nu_{\alpha}(x,y),0)
.

–

On
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}\times U_{\leq-1}\cong{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}\times U_{-1}
, we set
\displaystyle V(x,y,u):=(\nu_{\alpha}(x,y),0)
.

–

On
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\times U_{[-1,1]}
, we set
\displaystyle V(x,u):=(-1,0)
.

In particular,
\displaystyle V
is defined on
\displaystyle\{\tilde{\alpha}\geq 1\}\cup\{\tilde{\alpha}\leq-1\}
, and

\displaystyle V
is forward (resp. backward) complete on
\displaystyle\{\tilde{\alpha}\geq 1\}
(resp.
\displaystyle\{\tilde{\alpha}\leq-1\}
).
Then we define a diffeomorphism
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{+}:\{\tilde{\alpha}\geq 1\}\cong{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}\times\{\tilde{\alpha}=1\}
by

\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{+}(p):=(\tilde{\alpha}(p),q)
, where
\displaystyle q
is a unique point in
\displaystyle\{\tilde{\alpha}=1\}
which is connected to
\displaystyle p
by
an integral curve of
\displaystyle V
.
Similarly, we define a diffeomorphism
\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{-}:\{\tilde{\alpha}\leq-1\}\cong{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}\times\{\tilde{\alpha}=-1\}
by

\displaystyle\bar{\tau}_{-}(p):=(\tilde{\alpha}(p),q)
, where
\displaystyle q
is a unique point in
\displaystyle\{\tilde{\alpha}=-1\}
which is connected to
\displaystyle p
by
an integral curve of
\displaystyle V
.

•

We define
\displaystyle\bar{\omega}
by

\displaystyle\bar{\omega}(r,u):=\chi(r,\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u))\cdot(\text{pr}_{U})^{*}\omega
.

Step 3.

We prove that
\displaystyle K\partial+\partial K=\text{\rm id}_{\bar{C}}-i\circ e_{+}
.
It is easy to see that

(K\partial+\partial K)(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega)=({\mathbb{R}}\times U,\bar{\varphi},\bar{\tau}_{+},\bar{\tau}_{-},d\chi(r,\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u))\wedge(\text{\rm pr}_{U})^{*}\omega).

Let us denote
\displaystyle d\chi:=(d\chi)_{+}+(d\chi)_{-}
where

\displaystyle(d\chi)_{+}
is supported on
\displaystyle\{x>0,y>1\}
and
\displaystyle(d\chi)_{-}
is supported on
\displaystyle\{x<0\}
.
Then

(9)

({\mathbb{R}}\times U,\bar{\varphi},\bar{\tau}_{+},\bar{\tau}_{-},(d\chi)_{+}\wedge(\text{pr}_{U})^{*}\omega)=-(i\circ e_{+})(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega).

To check (
9
), let us consider a submersion

{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times U_{>1}\cong{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times({\mathbb{R}}_{>1}\times U_{1})\to{\mathbb{R}}\times U_{1}

where the first map (diffeomorphism) is
\displaystyle\text{\rm id}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}}\times\tau_{+}|_{U_{>1}}
, and the second map is
\displaystyle(x,y,u)\mapsto(\alpha(x,y),u)
.
Then
\displaystyle\pi:{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>1}\to{\mathbb{R}};\,(x,y)\mapsto\alpha(x,y)
satisfies
\displaystyle\pi_{!}((d\chi)_{+})=-1
,
and this shows (
9
).
On the other hand

({\mathbb{R}}\times U,\bar{\varphi},\bar{\tau}_{+},\bar{\tau}_{-},(d\chi)_{-}\wedge\text{pr}_{U}^{*}\omega)=(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega)

can be proved by the projection
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{<0}\times U\to U
,
which pushes
\displaystyle(d\chi)_{-}
to
\displaystyle 1
.
∎

Let us define the fiber product on
\displaystyle\bar{C}_{*}
.
For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
\displaystyle k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
and
\displaystyle a,a^{\prime}\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
, we define

(10)

\circ_{j}:\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\otimes\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k^{\prime}+1}(a^{\prime}))\to\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n+d+d^{\prime}}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+k^{\prime}}(a+a^{\prime}));\,x\otimes y\mapsto x\circ_{j}y

in the following way. Setting

x=(U,\varphi,\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\omega),\qquad y=(U^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}_{+},\tau^{\prime}_{-},\omega^{\prime}),

let us define
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-maps

\displaystyle\varphi_{j},\,\varphi^{\prime}_{0}:U\to{\mathbb{R}}\times L
by

\varphi_{j}:=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}},\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}),\qquad\varphi^{\prime}_{0}:=(\varphi^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}},\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\varphi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{L}}}).

Note that
\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}_{0}
is a submersion, thus the fiber product

\displaystyle U\,_{\varphi_{j}}\!\times_{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}U^{\prime}
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold.
Then we define

x\circ_{j}y:=(-1)^{(\text{\rm dim}\,U-|\omega|-n-1)|\omega^{\prime}|+n}(U\,_{\varphi_{j}}\!\times_{\varphi^{\prime}_{0}}U^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime\prime},\tau^{\prime\prime}_{+},\tau^{\prime\prime}_{-},\omega\times\omega^{\prime})

where
\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime\prime}
is defined by

\varphi^{\prime\prime}(u,u^{\prime}):=(\varphi_{\mathbb{R}}(u),\text{\rm con}\,_{j}(\varphi_{{\mathcal{L}}}(u),\varphi^{\prime}_{{\mathcal{L}}}(u^{\prime}))),

and
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime\prime}_{+}
,
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime\prime}_{-}
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho_{+}(u,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}}\circ\tau_{+}(u)=\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}}\circ\tau^{\prime}_{+}(u^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau^{\prime\prime}_{+}(u,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=(\rho_{+}(u,u^{\prime}),(\text{\rm pr}_{U_{1}}\circ\tau_{+}(u),\text{\rm pr}_{U^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\tau^{\prime}_{+}(u^{\prime}))),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\rho_{-}(u,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}}\circ\tau_{-}(u)=\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}}\circ\tau^{\prime}_{-}(u^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau^{\prime\prime}_{-}(u,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=(\rho_{-}(u,u^{\prime}),(\text{\rm pr}_{U_{-1}}\circ\tau_{-}(u),\text{\rm pr}_{U^{\prime}_{-1}}\circ\tau^{\prime}_{-}(u^{\prime}))).

It is easy to see that the fiber product (
10
) is a chain map,
i.e. it satisfies the Leibniz rule:

\partial(x\circ_{j}y)=\partial x\circ_{j}y+(-1)^{d}x\circ_{j}\partial y.

Moreover, chain maps
\displaystyle i
,
\displaystyle e_{+}
,
\displaystyle e_{-}
intertwine fiber products on
\displaystyle C_{*}
and
\displaystyle\bar{C}_{*}
.

4.5.
dg Lie algebras
\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}
,
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
and their completions

For every
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
and
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we define

C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}:=C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*+n+\mu(a)+k-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))

and set

C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})\\
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\end{subarray}}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}.

To define the action filatration on
\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
,
for each
\displaystyle E\in{\mathbb{R}}
we set

F^{E}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\omega_{n}(\bar{a})>E\\
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\end{subarray}}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}

and take completion:

\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}:=\varprojlim_{E\to\infty}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}/F^{E}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}.

\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
has a dg Lie algebra structure
(see Remark
2.2
for our convention)
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\partial x)(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\partial(x(a,k)),

\displaystyle\displaystyle(x\circ y)(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k^{\prime}+k^{\prime\prime}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k^{\prime}\\
a^{\prime}+a^{\prime\prime}=a\end{subarray}}(-1)^{(i-1)(k^{\prime\prime}-1)+(k^{\prime}-1)(|y|+1+k^{\prime\prime})}x(a^{\prime},k^{\prime})\circ_{i}y(a^{\prime\prime},k^{\prime\prime}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle[x,y]

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=x\circ y-(-1)^{|x||y|}y\circ x.

For the sign in the RHS of the second formula, see Theorem 2.8 (ii) in
[
12
]
.
The Jacobi identity follows from the associativity of the fiber product.
Since this dg Lie algebra structure respects the decomposition
\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\cong\bigoplus_{(a,k)\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}
,
it extends to the completion
\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
.

We also consider
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{(a,k)\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}}\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*+n+\mu(a)+k-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))

and its completion
\displaystyle\widehat{\bar{C}}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
.
One can naturally define a dg Lie algebra structure on
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
,

\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
, and it extends to the completion.
One can define morphisms of dg Lie algebras

i:C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},\quad e_{+}:\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},\quad e_{-}:\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},

so that the following properties hold:

•

\displaystyle e_{+}\circ i=e_{-}\circ i=\text{\rm id}_{C^{{\mathcal{L}}}}
.

•

\displaystyle i\circ e_{+}
and
\displaystyle i\circ e_{-}
are chain homotopic to
\displaystyle\text{\rm id}_{\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}}
.
One can take chain homotopies to respect decompositions over
\displaystyle H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
.

•

\displaystyle(e_{+},e_{-}):\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\oplus C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}
is surjective.

5.
Chain level statement

The goal of this section is to reduce Theorem
3.2
to Theorem
5.1
,
which is formulated on the dg Lie algebra
\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
.
Throughout this section,
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}
-coefficient singular homology
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm sing}}_{*}(\,\cdot\,:{\mathbb{R}})
is abbreviated as
\displaystyle H_{*}(\,\cdot\,)
.

Theorem 5.1
.

There exist
\displaystyle x\in\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{-1}
,
\displaystyle y\in\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{2}
,
\displaystyle z\in\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{1}

and
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
such that:

(i):

\displaystyle\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0
, namely
\displaystyle x
is a Maurer-Cartan element of
\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
.

(ii):

\displaystyle\partial y-[x,y]=z
.

(iii):

\displaystyle x(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
,
\displaystyle k\geq 2
.
Moreover,
\displaystyle x(0,2)\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{3}(0))
is a cycle such that

\displaystyle[x(0,2)]\in H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{3}(0))\cong H_{n}({\mathcal{L}}(0))
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.

(iv):

\displaystyle z(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.
Moreover,
\displaystyle z(0,0)\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0))
is a cycle such that

\displaystyle[z(0,0)]\in H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0))\cong H_{n}({\mathcal{L}}(0))
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.

In (iii) and (iv),
\displaystyle[L]\in H_{n}({\mathcal{L}}(0))
is defined as in Theorem
3.2
.

Conditions (i) and (iii) imply that

x^{0}:=\sum_{k\geq 2}x(0,k)\in\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{-1}

is a Maurer-Cartan element of
\displaystyle\widehat{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
.
For every
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
, let

\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)_{*}:=\prod_{k\geq 0}C^{\mathcal{L}}(a,k)_{*}
.
We define

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{0}}:C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)_{*-1}
by

\displaystyle\partial_{x^{0}}(u):=\partial u-[x^{0},u]
.

Lemma 5.2
.

\displaystyle H_{*}(C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)_{*},\partial_{x^{0}})\cong H_{*+n+\mu(a)-1}({\mathcal{L}}(a))
.

Proof
.

Let us consider the exact sequence

0\to\prod_{k\geq 1}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a)_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,0)_{*}\to 0

where the first map is inclusion and the second map is projection.
Since
\displaystyle H_{*}(C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,0))\cong H_{*+n+\mu(a)-1}({\mathcal{L}}(a))
by Lemma
4.3
,
it is sufficient to show that
\displaystyle\prod_{k\geq 1}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}
is acyclic
with the boundary operator
\displaystyle\partial_{x^{0}}
.

For every positive integer
\displaystyle N
,
the operator
\displaystyle\partial_{x^{0}}
preserves
\displaystyle\prod_{k>2N}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}
.
Thus
\displaystyle\partial_{x^{0}}
acts on
\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq k\leq 2N}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}
,
preserving the filtration

\displaystyle\biggl(\prod_{i\leq k\leq 2N}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}\biggr)_{1\leq i\leq 2N}
.

Then the
\displaystyle E_{1}
-term is

H_{*}(C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k))\cong H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*+n+\mu(a)+k-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(a))\cong H_{*+n+\mu(a)+k-1}({\mathcal{L}}(a)).

Let us compute
\displaystyle d_{1}:H_{*}(C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k))\to H_{*-1}(C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k+1))
.
By direct computations

\displaystyle\displaystyle[x^{0},y](a,k+1)=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad(-1)^{|y|}\biggl(x(0,2)\circ_{2}y(a,k)+\sum_{1\leq i\leq k}(-1)^{i}y(a,k)\circ_{i}x(0,2)+(-1)^{k+1}x(0,2)\circ_{1}y(a,k)\biggr).

Since
\displaystyle[x(0,2)]\in H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{3}(0))
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]\in H_{n}({\mathcal{L}})

and the fiber product on homology level corresponds to the loop product (Lemma
4.4
),

\displaystyle d_{1}
coincides with
\displaystyle\pm\sum_{0\leq i\leq k+1}(-1)^{i}
.
Then all
\displaystyle E_{2}
-terms vanish, thus
\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq k\leq 2N}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}
is acyclic.
Finally, by taking an inverse limit (Theorem 3.5.8 in
[
15
]
) we have shown that

\displaystyle\prod_{k\geq 1}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}
is acyclic.
∎

Lemma 5.3
.

\displaystyle x^{+}:=x-x^{0}
satisfies equations

\partial_{x^{0}}x^{+}-\frac{1}{2}[x^{+},x^{+}]=0,\qquad\partial_{x^{0}}y-[x^{+},y]=z.

Proof
.

Straightforward from
\displaystyle\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0
,
\displaystyle\partial y-[x,y]=z
and
\displaystyle x=x^{0}+x^{+}
.
∎

Let
\displaystyle H^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}=\bigoplus_{a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})}H_{*+n+\mu(a)-1}({\mathcal{L}}(a))

as in Section 3. Then there exist linear maps

\iota:H^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},\quad\pi:C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to H^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},\quad\kappa:C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}\to C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*+1}

such that

\partial_{x^{0}}\circ\iota=0,\quad\pi\circ\partial_{x^{0}}=0,\quad\pi\circ\iota=\text{\rm id}_{H^{{\mathcal{L}}}},\quad\kappa\circ\partial_{x^{0}}+\partial_{x^{0}}\circ\kappa=\text{\rm id}_{C^{{\mathcal{L}}}}-\iota\circ\pi.

One can take these maps so that they preserve decompositions of
\displaystyle H^{{\mathcal{L}}}
and
\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}

over
\displaystyle H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
, thus they extend to the completions.
Also, one can take
\displaystyle\pi
so that it maps
\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}z(0,k)
to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]\in H_{n}({\mathcal{L}}(0))
.

Using homotopy transfer theorem (Thoerem
2.4
),
one can define an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-structure
\displaystyle l^{H}
on
\displaystyle H^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}

and an
\displaystyle L_{\infty}
-homomorphism

\displaystyle p
from
\displaystyle(C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},\partial_{x^{0}},[\,,\,])
to

\displaystyle(H^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*},l^{H})
so that
\displaystyle l^{H}_{1}=0
and
\displaystyle p_{1}=\pi
.
We can take
\displaystyle l^{H}
and
\displaystyle p
so that they respect decompositions over
\displaystyle H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
,
thus they extend to the completions.
Applying Proposition 4.9 in
[
14
]
,
elements in
\displaystyle\widehat{H}^{{\mathcal{L}}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}p_{k}(x^{+},\ldots,x^{+})\in\widehat{H}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{-1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}p_{k}(y,x^{+},\ldots,x^{+})\in\widehat{H}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}p_{k}(z,x^{+},\ldots,x^{+})\in\widehat{H}^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{1},

satisfy

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}l^{H}_{k}(X,\ldots,X)=0,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(k-1)!}l^{H}_{k}(Y,X,\ldots,X)=Z.

Notice that infinite sums in the definitions of
\displaystyle X
,
\displaystyle Y
,
\displaystyle Z
make sense
since
\displaystyle x^{+}(a)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
and

\displaystyle p
preserves decompositions over
\displaystyle H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Moreover
\displaystyle X(a)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
,
thus Theorem
3.2
(iii) holds with
\displaystyle c:=2\varepsilon
.
To complete the proof of Theorem
3.2
,
we need to show that
\displaystyle Z(0)=(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.
Since
\displaystyle p
respects decompositions over
\displaystyle H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})

and
\displaystyle z(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
, we obtain

Z(0)=\pi\biggl(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}z(0,k)\biggr)=(-1)^{n+1}[L].

6.
Sequence of approximate solutions

To prove Theorem
5.1
, we have to define chains
\displaystyle x
,
\displaystyle y
,
\displaystyle z
satisfying the equations

(11)

\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0,\qquad\partial y-[x,y]=z.

These chains are defined from virtual fundamental chains of moduli spaces of pseudo-holomorphic disks with boundaries on
\displaystyle L
.
However, it is difficult to get such chains in
one step
since it will involve simultaneous perturbations of Kuranishi maps of infinitely many moduli spaces.
Due to this difficulty, we first define a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})_{i}
such that the following conditions hold for every
\displaystyle i
:

•

The tuple
\displaystyle(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})
satisfies equations (
11
) up to certain energy level which goes to
\displaystyle\infty
as
\displaystyle i\to\infty
.

•

Tuples
\displaystyle(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i})
and
\displaystyle(x_{i+1},y_{i+1},z_{i+1})
are “gauge equivalent” up to certain energy level which goes to
\displaystyle\infty
as
\displaystyle i\to\infty
.

By the second condition, limits
\displaystyle x:=\lim_{i\to\infty}x_{i}
,
\displaystyle y:=\lim_{i\to\infty}y_{i}
,
\displaystyle z:=\lim_{i\to\infty}z_{i}
exist,
and by the first condition these limits satisfy (
11
).
This procedure is similar to the construction of the
\displaystyle A_{\infty}
-structure in Lagrangian Floer theory as a limit of
\displaystyle A_{K}
-structures;
see Section 7.2.3 in
[
8
]
, and Remark 22.27 in
[
10
]
.

The goal of this section is to explain details of the algebraic procedure sketched above;
we reduce Theorem
5.1
to Theorem
6.1
,
which asserts the existence of a sequence of approximate solutions connected by gauge equivalences.
The proof of Theorem
6.1
involves construction of virtual fundamental chains using the theory of Kuranishi structures,
which is carried out in Sections 7–9.

Let
\displaystyle J
be the standard complex structure on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
,
and take
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
so that

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon
is less than the minimal symplectic area of nonconstant
\displaystyle J
-holomorphic disks with boundaries on
\displaystyle L
.
We fix such
\displaystyle\varepsilon
in the following arguments.

For each
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, we set

F^{m}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{*}:=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})\\
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\\
\omega_{n}(\bar{a})\geq\varepsilon(m+1-k)\end{subarray}}C^{{\mathcal{L}}}(a,k)_{*}.

Obviously
\displaystyle\partial F^{m}\subset F^{m}
.
We can also show
\displaystyle[F^{m},F^{m^{\prime}}]\subset F^{m+m^{\prime}}
since

\omega_{n}(\bar{a})\geq\varepsilon(m+1-k),\,\omega_{n}(\bar{a^{\prime}})\geq\varepsilon(m^{\prime}+1-k^{\prime})\implies\omega_{n}(\bar{a}+\bar{a^{\prime}})\geq\varepsilon(m+m^{\prime}+1-(k+k^{\prime}-1)).

The filtration on
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
(see Section 4.5)
is defined in a similar manner.
These filtrations extend to the completions.
In the following we abbreviate
\displaystyle C^{{\mathcal{L}}}
and
\displaystyle\bar{C}^{{\mathcal{L}}}
by
\displaystyle C
and
\displaystyle\bar{C}
.

Theorem 6.1
.

There exist integers
\displaystyle I,U\geq 2

and a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{i},y_{i},z_{i},\bar{x}_{i},\bar{y}_{i},\bar{z}_{i})_{i\geq I}

satisfying the following conditions for every
\displaystyle i\geq I
:

x_{i}\in F^{1}C_{-1},\,\bar{x}_{i}\in F^{1}\bar{C}_{-1},\,y_{i}\in F^{-U}C_{2},\,\bar{y}_{i}\in F^{-U}\bar{C}_{2},\,z_{i}\in F^{-1}C_{1},\,\bar{z}_{i}\in F^{-1}\bar{C}_{1}.

x_{i}=e_{-}(\bar{x}_{i}),\quad y_{i}=e_{-}(\bar{y}_{i}),\quad z_{i}=e_{-}(\bar{z}_{i}).

\partial\bar{x}_{i}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i},\bar{x}_{i}]\in F^{i}\bar{C}_{-2},\quad\partial\bar{y}_{i}-[\bar{x}_{i},\bar{y}_{i}]-\bar{z}_{i}\in F^{i-U-1}\bar{C}_{1},\quad\partial\bar{z}_{i}-[\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i}]\in F^{i-2}\bar{C}_{0}.

x_{i+1}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i})\in F^{i}C_{-1},\quad y_{i+1}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i})\in F^{i-U-1}C_{2},\quad z_{i+1}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i})\in F^{i-2}C_{1}.

•

\displaystyle x_{i}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
,
\displaystyle k\geq 2
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle x_{i}(0,2)
is a cycle in
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{3}(0))

such that
\displaystyle[x_{i}(0,2)]
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]

via the isomorphism
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{3}(0))\cong H_{n}({\mathcal{L}})
.

•

\displaystyle z_{i}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle z_{i}(0,0)
is a cycle in
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0))

such that
\displaystyle[z_{i}(0,0)]
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]

via the isomorphism
\displaystyle H^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0))\cong H_{n}({\mathcal{L}})
.

Remark 6.2
.

\displaystyle\partial z-[x,z]=0
follows from
\displaystyle\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0
and
\displaystyle\partial y-[x,y]=z
,
thus the assumption
\displaystyle\partial\bar{z}_{i}-[\bar{x}_{i},\bar{z}_{i}]\in F^{i-2}

in Theorem
6.1
may seem redundant.
However, this assumption is necessary to carry out the induction argument.

The rest of this section is devoted to
the proof of Theorem
5.1
assuming Theorem
6.1
.
We first need the following elementary lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\displaystyle V_{*}
,
\displaystyle W_{*}
be chain complexes and
\displaystyle e:V_{*}\to W_{*}
be a surjective quasi-isomorphism.
For any
\displaystyle x\in V_{*}
and
\displaystyle y\in W_{*+1}
such that

\partial x=0,\qquad e(x)=\partial y

there exists
\displaystyle\bar{y}\in V_{*+1}
such that
\displaystyle e(\bar{y})=y
and
\displaystyle\partial\bar{y}=x
.

Proof
.

Since
\displaystyle e
is surjective, there exists
\displaystyle z\in V_{*+1}
such that
\displaystyle e(z)=y
.
Then
\displaystyle e(x-\partial z)=0
and
\displaystyle\partial(x-\partial z)=0
.
Thus
\displaystyle x-\partial z
is a cycle in
\displaystyle\text{\rm Ker}\,e
.
Since
\displaystyle\text{\rm Ker}\,e
is acyclic, there exists
\displaystyle w\in\text{\rm Ker}\,e
such that

\displaystyle\partial w=x-\partial z
.
Then,
\displaystyle\bar{y}:=z+w
satisfies the condition of the lemma.
∎

Key arguments are summarized in Lemma
6.4
below.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
\displaystyle I
,
\displaystyle U
be as in Theorem
6.1
.
There exists a sequence

(x_{i,j},y_{i,j},z_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j})_{i\geq I,\,j\geq 0}

satisfying the following conditions for every
\displaystyle i\geq I
and
\displaystyle j\geq 0
:

(12)

x_{i,0}=x_{i},\quad y_{i,0}=y_{i},\quad z_{i,0}=z_{i},\quad\bar{x}_{i,0}=\bar{x}_{i},\quad\bar{y}_{i,0}=\bar{y}_{i},\quad\bar{z}_{i,0}=\bar{z}_{i}.

(13)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x_{i,j}\in F^{1}C_{-1},\quad\bar{x}_{i,j}\in F^{1}\bar{C}_{-1},\quad y_{i,j}\in F^{-U}C_{2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{y}_{i,j}\in F^{-U}\bar{C}_{2},\quad z_{i,j}\in F^{-1}C_{1},\quad\bar{z}_{i,j}\in F^{-1}\bar{C}_{1}.

(14)

x_{i,j}=e_{-}(\bar{x}_{i,j}),\quad y_{i,j}=e_{-}(\bar{y}_{i,j}),\quad z_{i,j}=e_{-}(\bar{z}_{i,j}).

(15)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}]\in F^{i+j}\bar{C}_{-2},\qquad\partial\bar{y}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-\bar{z}_{i,j}\in F^{i+j-U-1}\bar{C}_{1}.

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}]\in F^{i+j-2}\bar{C}_{0}.

(16)
