\displaystyle\displaystyle x_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j}C_{-1},\qquad y_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j-U-1}C_{2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle z_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j-2}C_{1}.

(17)

\bar{x}_{i,j+1}-\bar{x}_{i,j}\in F^{i+j}\bar{C}_{-1},\quad\bar{y}_{i,j+1}-\bar{y}_{i,j}\in F^{i+j-U-1}\bar{C}_{2},\quad\bar{z}_{i,j+1}-\bar{z}_{i,j}\in F^{i+j-2}\bar{C}_{1}.

Moreover we require the following conditions:

•

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{i,j}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
,
\displaystyle k\geq 2
.
Moreover,
\displaystyle\bar{x}_{i,j}(0,2)
is a cycle in
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{3}(0))

such that
\displaystyle[\bar{x}_{i,j}(0,2)]
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.

•

\displaystyle\bar{z}_{i,j}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.
Moreover,
\displaystyle\bar{z}_{i,j}(0,0)
is a cycle in
\displaystyle C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{n}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0))

such that
\displaystyle[\bar{z}_{i,j}(0,0)]
corresponds to
\displaystyle(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.

Proof
.

The proof is by induction on
\displaystyle j
.
Let us define
\displaystyle x_{i,0},\ldots,\bar{z}_{i,0}
by (
12
).

Assuming that we have defined a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{i,j},\ldots,\bar{z}_{i,j})_{i\geq I}

which satisfies the required conditions,
we are going to define a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{i,j+1},\ldots,\bar{z}_{i,j+1})_{i\geq I}
.

Let us set

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta^{i}_{x}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=x_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j}C_{-1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta^{i}_{y}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=y_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j-U-1}C_{2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\Delta^{i}_{z}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=z_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j-2}C_{1}.

Since
\displaystyle e_{-}
preserves
\displaystyle\partial
,
\displaystyle[\,,\,]
and filtrations,
(
14
) and (
15
) show that

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial x_{i+1,j}-\frac{1}{2}[x_{i+1,j},x_{i+1,j}]\in F^{i+j+1}C_{-2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial y_{i+1,j}-[x_{i+1,j},y_{i+1,j}]-z_{i+1,j}\in F^{i+j-U}C_{1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial z_{i+1,j}-[x_{i+1,j},z_{i+1,j}]\in F^{i+j-1}C_{-2}.

Then we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{i}_{x}+e_{+}(\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}])\in F^{i+j+1}C_{-2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{i}_{y}+e_{+}(\partial\bar{y}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-\bar{z}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j-U}C_{1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\Delta^{i}_{z}+e_{+}(\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}])\in F^{i+j-1}C_{0}.

On the other hand, we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bigg(\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}]\bigg)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\bigg[\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}],\bar{x}_{i,j}\bigg]-\bigg[\bar{x}_{i,j},\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}]\bigg]\bigg)\in F^{i+j+1}\bar{C}_{-3},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial(\partial\bar{y}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-\bar{z}_{i,j})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\bigg[\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}],\bar{y}_{i,j}\bigg]+[\bar{x}_{i,j},\partial\bar{y}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-\bar{z}_{i,j}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad-(\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}])\in F^{i+j-U}\bar{C}_{0},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial(\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}])

\displaystyle\displaystyle=-\bigg[\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}],\bar{z}_{i,j}\bigg]+[\bar{x}_{i,j},\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}]]\in F^{i+j-1}\bar{C}_{-1}.

Applying Lemma
6.3
for

e_{+}:F^{D}\bar{C}/F^{D+1}\bar{C}\to F^{D}C/F^{D+1}C

where
\displaystyle D=i+j,i+j-U-1,i+j-2

(recall that
\displaystyle e_{+}
is a surjective quasi-isomorphism, as we have seen in Section 4.5),
we show that there exist

\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}\in F^{i+j}\bar{C}_{-1},\quad\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y}\in F^{i+j-U-1}\bar{C}_{2},\quad\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}\in F^{i+j-2}\bar{C}_{1}

such that

e_{+}(\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x})-\Delta^{i}_{x}\in F^{i+j+1}C_{-1},\quad e_{+}(\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y})-\Delta^{i}_{y}\in F^{i+j-U}C_{2},\quad e_{+}(\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z})-\Delta^{i}_{z}\in F^{i+j-1}C_{1}

and

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}+(\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}])\in F^{i+j+1}\bar{C}_{-2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y}+(\partial\bar{y}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-\bar{z}_{i,j})\in F^{i+j-U}\bar{C}_{1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}+(\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}])\in F^{i+j-1}\bar{C}_{0}.

Remark 6.5
.

•

The
\displaystyle(a,k)
-components of
\displaystyle\Delta^{i}_{x}
and
\displaystyle\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}+\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}]
are nonzero only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.
Then we may take
\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}
so that its
\displaystyle(a,k)
-component is nonzero only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.

•

Similarly, we can take
\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}
so that

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}(a,k)\neq 0
only if
\displaystyle\omega(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.

•

Since
\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}\in F^{2}\bar{C}_{-1}
and
\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}\in F^{0}\bar{C}_{1}
,
it follows that

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}(0,k)=0
if
\displaystyle k=0,1,2
and

\displaystyle\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}(0,k)=0
if
\displaystyle k=0
.

Finally, let us set

\bar{x}_{i,j+1}:=\bar{x}_{i,j}+\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x},\quad\bar{y}_{i,j+1}:=\bar{y}_{i,j}+\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y},\quad\bar{z}_{i,j+1}:=\bar{z}_{i,j}+\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}

and

x_{i,j+1}:=e_{-}(\bar{x}_{i,j+1}),\quad y_{i,j+1}:=e_{-}(\bar{y}_{i,j+1}),\quad z_{i,j+1}:=e_{-}(\bar{z}_{i,j+1}).

Now we have to check that, for every
\displaystyle i\geq I
there holds

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{x}_{i,j+1}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j+1},\bar{x}_{i,j+1}]\in F^{i+j+1}\bar{C}_{-2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{y}_{i,j+1}-[\bar{x}_{i,j+1},\bar{y}_{i,j+1}]-\bar{z}_{i,j+1}\in F^{i+j-U}\bar{C}_{1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{z}_{i,j+1}-[\bar{x}_{i,j+1},\bar{z}_{i,j+1}]\in F^{i+j-1}\bar{C}_{0},

\displaystyle\displaystyle x_{i+1,j+1}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i,j+1})\in F^{i+j+1}C_{-1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle y_{i+1,j+1}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i,j+1})\in F^{i+j-U}C_{-2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle z_{i+1,j+1}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i,j+1})\in F^{i+j-1}C_{1}.

The first formula holds since

\partial\bar{x}_{i,j+1}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j+1},\bar{x}_{i,j+1}]=\biggl(\partial\bar{x}_{i,j}+\partial\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{x}_{i,j}]\biggr)-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x},\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}]-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x}]

and all three terms in the RHS are in
\displaystyle F^{i+j+1}\bar{C}_{-2}
.
Similarly, the second and the third formulas follow from

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{y}_{i,j+1}-[\bar{x}_{i,j+1},\bar{y}_{i,j+1}]-\bar{z}_{i,j+1}=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad(\partial\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y}+\partial\bar{y}_{i,j}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-\bar{z}_{i,j})-[\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x},\bar{y}_{i,j}]-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y}]-[\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x},\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y}]-\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial\bar{z}_{i,j+1}-[\bar{x}_{i,j+1},\bar{z}_{i,j+1}]=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad(\partial\bar{z}_{i,j}+\partial\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{z}_{i,j}])-[\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x},\bar{z}_{i,j}]-[\bar{x}_{i,j},\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}]-[\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x},\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}].

The fourth formula holds since

\displaystyle\displaystyle x_{i+1,j+1}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i,j+1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(x_{i+1,j+1}-x_{i+1,j})+(x_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i,j}))+e_{+}(\bar{x}_{i,j}-\bar{x}_{i,j+1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e_{-}(\bar{\Delta}^{i+1}_{x})+(\Delta^{i}_{x}-e_{+}(\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{x})).

Similarly, the fifth and the sixth formulas follow from

\displaystyle\displaystyle y_{i+1,j+1}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i,j+1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(y_{i+1,j+1}-y_{i+1,j})+(y_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i,j}))+e_{+}(\bar{y}_{i,j}-\bar{y}_{i,j+1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e_{-}(\bar{\Delta}^{i+1}_{y})+\Delta^{i}_{y}-e_{+}(\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{y}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle z_{i+1,j+1}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i,j+1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(z_{i+1,j+1}-z_{i+1,j})+(z_{i+1,j}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i,j}))+e_{+}(\bar{z}_{i,j}-\bar{z}_{i,j+1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e_{-}(\bar{\Delta}^{i+1}_{z})+\Delta^{i}_{z}-e_{+}(\bar{\Delta}^{i}_{z}).

Finally, by the induction hypothesis,
\displaystyle\bar{x}_{i,j}
and
\displaystyle\bar{z}_{i,j}
satisfy the last two conditions in the statement.
Then Remark
6.5
shows that
\displaystyle\bar{x}_{i,j+1}
and
\displaystyle\bar{z}_{i,j+1}
also satisfy these conditions.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.1
assuming Theorem
6.1
.

Let us fix an integer
\displaystyle i\geq I
.
Then for every
\displaystyle j\geq 0
, there holds

x_{i,j+1}-x_{i,j}\in F^{i+j}C_{-1},\quad y_{i,j+1}-y_{i,j}\in F^{i+j-U-1}C_{2},\quad z_{i,j+1}-z_{i,j}\in F^{i+j-2}C_{1}.

Then, the limits

x:=\lim_{j\to\infty}x_{i,j}\in\widehat{C}_{-1},\quad y:=\lim_{j\to\infty}y_{i,j}\in\widehat{C}_{2},\quad z:=\lim_{j\to\infty}z_{i,j}\in\widehat{C}_{1}

exist, and satisfy

\partial x-\frac{1}{2}[x,x]=0,\qquad\partial y-[x,y]=z.

Conditions (iii), (iv) in Theorem
5.1
follow from the last two conditions in Lemma
6.4
.
∎

7.
Proof of Theorem
6.1
modulo technical results

Our arguments in the rest of this paper are based on the theory of Kuranishi structures (abbreviated as K-structures),
in particular we heavily rely on
[
10
]
by Fukaya-Oh-Ohta-Ono.
Section 10 very briefly explains some notions in the theory of Kuranishi structures.

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
6.1
modulo technical results,
which are proved in Sections 8 and 9.
Our proof of Theorem
6.1
is based on the following principle:
given a compact K-space
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})

with a CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}=(\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
and a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
,
one can define a de Rham chain
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})
for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon
.
In Section 7.1, we state this principle and its variants in rigorous forms.
Proofs of these results will be carried out in Section 8.
In Sections 7.2–7.6, we apply this principle to prove Theorem
6.1
.

In Section 7.2, we introduce compactified moduli spaces of (perturbed) pseudo-holomorphic disks,
and equip K-structures (with boundaries and corners) on these spaces
so that the (normalized) boundary of each moduli space is naturally isomorphic to a disjoint union
of fiber products of lower (in terms of symplectic area) moduli spaces.
We call this relation “boundary
\displaystyle\cong
fiber product” relation.

To apply the principle in Section 7.1 to moduli spaces defined in Section 7.2,
we have to define
strongly smooth
maps from these moduli spaces to spaces of
smooth
loops with marked points,
so that these maps are compatible with the “boundary
\displaystyle\cong
fiber product” relation.
The idea is to assign the boundary loop to each pseudo-holomorphic disk, however we have to be careful to achieve smoothness,
which can be very subtle on boundary of moduli spaces.
In this paper, we achieve smoothness in the following two steps.
The first step is to define
strongly continuous
maps from these moduli spaces to spaces of
continuous
loops with marked points.
This is much easier and we explain details in Sections 7.3 and 7.4.
The second step is to approximate (with respect to the
\displaystyle C^{0}
-topology) these continuous maps by smooth maps.
In this step we use an abstract approximation result (
\displaystyle C^{0}
-approximation lemma) which we state in Section 7.5 and prove in Section 9.
In Section 7.5, we also introduce CF-perturbations of K-structures on these moduli spaces.
Finally in Section 7.6 we complete the proof assuming technical results presented in Sections 7.1 and 7.5,
which are proved in Sections 8 and 9, respectively.

7.1.
Strongly smooth map from a K-space with a CF-perturbation gives a de Rham chain

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a K-space equipped with a differential form
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}
and a CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}=(\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
.
(The notion of CF-perturbation is explained in Section 7 in
[
10
]
).
Given a strongly smooth map (see Definition
7.1
below)

\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
, we define

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
,
and state Stokes’ formula and fiber product formula.
We also consider the version that
\displaystyle X
is an admissible K-space with boundaries and corners.
The goal of this subsection is to state these results in a formal manner so that we can use them to complete the proof of Theorem
6.1
.
Proofs of these results are explained in Section 8.

7.1.1.
K-space without boundary

Let us start from the following definition:

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a K-space without boundary.
A
strongly smooth map
from
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is a family

\displaystyle\widehat{f}=(f_{p})_{p\in X}
such that the following conditions hold:

•

\displaystyle f_{p}
is a smooth map (in the sense of Definition
4.1
) from
\displaystyle U_{p}
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
.

•

For every
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
,
there holds
\displaystyle f_{p}\circ\varphi_{pq}=f_{q}|_{U_{pq}}
.

The underlying set-theoretic map
\displaystyle X\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is denoted by
\displaystyle f
.

Theorem 7.2
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a compact, oriented K-space of dimension
\displaystyle d

with a
strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
,
a differential form
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}
,
and a CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}=(\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
.
We assume that
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
is transversal to
\displaystyle 0
,
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
(see Definition 9.2 in
[
10
]
).
Under these assumptions, one can define a de Rham chain

(18)

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{d-|\hat{\omega}|}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon
,
so that Stokes’ formula (Theorem
7.5
) and
the fiber product formula (Theorem
7.6
) hold.

Remark 7.3
.

When
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}\equiv 1
, we abbreviate
the LHS of (
18
)
as
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})
.

Remark 7.4
.

In the statement of Theorem
7.2
,
“for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon
” is used slightly loosely.
Strictly speaking, it means the following:
the definition of
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})
involves
some auxiliary choices (good coordinate system and partition of unity),
however it is well-defined in the sense of
\displaystyle\spadesuit
(see Definition
8.12
).
Namely, for any choices
\displaystyle c_{1}
and
\displaystyle c_{2}
, there exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon(c_{1},c_{2})>0
such that,
the definition with
\displaystyle c_{1}
coincides with the definition with
\displaystyle c_{2}
when
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon(c_{1},c_{2}))
.
A similar remark applies to all places in Section 7.1 where we use
“for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon
”.

Stokes’ formula is easy to state:

Theorem 7.5
.

For sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, there holds

\partial(\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}))=(-1)^{|\widehat{\omega}|+1}\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},d\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}).

To state the fiber product formula,
we need some notations.
Suppose, for
\displaystyle i=1,2
, we have the following data:

•

\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
: a compact oriented K-space of dimension
\displaystyle d_{i}
,

•

a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}:(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1}
,

•

a differential form
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{i}
on
\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
,

•

a CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i}
on
\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}_{i}:(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})\to L
is strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i}
.

Due to Theorem
7.2
, one can define

\displaystyle(\widehat{f}_{i})_{*}(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i},\widehat{\omega}_{i},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{i})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{d_{i}-|\widehat{\omega}_{i}|}({\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon
.
On the other hand, for every
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}\}
,
one can take a fiber product of K-spaces and define

(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12}):=(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})\,_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{f}_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}_{2}}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2}).

For the definition of fiber product of K-spaces, see Section 4.1 in
[
10
]
.
Our sign convention for the fiber product is explained in Section 4.2.
One can also define fiber product of CF-perturbations
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}\times\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}

on
\displaystyle(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})
(see Definition 10.13 in
[
10
]
).
Finally we define a differential form
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{12}
on
\displaystyle(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})
by

\widehat{\omega}_{12}:=(-1)^{(d_{1}-|\hat{\omega}_{1}|-n)|\hat{\omega}_{2}|}\cdot\widehat{\omega}_{1}\times\widehat{\omega}_{2}

and a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{12}:(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}
by

(19)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(f_{12})_{(p_{1},p_{2})}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\text{\rm con}\,_{j}((f_{1})_{p_{1}}(x_{1}),(f_{2})_{p_{2}}(x_{2})),

\displaystyle\displaystyle(x_{1}\in U_{p_{1}},\,x_{2}\in U_{p_{2}},\,\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{p_{1}}(x_{1})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f_{p_{2}}(x_{2})).

Then one can state the fiber product formula
(the proof will be sketched in Section 8):

Theorem 7.6
.

In the situation described above, there holds

(\widehat{f}_{12})_{*}(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12},\widehat{\omega}_{12},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{12}^{\varepsilon})=(\widehat{f}_{1})_{*}(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}^{\varepsilon})\circ_{j}(\widehat{f}_{2})_{*}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},\widehat{\omega}_{2},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}^{\varepsilon})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, where the RHS is the fiber product of de Rham chains (see Section 4.3).

7.1.2.
Admissible K-space

Next we consider the case that
\displaystyle X
is an admissible K-space,
which roughly means that
\displaystyle X
is a K-space with boundaries and corners,
and all coordinate change data decay exponentially near boundaries.
For rigorous definitions of
admissible manifolds, vector bundles, and K-structures etc.,
see Section 25 in
[
10
]
.

Definition 7.7
.

(i):

Let
\displaystyle U
be an admissible manifold.
A
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-map

f:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1};\quad u\mapsto(T(u),\gamma(u),t_{1}(u),\ldots,t_{k}(u))

is called
admissible
if the following conditions hold:

•

\displaystyle T,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}:U\to{\mathbb{R}}
are admissible (see Definition 25.3 in
[
10
]
).

•

\displaystyle U\times S^{1}\to L;\,(u,\theta)\mapsto\gamma(u)(T(u)\theta)
is admissible (see Remark
7.8
below).

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f:U\to L
is strata-wise submersive
(i.e. the restriction of
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f
to each open strata of
\displaystyle U
is a submersion).

(ii):

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be an admissible K-space.
An admissible map from
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is a family
\displaystyle\widehat{f}=(f_{p})_{p\in X}
such that

•

\displaystyle f_{p}
is an admissible map from
\displaystyle U_{p}
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
.

•

For every
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
, there holds
\displaystyle f_{p}\circ\varphi_{pq}=f_{q}|_{U_{pq}}
.

The underlying set-theoretic map
\displaystyle X\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

is denoted by
\displaystyle f
.

Remark 7.8
.

In the second bullet of the definition (i) above,
the admissible structure on
\displaystyle U\times S^{1}
is defined as follows:
let
\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l},y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})\,(x_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}},y_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})

be an admissible chart on
\displaystyle U
near a point on the codimension
\displaystyle k
corner,
and
\displaystyle z
be any chart on
\displaystyle S^{1}
.
Then we define the admissible structure on
\displaystyle U\times S^{1}
so that

\displaystyle(x_{1},\ldots,x_{l},y_{1},\ldots,y_{k},z)
is an admissible chart on
\displaystyle U\times S^{1}
.

The next result is a version of Theorem
7.2

for admissible K-spaces.

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a compact, oriented, admissible K-space of dimension
\displaystyle d
,
and

\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
be an admissible map,

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}
be an admissible differential form on
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
, and

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
be an admissible CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
.
We assume that
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
is transversal to
\displaystyle 0
, and

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
.
Then one can define

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{d-|\hat{\omega}|}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, so that Stokes’ formula (Theorem
7.11
) and
the fiber product formula (Theorem
7.12
) hold.

Remark 7.10
.

To define the de Rham chain
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})
,
we put a collar on
\displaystyle X
and take an auxiliary cut-off function on the collar.
It seems that the chain
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})

depends on choice of the cut-off function.
See Section 8.1.2 for more details.

Now we state Stokes’ formula:

Theorem 7.11
.

In the situation of Theorem
7.9
, for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
there holds

\partial(\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}))=(-1)^{|\widehat{\omega}|}\widehat{f}_{*}(\partial X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\partial X},\widehat{\omega}|_{\partial X},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial X})+(-1)^{|\widehat{\omega}|+1}\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},d\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}),

where
\displaystyle\partial X
denotes the normalized boundary of
\displaystyle X
,
which is again an admissible K-space.

Next we state the fiber product formula.
Suppose, for
\displaystyle i=1,2
, we have the following data:

•

An admissible K-space
\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
.

•

An admissible map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}:(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1}
.

•

An admissible differential form
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{i}
on
\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
.

•

An admissible CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i}
on
\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}_{i}:(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})\to L
is strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i}
.

Under these assumptions, for every
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}\}
,
the fiber product

(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12}):=(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})\,_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f_{2}}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2})

has an admissible K-structure.
Moreover, both
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{1}\times\widehat{\omega}_{2}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}\times\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}
are admissible.
Finally, a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{12}:(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}

which is defined by the same formula as (
19
) is also admissible.

Theorem 7.12
.

In the situation described above, there holds

(\widehat{f}_{12})_{*}(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12},\widehat{\omega}_{12},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{12}^{\varepsilon})=(\widehat{f}_{1})_{*}(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}^{\varepsilon})\circ_{j}(\widehat{f}_{2})_{*}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},\widehat{\omega}_{2},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}^{\varepsilon})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
.

7.1.3.
Admissible K-space over an interval

Finally we consider the case that
\displaystyle X
is an admissible K-space and
the target of the map
\displaystyle f
is
\displaystyle[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.

Definition 7.13
.

(i):

Let
\displaystyle U
be an admissible manifold.
A
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-map
\displaystyle f:U\to[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

is called
admissible
if the following conditions hold:

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}\circ f:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is admissible in the sense of Definition
7.7
.

•

Let us denote
\displaystyle\tau:=\text{\rm pr}_{[a,b]}\circ f
, and suppose that
\displaystyle\tau(p)=a
(resp.
\displaystyle\tau(p)=b
) for
\displaystyle p\in U
.
Then
\displaystyle p
is on codimension
\displaystyle k\geq 1

corner of
\displaystyle U
,
and there exists an admissible chart

\displaystyle(y,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\,(t_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})
defined near
\displaystyle p
,
such that

\displaystyle p
corresponds to
\displaystyle(y_{0},0,\ldots,0)
and

\displaystyle\tau(y,t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})=t_{1}+a
(resp.
\displaystyle b-t_{1}
).

•

\displaystyle(\tau,\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}\circ f):U\to[a,b]\times L
is a corner stratified submersion (see Definition 26.3 in
[
10
]
).

(ii):

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be an admissible K-space.
An admissible map from
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
to
\displaystyle[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

is a family
\displaystyle\widehat{f}=(f_{p})_{p\in X}
such that

•

\displaystyle f_{p}
is an admissible map from
\displaystyle U_{p}
to
\displaystyle[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
.

•

For any
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
, there holds
\displaystyle f_{p}\circ\varphi_{pq}=f_{q}|_{U_{pq}}
.

The underlying set-theoretic map

\displaystyle X\to[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

is denoted by
\displaystyle f
.

In the situation of Definition
7.13
(ii), the normalized boundary
\displaystyle\partial X
is decomposed as

\displaystyle\partial X=\partial_{v}X\sqcup\partial_{h}X
, where

\displaystyle\partial_{v}X
(resp.
\displaystyle\partial_{h}X
) denotes the
vertical
(resp.
horizontal
) boundary
(see Definition 26.10 in
[
10
]
).
Moreover,
\displaystyle\partial_{v}X
is decomposed into

\displaystyle\partial_{-}X:=f^{-1}(\{a\}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})
and

\displaystyle\partial_{+}X:=f^{-1}(\{b\}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})
.

Theorem 7.14
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a compact, oriented, admissible K-space of dimension
\displaystyle d
,

\displaystyle\widehat{f}
be an admissible map (in the sense of Definition
7.13
)
from
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
to
\displaystyle[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}
be an admissible differential form on
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
,

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
be an admissible CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
.
Assume that
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
is transversal to
\displaystyle 0
, and

(\text{\rm pr}_{[a,b]}\circ\widehat{f},\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}\circ\widehat{f}):(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to[a,b]\times L

is a stratified submersion with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
.
Then one can define

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{d-|\widehat{\omega}|-1}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, so that there holds

(20)

e_{+}(\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}))=(\widehat{f}|_{\partial_{+}X})_{*}(\partial_{+}X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\partial_{+}X},\widehat{\omega}|_{\partial_{+}X},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial_{+}X}),

(21)

e_{-}(\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}))=(\widehat{f}|_{\partial_{-}X})_{*}(\partial_{-}X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\partial_{-}X},\widehat{\omega}|_{\partial_{-}X},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial_{-}X}).

Moreover Stokes’ formula and the fiber product formula hold.

Let us state Stokes’ formula:

Proposition 7.15
.

For sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, there holds

\partial\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})=(-1)^{|\hat{\omega}|}(\widehat{f}|_{\partial_{h}X})_{*}(\partial_{h}X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\partial_{h}X},\widehat{\omega}|_{\partial_{h}X},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial_{h}X})+(-1)^{|\hat{\omega}|+1}\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},d\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}).

Finally we state the fiber product formula.
Suppose, for
\displaystyle i\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\displaystyle(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{i}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i}
,

\displaystyle k_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{i}:(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})\to[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1}
satisfying the assumptions
in Theorem
7.14
.
For every
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}\}
,
the fiber product

(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12}):=(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})\,_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{f}_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}_{2}}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2})

has an admissible K-structure.
Moreover, both
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}_{1}\times\widehat{\omega}_{2}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}\times\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}
are admissible.
Finally, a smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{12}:(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})\to[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}

which is defined by the same formula as (
19
) is also admissible.

Theorem 7.16
.

In the situation described above, there holds

(\widehat{f}_{12})_{*}(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12},\widehat{\omega}_{12},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{12}^{\varepsilon})=(\widehat{f}_{1})_{*}(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}^{\varepsilon})\circ_{j}(\widehat{f}_{2})_{*}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},\widehat{\omega}_{2},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}^{\varepsilon})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
.

7.2.
Moduli spaces of (perturbed) pseudo-holomorphic disks with boundary marked points

7.2.1.
Uncompactified moduli spaces

Let
\displaystyle D
denote the unit holomorphic disk, namely
\displaystyle D:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid|z|\leq 1\}
.
For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
, we define a set
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)

in the following way.
When
\displaystyle\beta=0
and
\displaystyle k=0
or
\displaystyle k=1
, we define
both

\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{1}(0)
and
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{2}(0)
to be empty set.
In the other cases, namely
\displaystyle\beta\neq 0
or
\displaystyle k\neq 0,1
, we define

{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta):=\{(u,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k})\}/\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)

where
\displaystyle u:(D,\partial D)\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
satisfies
\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u=0
,
\displaystyle[u]=\beta
, and
\displaystyle z_{0},\ldots,z_{k}
are distinct points on
\displaystyle\partial D
aligned in anti-clockwise order.
The right action of
\displaystyle\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)
is defined so that

(u,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k})^{\rho}:=(u\circ\rho,\rho^{-1}(z_{0}),\ldots,\rho^{-1}(z_{k})).

For each
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, we define an evaluation map

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}:{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)\to L
by

(22)

\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}[(u,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k})]:=u(z_{j}).

Next we take a Hamiltonian
\displaystyle H\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,1]\times{\mathbb{C}}^{n})
which displaces
\displaystyle L
.
For every
\displaystyle t\in[0,1]
, let us set
\displaystyle H_{t}(z):=H(t,z)
,
define a Hamiltonian vector field
\displaystyle X_{H_{t}}
by

\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\,\cdot\,,X_{H_{t}})=dH_{t}(\,\cdot\,)
,
and define an isotopy
\displaystyle(\varphi^{t}_{H})_{t\in[0,1]}
on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
by

\varphi^{0}_{H}=\text{\rm id}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{n}},\qquad\partial_{t}\varphi^{t}_{H}=X_{H_{t}}(\varphi^{t}_{H})\quad(\forall t\in[0,1]).

Then “
\displaystyle H
displaces
\displaystyle L
” means that
\displaystyle\varphi^{1}_{H}(L)\cap L=\emptyset
.
We also assume that
\displaystyle H_{t}\equiv 0
when
\displaystyle t\in[0,1/3]\cup[2/3,1]
.

Let us define a family of perturbed Cauchy-Riemann operators
\displaystyle(\bar{\partial}_{r})_{r\geq 0}
in the following way.
We first take a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-function
\displaystyle\chi:{\mathbb{R}}\to[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\chi\equiv 0
on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq 0}

and
\displaystyle\chi\equiv 1
on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 1}
.
For every
\displaystyle r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
, we define

\displaystyle\chi_{r}(s):=\chi(r+s)\chi(-r-s)
.
In particular
\displaystyle\chi_{0}\equiv 0
.

Let us take a complex structure
\displaystyle J
on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]
so that

\displaystyle J(\partial_{s})=\partial_{t}
, where
\displaystyle s
denotes the coordinate on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}
and
\displaystyle t
denotes the coordinate on
\displaystyle[0,1]
.
Next we take a biholomorphic map from
\displaystyle D\setminus\{-1,1\}
to
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]
.
This defines
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-functions

\displaystyle s:D\setminus\{-1,1\}\to{\mathbb{R}}

and
\displaystyle t:D\setminus\{-1,1\}\to[0,1]
.
For every
\displaystyle r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}

and
\displaystyle u:(D,\partial D)\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
, we define

\bar{\partial}_{r}u:=(du-X_{\chi_{r}(s)H_{t}}(u)\otimes dt)^{0,1}.

Obviously
\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{0}=\bar{\partial}
.
Now let us define

{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{k+1}(\beta):=\{(u,z_{0}=1,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\}

where
\displaystyle u:(D,\partial D)\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
satisfies
\displaystyle\bar{\partial}_{r}u=0
,
\displaystyle[u]=\beta
, and
\displaystyle 1,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
are distinct points on
\displaystyle\partial D
aligned in anti-clockwise order.
For each
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
the evaluation map
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}:{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{k+1}(\beta)\to L
is defined in the same formula as (
22
).
Finally we define

{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta):=\bigcup_{r\geq 0}{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{k+1}(\beta).

Let us summarize basic properties of these moduli spaces:

Lemma 7.17
.

(i):

If
\displaystyle\omega(\beta)<0
or
\displaystyle\omega(\beta)=0
and
\displaystyle\beta\neq 0
, then
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)={\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)=\emptyset
.

(ii):

Consider the case
\displaystyle\beta=0
:

•

\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(0)
consists of constant maps for every
\displaystyle k\geq 2
.

•

\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k+1}(0)
consists of constant maps for every
\displaystyle k\geq 0
.

(iii):

Let
\displaystyle\|\,\cdot\,\|
denote the Hofer’s norm, namely

\|H\|:=\int_{0}^{1}\,(\max H_{t}-\min H_{t})\,dt.

If
\displaystyle\omega(\beta)+2\|H\|<0
, then
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)=\emptyset
.

(iv):

First notice that, for every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle r\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
,

{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)=\emptyset\iff{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{1}(\beta)=\emptyset,\qquad{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{k+1}(\beta)=\emptyset\iff{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{1}(\beta)=\emptyset.

Now for every
\displaystyle c\in{\mathbb{R}}
, sets

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)\mid{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{1}(\beta)\neq\emptyset,\,\omega(\beta)<c\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)\mid{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{1}(\beta)\neq\emptyset,\,\omega(\beta)<c\}

are both finite.

(v):

For every
\displaystyle\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
,
there exists
\displaystyle r(\beta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{r\geq r(\beta)}{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{r}_{1}(\beta)=\emptyset
.

Proof
.

(i), (ii) are straightforward from definitions.
(iii) follows from standard computations.
(iv) follows from Gromov compactness theorem.
If (v) is not the case, there exists

\displaystyle v:({\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1],{\mathbb{R}}\times\{0,1\})\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
satisfying

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]}|\partial_{s}v|^{2}\,dsdt<\infty,\qquad\partial_{s}v-J(\partial_{t}v-X_{H_{t}}(v))=0,

where
\displaystyle J
denotes the standard complex structure on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
.
Then
\displaystyle\gamma(t):=\lim_{s\to\infty}v(s,t)
satisfies

\displaystyle\gamma(0),\gamma(1)\in L
and
\displaystyle\partial_{t}\gamma(t)=X_{H_{t}}(\gamma(t))
,
contradicting the assumption that
\displaystyle H
displaces
\displaystyle L
.
∎

7.2.2.
Compactified moduli spaces

In this subsubsection, we define compactified moduli spaces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)
,
taking fiber products of uncompactified moduli spaces along decorated rooted ribbon trees
(see Definition
7.18
below).
K-structures on these spaces are defined in the next subsubsection.

The next definition is a modified version
of Definition 21.2 in
[
10
]
.

Definition 7.18
.

A
decorated rooted ribbon tree
is a pair
\displaystyle(T,B)
such that:

•

\displaystyle T
is a connected tree. Let
\displaystyle C_{0}(T)
and
\displaystyle C_{1}(T)
be the set of all vertices and edges of
\displaystyle T
, respectively.

•

For each
\displaystyle v\in C_{0}(T)
we fix a cyclic order of the set of edges containing
\displaystyle v
(ribbon structure).

•

\displaystyle C_{0}(T)
is divided into the set of
exterior
vertices
\displaystyle C_{0,\text{\rm ext}\,}(T)
and the set of
interior
vertices
\displaystyle C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)
.
For every
\displaystyle v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)
, we define
\displaystyle k_{v}
to be the valency of
\displaystyle v
minus
\displaystyle 1
.

•

We fix one element of
\displaystyle C_{0,\text{\rm ext}\,}(T)
, which we call the
root
.

•

The valency of every exterior vertex is
\displaystyle 1
.

•

\displaystyle B
is a map from
\displaystyle C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)
to
\displaystyle H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
.
For every vertex
\displaystyle v
, either
\displaystyle\omega_{n}(B(v))>0
or
\displaystyle B(v)=0
holds.

•

Every vertex
\displaystyle v
with
\displaystyle B(v)=0
has valency at least
\displaystyle 3
.

For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
,
we denote by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)

the set of decorated rooted ribbon trees
\displaystyle(T,B)
such that:

(I):

\displaystyle\#C_{0,\text{\rm ext}\,}(T)=k+1
.

(II):

\displaystyle\sum_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}B(v)=\beta
.

An edge is called
exterior
if it contains an exterior vertex.
Otherwise it is called
interior
.

\displaystyle C_{1,\text{\rm ext}\,}(T)
(resp.
\displaystyle C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)
)
denotes the set of exterior (resp. interior) edges.

We also define a natural notion of
reduction
on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(k+1:\beta)
.

Definition 7.19
.

Let
\displaystyle(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1:\beta)
and
\displaystyle e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)
, and
\displaystyle v_{0}
,
\displaystyle v_{1}
be vertices of
\displaystyle e
.
By collapsing
\displaystyle e
to a new vertex
\displaystyle v_{01}
, we get
\displaystyle(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1:\beta)
such that

\displaystyle\displaystyle C_{0}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=(C_{0}(T)\setminus\{v_{0},v_{1}\})\cup\{v_{01}\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle C_{1}(T^{\prime})

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=C_{1}(T)\setminus\{e\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle B^{\prime}(v)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}B(v)&(v\neq v_{01})\\
B(v_{0})+B(v_{1})&(v=v_{01}).\end{cases}

An element of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(k+1:\beta)
which can be obtained from
\displaystyle(T,B)
by repeating
this process is called a
reduction
of
\displaystyle(T,B)
.

Now let us define moduli spaces
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)
.
For every
\displaystyle(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)
,
one can define

(23)

\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}:\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\to\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L^{2}

in the same manner as the definition of the
map (21.2) in
[
10
]
.
We also consider

(24)

\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm ext}\,}:\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\to\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm ext}\,}(T)}L\cong L^{k+1}

where the isomorphism on the right is defined
by labeling exterior vertices by
\displaystyle\{0,\ldots,k\}
in positive cyclic order so that
the root is labeled by
\displaystyle 0
.

We also consider the diagonal map:

(25)

\Delta:\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L\to\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L^{2};\quad(x_{e})_{e}\mapsto(x_{e},x_{e})_{e}.

Then we define
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)

by taking fiber products of (
23
) and (
25
).
Namely

{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta):=\bigsqcup_{(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)}\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\bigg).

We define
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}=(\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{0},\ldots,\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k}):{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)\to L^{k+1}

by restricting
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm ext}}\,
.

The definition of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
is similar.
For any
\displaystyle(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)

and
\displaystyle v_{0}\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)
,
we define

(26)

\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}:\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\setminus\{v_{0}\}}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\times{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}(B(v_{0}))\to\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L^{2}

and

(27)

\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm ext}\,}:\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\setminus\{v_{0}\}}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\times{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}(B(v_{0}))\to L^{k+1}

in manners similar to (
23
) and (
24
).
Then we define:

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)\\
v_{0}\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\setminus\{v_{0}\}}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\times{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}(B(v_{0}))\bigg).

We define
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}}=(\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}}_{0},\ldots,\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}}_{k}):{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)\to L^{k+1}

by restring
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm ext}}\,
defined in (
27
).

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)

and

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}}:{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)\to L^{k+1}

are defined in a similar way.

Now we have defined
sets

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)
.
These sets have natural topologies:
the topology on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
is described in Section 7.1.4
[
8
]

(actually our situation is much simpler, since we have neither interior marked points nor sphere bubbles).
The topologies on

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)
are defined in similar ways,
and details are omitted.

7.2.3.
K-structures on compactified moduli spaces

In this subsubsection, we consider a system of K-structures on compactified moduli spaces defined in the previous subsubsection.
More accurately, we consider admissible K-structures (see Section 25 in
[
10
]
),
and define a certain inductive system of such admissible K-spaces (this is a variant of “inductive system of
\displaystyle A_{\infty}
correspondence” in Definition 21.17 in
[
10
]
).
Our goal in this subsubsection is to spell out an accurate statement in Theorem
7.20
.

Recall that we took
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
so that
every nonconstant holomorphic disk with boundary on
\displaystyle L
has area at least
\displaystyle 2\varepsilon
.
We take
\displaystyle U\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{>0}
so that

\displaystyle\varepsilon(U-1)\geq 2\|H\|
.

Theorem 7.20
.

For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle P\in\{\{m\},[m,m+1]\}
,
there exist the following data:

(i):

(Moduli spaces)

Compact, oriented, admissible K-spaces

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\qquad(\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L),\,\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k)),

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\qquad(\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L),\,\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k)),

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\qquad(\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L),\,\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-k-U)),

whose underlying topological spaces are

P\times{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta),\quad P\times{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta),\quad P\times{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta),

respectively.
Dimensions of these K-spaces are given by

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)=\mu(\beta)+n+k-2+\text{\rm dim}\,P,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)=\mu(\beta)+n+k+\text{\rm dim}\,P,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)=\mu(\beta)+n+k+1+\text{\rm dim}\,P.

(ii):

(Evaluation maps)

Corner stratified strongly smooth maps
(from K-spaces to manifolds with corners, see Definition 26.6 (1) in
[
10
]
)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}},P}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times L^{k+1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0},P}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times L^{k+1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0},P}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times L^{k+1},

such that their underlying set-theoretic maps are:

\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}},\quad\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}},\quad\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}}.

We require that the following maps to
\displaystyle P\times L

(\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm pr}_{0})\circ\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}},P},\quad(\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm pr}_{0})\circ\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0},P},\quad(\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm pr}_{0})\circ\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0},P}

are corner stratified weak submersions (see Definition 26.6 (2) in
[
10
]
).
Here
\displaystyle\text{\rm pr}_{0}:L^{k+1}\to L
is defined by
\displaystyle(p_{0},\ldots,p_{k})\mapsto p_{0}
.

(iii):

(Energy zero part)

An isomorphism of admissible K-structures

{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(0:P)\cong P\times L\times D^{k-2}

for every
\displaystyle k\geq 2
,
so that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}},P}:{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(0:P)\to P\times L^{k+1}

coincides with
\displaystyle\text{\rm pr}_{P}\times(\text{\rm pr}_{L})^{k+1}
.
Here
\displaystyle D^{k-2}
in the RHS is identified with the Stasheff cell; see
[
7
]
Section 10.
The coordinate near boundary is

\displaystyle 1/\log T
,
where
\displaystyle T
denotes the length of neck region;
see Remark 25.45 in
[
10
]
.

(iv):

(Compatibility at boundaries)

The following isomorphisms of admissible K-spaces preserving orientations
(
\displaystyle\partial
in the LHS denotes normalized boundaries,
and the fiber product

\,{}_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{i}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}}
is abbreviated as
\,{}_{i}\!\times_{0}
):

(28)

\partial{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\cong\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:m)\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:m),

(29)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\cong

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}\biggl((-1)^{\varepsilon_{1}}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:m)\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:m)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{2}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:m)\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:m)\biggr),

(30)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\cong{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\sqcup

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}\biggl((-1)^{\varepsilon_{3}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:m)\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:m)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{4}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:m)\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:m)\biggr),

(31)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])\cong(-1)^{\varepsilon_{5}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{6}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:m+1)\,\sqcup

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{7}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:[m,m+1])\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:[m,m+1]),

(32)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])\cong(-1)^{\varepsilon_{8}}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{9}}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:m+1)\,\sqcup

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}\biggl((-1)^{\varepsilon_{10}}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:[m,m+1])\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:[m,m+1])

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{11}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:[m,m+1])\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:[m,m+1])\biggr),

(33)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])\cong(-1)^{\varepsilon_{12}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{13}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:m+1)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{14}}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])\sqcup

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}\biggl((-1)^{\varepsilon_{15}}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:[m,m+1])\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:[m,m+1])

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sqcup(-1)^{\varepsilon_{16}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:[m,m+1])\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:[m,m+1])\biggr).

The signs
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0},\ldots,\varepsilon_{16}
are given as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(k_{1}-i)(k_{2}-1)+n+k_{1},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{0}+k+k_{1},\,\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{0}+k+k_{2},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon_{3}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{1}+1,\,\varepsilon_{4}=\varepsilon_{2}+k_{1}+1,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon_{5}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=1,\,\varepsilon_{6}=0,\,\varepsilon_{7}=\varepsilon_{0}+1,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon_{8}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=1,\,\varepsilon_{9}=0,\,\varepsilon_{10}=\varepsilon_{1}+1,\,\varepsilon_{11}=\varepsilon_{2}+1,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\varepsilon_{12}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=1,\,\varepsilon_{13}=0,\,\varepsilon_{14}=1,\,\varepsilon_{15}=\varepsilon_{3}+1,\,\varepsilon_{16}=\varepsilon_{4}+1.

(v):

(Compatibility at corners I)

First we introduce the following notations:

•

For any admissible K-space
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
let
\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l}X
denote the normalized codimension
\displaystyle l
corner of
\displaystyle X
;
see
[
10
]
Definition 24.17.

•

For every nonnegative integers
\displaystyle d
and
\displaystyle m
, we denote

\widehat{S}_{d}\{m\}:=\begin{cases}\{m\}&(d=0)\\
\emptyset&(d\geq 1),\end{cases}\qquad\widehat{S}_{d}[m,m+1]:=\begin{cases}[m,m+1]&(d=0)\\
\{m,m+1\}&(d=1)\\
\emptyset&(d\geq 2).\end{cases}

Then, there are the following isomorphisms of admissible K-spaces
(
34
), (
35
), and (
36
):
here we do not consider orientations of moduli spaces.

(34)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l}{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\cong

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)\\
\#C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)+d=l\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}\widehat{S}_{d}P\times L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\bigg)

where the fiber product in the RHS is taken over
\displaystyle\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}(\widehat{S}_{d}P\times L)^{2}
.
Notice that the fiber product makes sense, since

(\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm pr}_{0})\circ\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}},P}:{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times L

is a corner stratified weak submersion, as we assumed in (ii).

(35)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\cong\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)\\
\#C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)+d=l\\
v_{0}\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}\widehat{S}_{d}P\times L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\setminus\{v_{0}\}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\times{\mathcal{N}}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}^{0}(B(v_{0}):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\bigg).

(36)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l}{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\cong\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)\\
\#C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)+d=l\\
v_{0}\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}\widehat{S}_{d}P\times L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\setminus\{v_{0}\}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\times{\mathcal{N}}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}^{\geq 0}(B(v_{0}):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\bigg)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\sqcup\,\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)\\
\#C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)+d=l-1\\
v_{0}\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\end{subarray}}\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}\widehat{S}_{d}P\times L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)\setminus\{v_{0}\}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\times{\mathcal{N}}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}^{0}(B(v_{0}):\widehat{S}_{d}P)\bigg).

(vi):

(Compatibility at corners II)

Let
\displaystyle X
be either
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
or
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
.
Then, for every
\displaystyle l,l^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
the canonical covering map

\displaystyle\pi_{l^{\prime},l}:\widehat{S}_{l^{\prime}}(\widehat{S}_{l}X)\to\widehat{S}_{l+l^{\prime}}X

(see Proposition 24.16 in
[
10
]
)
coincides with the map defined from the fiber product presentation in (v).

Construction of these K-structures are mostly the same as the
construction of K-structure on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
,
which is explained in
[
8
]
Section 7.1.
Evaluation maps in (ii)
and isomorphisms in (iv), (v) naturally follow from the construction of these K-structures.
Admissibility of this K-structure is a consequence of the exponential decay estimate in
[
9
]
; see Section 25.5 in
[
10
]
.
We do not spell out a detailed proof of Theorem
7.20
.
Nevertheless, in the rest of this subsubsection, we explain:

•

Explicit description of Kuranishi charts of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
, which we use in Section 7.4.

•

Total order on the set of moduli spaces, which we need to carry out inductive argument.

•

Orientations: how signs
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0},\ldots,\varepsilon_{16}
are computed.

Explicit description of Kuranishi charts of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
.

Let
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
.
Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{MM}}_{k+1}(\beta)
denote the set consists of tuples
\displaystyle(u,z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})
such that

\displaystyle u:(D,\partial D)\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-map such that
\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u=0
on a
neighborhood of
\displaystyle\partial D

and
\displaystyle[u]=\beta
, and
\displaystyle z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
are distinct points on
\displaystyle\partial D
aligned in the anti-clockwise order.

Remark 7.21
.

To define K-structure on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
we consider an obstruction bundle
\displaystyle E
and a perturbed Cauchy-Riemann equation
\displaystyle\bar{\partial}u\in E
.
One can take
\displaystyle E
so that every section of
\displaystyle E
is supported on a compact set of
\displaystyle\text{int}D
,
thus these perturbed holomorphic maps (with boundary marked points) are elements in
\displaystyle{\mathcal{MM}}_{k+1}(\beta)
.

Now we can state the explicit description of Kuranishi chart in Lemma
7.22
below.

Lemma 7.22
.

Let
\displaystyle p\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{p}=(U_{p},{\mathcal{E}}_{p},s_{p},\psi_{p})
be a K-chart at
\displaystyle p
.
Let
\displaystyle(T,B)
be an element in
\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(k+1:\beta)
such that

p\in P\times\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v))\bigg).

Then,
\displaystyle U_{p}
can be (set theoretically) embedded into

\bigsqcup_{(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})}P\times\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T^{\prime})}L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T^{\prime})}{\mathcal{MM}}_{k_{v}+1}(B^{\prime}(v))\bigg)

where
\displaystyle(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})
runs over all reductions (see Definition
7.19
) of
\displaystyle(T,B)
.

Total order on the set of moduli spaces.

To achieve compatibility conditions (iv), (v) and (vi) in Theorem
7.20
,
we need a total order on the set of moduli spaces,
so that the following property is satisfied:
if a moduli space
\displaystyle X
is a part of the normalized boundary
\displaystyle\partial Y
of
another moduli space
\displaystyle Y
, then
\displaystyle X<Y
.
To achieve this property we take a total order
which is described as follows:

•

If the
\displaystyle P
-part of a moduli space
\displaystyle X
is
\displaystyle\{m\}
and the
\displaystyle P
-part of a moduli space
\displaystyle Y
is
\displaystyle[m^{\prime},m^{\prime}+1]
, then
\displaystyle X<Y

(
\displaystyle m
and
\displaystyle m^{\prime}
are arbitrary elements in
\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
).

•

If the
\displaystyle P
-part of
\displaystyle X
is
\displaystyle\{m\}
and the
\displaystyle P
-part of
\displaystyle Y
is
\displaystyle\{m+1\}
, then
\displaystyle X<Y
.

•

If the
\displaystyle P
-part of
\displaystyle X
is
\displaystyle[m,m+1]
and the
\displaystyle P
-part of
\displaystyle Y
is
\displaystyle[m+1,m+2]
, then
\displaystyle X<Y
.

•

For each
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we take a total order on moduli spaces with
\displaystyle P
-parts equal to
\displaystyle\{m\}
, in the following manner:

–

For any
\displaystyle k,k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle\beta,\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime\prime}\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
,

{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:m)<{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k^{\prime}+1}(\beta^{\prime}:m)<{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k^{\prime\prime}+1}(\beta^{\prime\prime}:m).

–

If
\displaystyle k,k^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
satisfy

\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\beta)+\varepsilon(k-1)<\omega_{n}(\beta^{\prime})+\varepsilon(k^{\prime}-1)
, then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:m)<{\mathcal{M}}_{k^{\prime}+1}(\beta^{\prime}:m)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)<{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k^{\prime}+1}(\beta^{\prime}:m)
, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)<{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k^{\prime}+1}(\beta^{\prime}:m)
.

–

For each
\displaystyle c\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
we choose arbitrary total orders on sets

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\mid\omega_{n}(\beta)+\varepsilon(k-1)=c\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\mid\omega_{n}(\beta)+\varepsilon(k-1)=c\},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\mid\omega_{n}(\beta)+\varepsilon(k-1)=c\}.

•

For each
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we take a total order on moduli spaces with
\displaystyle P
-parts equal to
\displaystyle[m,m+1]
,
in a manner similar to above.

Remark 7.23
.

For each moduli space
\displaystyle X
,
there are only finitely many moduli spaces which are smaller than
\displaystyle X
.
Therefore, we can assign
\displaystyle\tau(X)\in(1/2,1)
for each moduli space
\displaystyle X
,
so that
\displaystyle Y<X\implies\tau(Y)>\tau(X)
.
We use these numbers in Section 7.6.

Orientations.

We explain how signs
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0},\ldots,\varepsilon_{16}
are computed.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}
: First we orient
\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)
following Section 8.3 in
[
8
]
.
It is sufficient to orient its interior
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)=\{(u,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k})\}/\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)
.
We consider the set
\displaystyle\{(u,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k})\}
as an open subset of

\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}(\beta)\times(\partial D)^{k+1}
, where

{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}(\beta):=\{u:(D,\partial D)\to({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)\mid\bar{\partial}u=0,\,[u]=\beta\}

is canonically oriented by Theorem 8.1.1 in
[
8
]
and

\displaystyle\partial D=\{e^{i\theta}\mid\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}\}
is oriented so that
\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}
is of positive direction
(anti-clockwise orientation).
On the other hand,
following Convention 8.3.1 in
[
8
]
,

\displaystyle\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)
is oriented so that the diffeomorphism

\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)\to(\partial D)^{3};\,\rho\mapsto(\rho^{-1}(1),\rho^{-1}(e^{2\pi i/3}),\rho^{-1}(e^{4\pi i/3}))

is orientation preserving.
Finally, the quotient
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)
is oriented so that a natural isomorphism (determined uniquely up to homotopy)

T({\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta))\oplus T(\text{\rm Aut}\,(D))\cong T({\mathcal{M}}(\beta))\oplus T(\partial D)^{\oplus k+1}

preserves orientations (see page 692 in
[
8
]
).

Now let us compute
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}
.
When
\displaystyle i=1
, Proposition 8.3.3 in
[
8
]
shows
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}=(k_{1}-1)(k_{2}-1)+n+k_{1}
.
For arbitrary
\displaystyle i\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}\}
,
we can compute
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}
as

\varepsilon_{0}=(k_{1}-1)(k_{2}-1)+n+k_{1}+(i-1)+(i-1)k_{2}=(k_{1}-i)(k_{2}-1)+n+k_{1}

where the term
\displaystyle(i-1)
comes from exchanging input boundary marked points
\displaystyle 1,\ldots,i-1
and
\displaystyle i
,
and the term
\displaystyle(i-1)k_{2}
comes from exchanging output boundary marked points
\displaystyle 1,\ldots,i-1
and
\displaystyle i,\ldots,i+k_{2}-1
.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{2}
:
We orient
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)=\{(u,z_{0}=1,z_{1},\ldots,z_{k})\}

as an open subset of
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}(\beta)\times(\partial D)^{k}
.
Then
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{0}+k+k_{1}
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{0}+k+k_{2}
follow from the next lemma.
Following
[
8
]
page 694,
we say that an isomorphism of K-spaces is
\displaystyle(-1)
-oriented (resp.
\displaystyle(+1)
-oriented)
if the isomorphism reverses (resp. preserves) orientations.

Lemma 7.24
.

Let us orient
\displaystyle\text{\rm Aut}\,(D,1)
so that the embedding

\text{\rm Aut}\,(D,1)\to(\partial D)^{2};\,\rho\mapsto(\rho^{-1}(e^{2\pi i/3}),\rho^{-1}(e^{4\pi i/3}))

preserves orientations.
Then, the isomorphism of K-spaces

{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)\cong{\mathring{{\mathcal{N}}}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)/\text{\rm Aut}\,(D,1)

is
\displaystyle(-1)^{k}
-oriented.

Proof
.

Recall that
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{M}}}}_{k+1}(\beta)=\{(u,z_{0},\ldots,z_{k})\}/\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)
.
The natural diffeomorphism
\displaystyle\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)\cong\partial D\times\text{\rm Aut}\,(D,1)
preserves orientations,
and the first factor
\displaystyle\partial D
acts on the
\displaystyle 0
-th marked point
\displaystyle z_{0}
,
thus the factor
\displaystyle(-1)^{k}
comes from exchanging
\displaystyle z_{0}
and
\displaystyle z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
.
∎

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{3}
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{4}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta)
can be identified (on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
) with

\displaystyle[0,1)\times{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta)
.
Following Convention 8.2.1 in
[
8
]
,
we orient
\displaystyle[0,1)
so that
\displaystyle\partial/\partial t
is of
negative
direction,
where
\displaystyle t
denotes the canonical coordinate on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then

\partial([0,1)\times{\mathcal{N}}^{0})=\{0\}\times{\mathcal{N}}^{0}\sqcup(-1)\cdot[0,1)\times\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{0}

and
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{3}=\varepsilon_{1}+1
immediately follows.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{4}=\varepsilon_{2}+k_{1}+1
follows from

[0,1)\times({\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1})\,_{i}\!\times_{0}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}))=(-1)^{\delta}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1})\,_{i}\!\times_{0}([0,1)\times{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}))

where
\displaystyle\delta=\text{\rm dim}\,{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1})-n\equiv k_{1}\,(\text{mod}\,2)
.

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{5},\ldots,\varepsilon_{16}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])
and
\displaystyle[m,m+1]\times{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta)

are different K-spaces with a common underlying topological space.
One can take a common open substructure
(see Definition 3.20 in
[
10
]
)
of these two K-spaces.
Thus, to fix orientations, one can identify these two K-spaces.
The same remark applies to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:[m,m+1])
.
Now let us orient
\displaystyle[m,m+1]
so that
\displaystyle\partial/\partial t
is of
positive
direction,
where
\displaystyle t
denotes the canonical coordinate on
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}
.
Then

\partial([m,m+1])=(+1)\cdot\{m+1\}\sqcup(-1)\cdot\{m\}.

Then
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{5},\ldots,\varepsilon_{16}
can be determined from

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0},\ldots,\varepsilon_{4}
using the following formulas (
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle Y
denote arbitrary K-spaces):

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial([m,m+1]\times X)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\{m+1\}\times X\sqcup(-1)\cdot\{m\}\times X\sqcup(-1)\cdot[m,m+1]\times\partial X,

\displaystyle\displaystyle[m,m+1]\times(X\times_{L}Y)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=([m,m+1]\times X)\times_{[m,m+1]\times L}([m,m+1]\times Y).

7.3.
Spaces of continuous paths and loops

Let
\displaystyle\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
denote the set of continuous Moore paths on
\displaystyle L
, namely:

\Pi^{\text{\rm con}\,}:=\{(T,\gamma)\mid T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0},\,\gamma\in C^{0}([0,T],L)\}.

For each
\displaystyle q\in L
, let
\displaystyle c_{q}
denote the constant path at
\displaystyle q
with length
\displaystyle 0
.
Namely,
\displaystyle c_{q}=(0,\gamma_{q})

where
\displaystyle\gamma_{q}
denotes the unique map from
\displaystyle\{0\}
to
\displaystyle q
.

To define a metric
\displaystyle d_{\Pi}
on
\displaystyle\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
,
we fix (throughout this paper) an auxiliary Riemannian metric on
\displaystyle L
,
and
\displaystyle d_{L}
denotes the associated metric on
\displaystyle L
.
For later use we fix a constant
\displaystyle\rho_{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}

which is smaller than the injectivity radius of
\displaystyle L

such that for any
\displaystyle r\in(0,\rho_{L}]
and
\displaystyle x\in L

the ball with center
\displaystyle x
and radius
\displaystyle r
is geodesically convex.

Now let us define a metric
\displaystyle d_{\Pi}
on
\displaystyle\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
by

d_{\Pi}((T,\gamma),(T^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}))):=\max\big\{|T-T^{\prime}|,\,\max_{0\leq s\leq 1}d_{L}(\gamma(sT),\gamma^{\prime}(sT^{\prime}))\big\}.

We define
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0},\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}:\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,\to L
by

\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(T,\gamma):=\gamma(0),\qquad\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(T,\gamma):=\gamma(T).

Then we can define a concatenation map

\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,\,_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}}\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,\to\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,;\qquad(\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1})\mapsto\Gamma_{0}*\Gamma_{1}

by
\displaystyle(T,\gamma)*(T^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}):=(T+T^{\prime},\gamma*\gamma^{\prime})
,
where

\displaystyle\gamma*\gamma^{\prime}:[0,T+T^{\prime}]\to L
is defined by

(\gamma*\gamma^{\prime})(t):=\begin{cases}\gamma(t)&(0\leq t\leq T),\\
\gamma^{\prime}(t-T)&(T\leq t\leq T+T^{\prime}).\end{cases}

Lemma 7.25
.

The concatenation map defined as above is continuous with respect to
\displaystyle d_{\Pi}
.

Proof
.

We have to show that, if sequences

\displaystyle(T_{j},\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
and

\displaystyle(T^{\prime}_{j},\gamma^{\prime}_{j})_{j\geq 1}
satisfy

\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Pi}((T_{j},\gamma_{j}),(T,\gamma))=\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Pi}((T^{\prime}_{j},\gamma^{\prime}_{j}),(T^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}))=0

then

(37)

\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Pi}((T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j},\gamma_{j}*\gamma^{\prime}_{j}),(T+T^{\prime},\gamma*\gamma^{\prime}))=0.

If (
37
) is not the case, there exists a sequence
\displaystyle(s_{j})_{j\geq 1}
in
\displaystyle[0,1]
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
such that

(38)

d_{L}((\gamma_{j}*\gamma^{\prime}_{j})(s_{j}(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})),(\gamma*\gamma^{\prime})(s_{j}(T+T^{\prime})))\geq\varepsilon

for every
\displaystyle j
.
Then
\displaystyle T_{j},T^{\prime}_{j}>0
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle j
, thus we may assume that

\displaystyle T_{j},T^{\prime}_{j}>0
holds for every
\displaystyle j\geq 1
.

By taking a subsequence,
we may also assume that either
\displaystyle s_{j}\leq T_{j}/(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})
or
\displaystyle s_{j}\geq T_{j}/(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})
holds for all
\displaystyle j
.
In the following we only consider the case
\displaystyle s_{j}\leq T_{j}/(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})
, since the proof in the other case is parallel.
Now we obtain

(\gamma_{j}*\gamma^{\prime}_{j})(s_{j}(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j}))=\gamma_{j}\biggl(s_{j}\cdot\frac{T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j}}{T_{j}}\cdot T_{j}\biggr).

We set
\displaystyle\tau_{j}:=s_{j}(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})/T_{j}\in[0,1]
. Then the LHS of (
38
) is bounded from above by

(39)

d_{L}(\gamma_{j}(\tau_{j}T_{j}),\gamma(\tau_{j}T))+d_{L}(\gamma(\tau_{j}T),(\gamma*\gamma^{\prime})(s_{j}(T+T^{\prime}))).

Then the first term of (
39
) goes to
\displaystyle 0
as
\displaystyle j\to\infty
, since

d_{L}(\gamma_{j}(\tau_{j}T),\gamma(\tau_{j}T))\leq d_{\Pi}((T_{j},\gamma_{j}),(T,\gamma)).

Since
\displaystyle\gamma*\gamma^{\prime}
is (uniformly) continuous,
to show that
the second term of (
39
) goes to
\displaystyle 0
as
\displaystyle j\to\infty
,
it is sufficient to check:

(40)

\lim_{j\to\infty}\tau_{j}T-s_{j}(T+T^{\prime})=0.

Using the obvious identity

(41)

\tau_{j}T-s_{j}(T+T^{\prime})=s_{j}(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})(T/T_{j})-s_{j}(T+T^{\prime}),

we can check (
40
) by considering the following three cases:

•

\displaystyle T>0
: this case
\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}T/T_{j}=1
, thus the RHS of (
41
) goes to
\displaystyle 0
.

•

\displaystyle T=0
and
\displaystyle T^{\prime}>0
: this case the first term is
\displaystyle 0
, and the second term goes to
\displaystyle 0
because

\displaystyle 0\leq s_{j}\leq T_{j}/(T_{j}+T^{\prime}_{j})
implies
\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}s_{j}=0
.

•

\displaystyle T,T^{\prime}=0
: this case both the first and second terms are
\displaystyle 0
.

This completes the proof.
∎

Next we consider the space of continuous Moore loops with marked points.
For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
denote the set consists of

\displaystyle(T,\gamma,t_{0},\ldots,t_{k})
such that

•

\displaystyle T\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle\gamma\in C^{0}([0,T],L)
such that
\displaystyle\gamma(0)=\gamma(T)
.

•

\displaystyle 0=t_{0}\leq t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{k}\leq T
.

For each
\displaystyle a\in H_{1}(L:{\mathbb{Z}})
,
let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(a)
denote the subspace of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
which consists of

\displaystyle(T,\gamma,t_{0},\ldots,t_{k})
such that
\displaystyle[\gamma]=a
.
The set
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
can be identified with the set

\{(\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{k})\in(\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,)^{k+1}\mid\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(\Gamma_{i})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(\Gamma_{i+1})\,(0\leq i\leq k-1),\,\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(\Gamma_{k})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(\Gamma_{0})\}.

Using this identification, a metric
\displaystyle d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}
on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
is defined by

d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}((\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{k}),(\Gamma^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,\Gamma^{\prime}_{k})):=\max_{0\leq i\leq k}d_{\Pi}(\Gamma_{i},\Gamma^{\prime}_{i}).

We consider the topology on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
induced from
\displaystyle d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}
.

The evaluation map

\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}:{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,\to L\quad(j\in\{0,\ldots,k\})

and the concatenation map

\text{\rm con}\,_{j}:{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,\,{}_{\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}}{\mathcal{L}}_{k^{\prime}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+k^{\prime}}^{\text{\rm con}}\,\quad(j\in\{1,\ldots,k\})

are defined in the same way as in the case of smooth loops (see Section 4.1).
The evaluation map is obviously continuous,
and the concatenation map is continuous by Lemma
7.25
.
The evaluation map
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}
will be abbreviated as
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}
when there is no risk of confusion.

7.4.
Strongly continuous maps to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,

The goal of this subsection is to define
strongly continuous maps
from moduli spaces of (perturbed) pseudo-holomorphic disks (with boundary marked points)
to spaces of continuous loops (with marked points),
so that natural compatibility conditions (spelled out in Proposition
7.26
) are satisfied.
Throughout this subsection, the space
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
is equipped with the topology defined from the metric
\displaystyle d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}
,
which is defined in Section 7.3.

To state Proposition
7.26
we have to introduce the following notations.
For any
\displaystyle\beta\in H_{2}({\mathbb{C}}^{n},L)
and
\displaystyle(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1:\beta)
,
one can define a continuous map

(42)

\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}:\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{v}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial B(v))\to\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}(P\times L)^{2}

in a way similar to (
23
).
Also let us recall the diagonal map (
25
).
Then one can define a continuous map

(43)

\biggl(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}}\biggl(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{v}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial B(v))\biggr)\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)

by taking concatenations of loops,
where the fiber product in the LHS is taken over
\displaystyle\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T)}(P\times L)^{2}
.

Proposition 7.26
.

For every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
