, and
\displaystyle P\in\{\{m\},[m,m+1]\}
,
one can define strongly continuous maps

(44)

\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}:{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)\qquad(\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k)),

(45)

\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}}:{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)\qquad(\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k)),

(46)

\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}}:{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)\qquad(\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-k-U)),

so that the following diagrams commute
for every
\displaystyle(T,B)\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)
:

•

\displaystyle\textstyle{\biggl(\prod_{e}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}\biggl(\prod_{v}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):P)\biggr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\biggl(\prod_{e}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}\biggl(\prod_{v}P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{v}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial B(v))\biggr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{(\ref{170615_1})}
\displaystyle\textstyle{P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)}

where the first horizontal map is defined from (
34
) by setting
\displaystyle d=0
, and
vertical maps are defined by (
44
).

•

\displaystyle\textstyle{\biggl(\prod_{e}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}(\star)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\biggl(\prod_{e}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}\biggl(\prod_{v}P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{v}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial B(v))\biggr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{(\ref{170615_1})}
\displaystyle\textstyle{P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)}

where
\displaystyle(\star):=\prod_{v\neq v_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):P)\times{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}(B(v_{0}):P)
and
the first horizontal map is defined from (
35
) by setting
\displaystyle d=0
, and
vertical maps are defined by (
45
).

•

\displaystyle\textstyle{\biggl(\prod_{e}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}(\star)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\biggl(\prod_{e}P\times L\biggr)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}}\,}\biggl(\prod_{v}P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{v}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial B(v))\biggr)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\scriptstyle{(\ref{170615_1})}
\displaystyle\textstyle{P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)}

where
\displaystyle(\star)=\prod_{v\neq v_{0}}{\mathcal{M}}_{k_{v}+1}(B(v):P)\times{\mathcal{N}}^{\geq 0}_{k_{v_{0}}+1}(B(v_{0}):P)
and
the first horizontal map is defined from (
36
) by setting
\displaystyle d=0
, and
vertical maps are defined by (
46
).

In the rest of this subsection,
we explain the definition of
the strongly continous map (
44
).
Definitions of
(
45
) and (
46
) are similar.
For each
\displaystyle p\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
,
let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{p}=(U_{p},{\mathcal{E}}_{p},s_{p},\psi_{p})

be a K-chart at
\displaystyle p
.
To define a strongly continuous map (
44
),
it is sufficient to define a continuous map

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}:U_{p}\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,

for each
\displaystyle p
,
so that compatibility conditions with coordinate changes are satisfied.

By Lemma
7.22
, every
\displaystyle x\in U_{p}
is represented by an element of

P\times\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T^{\prime})}L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T^{\prime})}{\mathcal{MM}}_{k_{v}+1}(B^{\prime}(v))\bigg)

where
\displaystyle(T^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is a
reduction
of
\displaystyle(T,B)
.
We denote the representative by

(\pi,(u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}})_{v}).

Now we define

\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}(x):=(\pi,\text{\rm ev}\,((u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}})_{v}))\in P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta),

where
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,((u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}})_{v})
is the concatenation (defined by (
43
))
of
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,(u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}})\in{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{v}+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial B^{\prime}(v))
(which is defined below) for all
\displaystyle v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T^{\prime})
.

To define
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,(u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}})
,
we distinguish the case
\displaystyle u^{v}
is constant and the case
\displaystyle u^{v}
is nonconstant.

•

The case
\displaystyle u^{v}
is a constant map to
\displaystyle q\in L
: we define

\text{\rm ev}\,(u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}}):=(c_{q},\ldots,c_{q})

where
\displaystyle c_{q}
denotes the constant path at
\displaystyle q
with length
\displaystyle 0
.

•

The case
\displaystyle u^{v}
is nonconstant: we use the length parametrization.
For every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k_{v}\}
we define
\displaystyle\theta_{j}\in[0,2\pi)
by
\displaystyle z^{v}_{j}/z^{v}_{0}=e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta_{j}}
, thus
\displaystyle 0=\theta_{0}<\theta_{1}<\cdots<\theta_{k_{v}}<2\pi
.
Then we define

\displaystyle\displaystyle T^{u^{v}}_{j}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\theta_{j+1}}\bigg\lvert\frac{d}{d\theta}u^{v}(z^{v}_{0}\cdot e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta})\bigg\rvert\,d\theta,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lambda^{u^{v}}_{j}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:[\theta_{j},\theta_{j+1}]\to[0,T^{u^{v}}_{j}];\quad\Theta\mapsto\int_{\theta_{j}}^{\Theta}\bigg\lvert\frac{d}{d\theta}u^{v}(z^{v}_{0}\cdot e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta})\bigg\rvert\,d\theta,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\gamma^{u^{v}}_{j}

\displaystyle\displaystyle:[0,T^{u^{v}}_{j}]\to L;\quad t\mapsto u^{v}(e^{\sqrt{-1}(\lambda^{u^{v}}_{j})^{-1}(t)}).

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\theta}u^{v}(e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta})
vanishes at only finitely many
\displaystyle\theta

since
\displaystyle u^{v}
is nonconstant,
thus
\displaystyle T^{u^{v}}_{j}
is positive and
\displaystyle\lambda^{u^{v}}_{j}
is strictly increasing.
Hence
\displaystyle(\lambda^{u^{v}}_{j})^{-1}:[0,T^{u^{v}}_{j}]\to[\theta_{j},\theta_{j+1}]
is a well-defined continuous map.
Then we define

\text{\rm ev}\,(u^{v},z^{v}_{0},\ldots,z^{v}_{k_{v}}):=(T^{u^{v}}_{j},\gamma^{u^{v}}_{j})_{0\leq j\leq k_{v}}.

This completes the definition of
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}(x)
.

Remark 7.27
.

For later purpose (proof of Lemma
7.28
) we define

\displaystyle\Lambda^{u^{v}}_{j}:[0,1]\to[0,T^{u^{v}}_{j}]
by

\displaystyle\Lambda^{u^{v}}_{j}(s):=\lambda^{u^{v}}_{j}((1-s)\theta_{j}+s\theta_{j+1})
.

The above definition of
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}(x)
does not depend on choices of representatives of
\displaystyle x
.
In particular, the family of maps
\displaystyle(\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p})_{p}
is compatible with coordinate changes of K-charts.
To show that this family defines a strongly continuous map,
we have to check that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}
is continuous for each
\displaystyle p
.

Lemma 7.28
.

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{M}}}_{p}:U_{p}\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,(\partial\beta)
is continuous for every
\displaystyle p\in{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)
.

Proof
.

Step 1.

Let
\displaystyle(x_{l})_{l\geq 1}
be a sequence in
\displaystyle U_{p}
which converges to
\displaystyle x_{\infty}\in U_{p}
.
For simplicity, we work under the following assumptions:

•

\displaystyle k=0
.

•

Each
\displaystyle x_{l}
is represented by a single holomorphic map; we denote the representative by
\displaystyle(\pi_{l},u_{l},z_{l,0})
.

•

\displaystyle x_{\infty}
is represented by an element of

P\times\bigg(\prod_{e\in C_{1,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})}L\bigg)\,_{\Delta}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{\text{\rm int}\,}}\bigg(\prod_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})}{\mathcal{MM}}_{k_{v}+1}(B_{\infty}(v))\bigg)

for some
\displaystyle(T_{\infty},B_{\infty})\in{\mathcal{G}}(k+1,\beta)
; see Lemma
7.22
.
We denote the representative by

\displaystyle(\pi_{\infty},(u^{v}_{\infty},z^{v}_{\infty,0},\ldots,z^{v}_{\infty,k_{v}})_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})})
.

Since
\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}\pi_{l}=\pi_{\infty}
is obvious, it is sufficient to show that

(47)

\lim_{l\to\infty}d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}(\text{\rm ev}\,(u_{l},z_{l,0}),\text{\rm ev}\,((u^{v}_{\infty},z^{v}_{\infty,0},\ldots,z^{v}_{\infty,k_{v}})_{v}))=0.

Step 2.

We first consider the case
\displaystyle\sharp C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})=1
,
and abbreviate
\displaystyle u^{v}_{\infty}
as
\displaystyle u_{\infty}
.
We may assume that

\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}u_{l}=u_{\infty}
in the
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-topology, and

\displaystyle z_{l,0}=1
for every
\displaystyle l
(including
\displaystyle l=\infty
).
When
\displaystyle u_{\infty}
is constant, then
\displaystyle u_{l}|_{\partial D}
converges to a constant map in
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
(in particular
\displaystyle C^{0}
) topology,
then (
47
) follows.
When
\displaystyle u_{\infty}
is nonconstant,
then
\displaystyle u_{l}
is nonconstant for sufficiently large
\displaystyle l
.
Moreover

\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}T^{u_{l}}_{0}=T^{u_{\infty}}_{0}

and

\displaystyle\lim_{l\to\infty}\Lambda^{u_{l}}_{0}=\Lambda^{u_{\infty}}_{0}
in the
\displaystyle C^{0}
-topology.
Then (
47
) follows from the next lemma,
the proof of which is completely elementary and omitted.

Lemma 7.29
.

Let
\displaystyle(\tau_{l})_{l}
be a sequence in
\displaystyle C^{0}([0,1],[0,1])
such that

•

\displaystyle(\tau_{l})_{l}
has a
\displaystyle C^{0}
-limit
\displaystyle\tau_{\infty}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
.

•

For every
\displaystyle l
(including
\displaystyle\infty
),
\displaystyle\tau_{l}
is strictly increasing and
\displaystyle\tau_{l}(0)=0
,
\displaystyle\tau_{l}(1)=1
.

Then
\displaystyle(\tau_{l})^{-1}
converges to
\displaystyle(\tau_{\infty})^{-1}
in the
\displaystyle C^{0}
-topology.

Step 3.

Now we consider the case
\displaystyle\sharp C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})>1
.
We fix
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, which can be arbitrarily small.
For sufficiently large every
\displaystyle l
,
there exists a decomposition (depends on
\displaystyle\varepsilon
but we drop it from the following notations)

D=V_{l}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})}U^{v}_{l}

such that the following conditions are satisfied (see
[
8
]
Section 7.1.4):

•

\displaystyle V_{l}
is a compact set
with the number of connected components is
\displaystyle\sharp C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})
,
and
\displaystyle U^{v}_{l}
is a connected open set for every
\displaystyle v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})
.

•

\displaystyle V_{l}\cap\partial D
is a disjoint union of
\displaystyle 2\sharp C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})-1
closed intervals.

•

\displaystyle\int_{V_{l}\cap\partial D}\bigg\lvert\frac{d}{d\theta}u_{l}(e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta})\bigg\rvert<\varepsilon
.

•

For every
\displaystyle v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})
,

\displaystyle\overline{U^{v}_{l}}\cap\partial D
is a disjoint union of
\displaystyle k_{v}+1
closed intervals.
We denote them as
\displaystyle I^{v}_{l}(0),\ldots,I^{v}_{l}(k_{v})
aligned in anti-clockwise order.

•

There exists a sequence
\displaystyle(\rho^{v}_{l})_{l}
in
\displaystyle\text{\rm Aut}\,(D)
such that
\displaystyle(\rho^{v}_{l})^{-1}(U^{v}_{l})
is independent on
\displaystyle l
(denoted by
\displaystyle U^{v}
),
and
\displaystyle u_{l}\circ\rho^{v}_{l}
converges to
\displaystyle u^{v}_{\infty}
(with respect to the
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-topology) on
\displaystyle\overline{U^{v}}
.

•

\displaystyle\overline{U^{v}}\cap\partial D
is a disjoint union of
\displaystyle k_{v}+1
closed intervals,
which we denote by
\displaystyle I^{v}_{\infty}(0),\ldots,I^{v}_{\infty}(k_{v})
in anti-clockwise order.

For each
\displaystyle v\in C_{0,\text{\rm int}\,}(T_{\infty})
and
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k_{v}\}
,
our assumption
\displaystyle u_{l}\circ\rho^{v}_{l}\to u^{v}_{\infty}

implies that

\lim_{l\to\infty}d_{\Pi}(u_{l}|_{I^{v}_{l}(j)},u^{v}_{\infty}|_{I^{v}_{\infty}(j)})=0.

The proof is same as our argument in Step 2.
On the other hand, for every
\displaystyle l
, the total lengths of
\displaystyle u_{l}|_{V_{l}\cap\partial D}
is at most
\displaystyle\varepsilon
.
Since
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,(u_{l},z_{l,0})
is obtained as the concatenation of these paths,
it is not difficult to see that

\limsup_{l\to\infty}\,d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1}}(\text{\rm ev}\,(u_{l},z_{l,0}),\text{\rm ev}\,((u^{v}_{\infty},z^{v}_{\infty,0},\ldots,z^{v}_{\infty,k_{v}})_{v}))\leq 10\varepsilon.

Since
\displaystyle\varepsilon
can be arbitrarily small, this implies
the convergence (
47
).
∎

7.5.
\displaystyle C^{0}
-approximation lemma and CF-perturbation

We first introduce the notion of “
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-closeness”
for strongly continuous maps from a K-space to a metric space:

Definition 7.30
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a K-space,

\displaystyle(Y,d)
be a metric space,
and
\displaystyle\widehat{f},\widehat{g}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to Y
be strongly continuous maps.
For any
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, we say that

\displaystyle\widehat{f}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{g}
are
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close, if

\displaystyle d(f_{p}(x),g_{p}(x))<\varepsilon
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle x\in U_{p}
.

Let us state a key technical result in this subsection, which we call
\displaystyle C^{0}
-approximation lemma.
The notion of “open substrcture” of a given K-structure is defined in Definition 3.20 in
[
10
]
.
The constant
\displaystyle\rho_{L}
in the statement was introduced in the second paragraph of Section 7.3.

Theorem 7.31
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a compact K-space and

\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,
be a strongly continuous map such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strongly smooth for every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.
Let
\displaystyle Z
be a closed subset of
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle\widehat{g}:(Z,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{Z})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

be a strongly smooth map such that:

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{g}=\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f}|_{Z}
for every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.

•

\displaystyle\widehat{g}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widehat{f}|_{Z}
with respect to
\displaystyle d_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}
.

If
\displaystyle\varepsilon<\rho_{L}
,
there exists an open substructure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}

and a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

such that the following conditions hold:

•

\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{g^{\prime}}=\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}
for every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.

•

\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}=\widehat{g}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}|_{Z}
.

The proof of Theorem
7.31
is carried out in Section 9.
Combining Theorem
7.31
with results from
[
10
]
,
we obtain Theorem
7.32
below.
In the statement of Theorem
7.32
we use the following notions from
[
10
]

without repeating their definitions:

•

Thickening of K-structures: see Section 5.2 in
[
10
]
.

•

Collared versions of K-structures, strongly smooth maps and CF-perturbations: see Section 17.5 in
[
10
]
.

The assumptions in Theorem
7.32

on K-structures and CF-perturbations are
similar to Situations 17.43 and 17.57 in
[
10
]
, respectively.

Theorem 7.32
.

Suppose that we are given the following data:

•

\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\displaystyle\tau\in(0,1)
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,\rho_{L})
.

•

A
\displaystyle\tau
-collared K-space
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
.

•

A
\displaystyle\tau
-collared strongly continuous map
\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,

such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is admissible for every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\widehat{f}
is strata-wise weakly submersive.

•

For every
\displaystyle l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
a
\displaystyle\tau
-collared K-structure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l}(X)

which is a thickening of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}
.

•

For every
\displaystyle l_{1},l_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,

\displaystyle(l_{1}+l_{2})!/(l_{1})!(l_{2})!
-fold covering of
\displaystyle\tau
-collared
\displaystyle K
-spaces

\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{2}}})\to(\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}})

such that the following diagrams commute for every
\displaystyle l_{1},l_{2},l_{3}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
:

\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{3}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{3}}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{3}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{3}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}+l_{3}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{2}+l_{3}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{(\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}+l_{3}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}+l_{3}}}),}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{2}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\displaystyle\textstyle{(\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}}).}

•

A
\displaystyle\tau
-collared CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{l}}
of
\displaystyle(\widehat{S}_{l}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}})
for every
\displaystyle l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
,
such that the pullback of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{2}}})\to(\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}})

coincides with the restriction of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{l_{2}}}

for every
\displaystyle l_{1},l_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.

•

A
\displaystyle\tau
-collared admissible map
\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}_{l}}:(\widehat{S}_{l}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

for every
\displaystyle l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
such that:

–

The pullback of
\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}}
by
\displaystyle\widehat{S}_{l_{1}}(\widehat{S}_{l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{2}}})\to(\widehat{S}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l_{1}+l_{2}}})

coincides with the restriction of
\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}_{l_{2}}}

for every
\displaystyle l_{1},l_{2}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.

–

For every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f_{l}^{+}}:(\widehat{S}_{l}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}})\to L
coincides with

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}:(\widehat{S}_{l}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)})\to L

via the KK-embedding
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}}
.

–

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\widehat{f_{l}^{+}}:(\widehat{S}_{l}(X),\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}})\to L

is strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{l}}
.

–

\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}_{l}}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}
.

Then, for any
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}\in(0,\tau)
, there exist the following data:

•

A
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}
-collared K-structure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}}
on
\displaystyle X
, which is a thickening of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
.

•

An isomorphism of
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}
-collared K-structures
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}\cong\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}}
for every
\displaystyle l\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 1}
.

•

A
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}
-collared CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}}
of
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}})
such that

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}
coincides with
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}_{l}}

via the isomorphism of K-spaces
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}}|_{\widehat{S}_{l}(X)}\cong\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}_{l}}
.

•

A
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}
-collared admissible map
\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
such that:

–

\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widehat{f}
.

–

For every
\displaystyle j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
,
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f^{+}}
coincides with
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{j}\circ\widehat{f}
with respect to the KK-embedding
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}}
.

–

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\widehat{f^{+}}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}})\to L
is strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}}
.

Proof
.

The
\displaystyle K
-structure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}^{+}}
and the CF-perturbation
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}}
are defined by
Propositions 17.62 and 17.65 in
[
10
]
, respectively;
here we apply Proposition 17.65 (2) to
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{0}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
.
Moreover
\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}_{1}}:\widehat{S}_{1}(X)\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}

extends to the
\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}
-neighborhood of
\displaystyle\partial X
(denoted by
\displaystyle N(\tau^{\prime})
).
Then we can apply Theorem
7.31
to conclude the proof,
taking
\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}(\tau^{\prime})
as
\displaystyle Z
and the extension of
\displaystyle\widehat{f^{+}_{1}}
as
\displaystyle\widehat{g}
.
∎

Remark 7.33
.

Nextly, we should state and prove a version of Theorem
7.32

such that
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
is a
\displaystyle\tau
-collared K-space, and

\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to[a,b]^{\boxplus\tau}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}^{\text{\rm con}}\,

is a
\displaystyle\tau
-collared strongly continuous map,
where
\displaystyle a<b
are real numbers and
\displaystyle[a,b]^{\boxplus\tau}:=[a-\tau,b+\tau]
.
However, the statement is similar to Theorem
7.32

and involves further notations,
thus here we choose not to write it down in detail.

7.6.
Wrap-up of the proof

Now we can complete the proof of Theorem
6.1
assuming results
presented in Sections 7.1 and 7.5.

Let
\displaystyle X
be one of moduli spaces considered in Theorem
7.20
(i).
In Section 7.4, we defined a strongly continuous map from
\displaystyle X
to the space of continuous loops (with marked points).
This map naturally extends to a
\displaystyle 1
-collared strongly continuous map from
\displaystyle X^{\boxplus 1}
,
see Lemma-Definition 17.35 and Lemma 17.37 (3) in
[
10
]
.
Taking
\displaystyle\tau(X)\in(1/2,1)
as in Remark
7.23

and successively applying Theorem
7.32
and Remark
7.33
,

\displaystyle\bar{X}:=X^{\boxplus 1/2}
is equipped with an admissible CF-perturbation and
an admissible strongly smooth map to the space of smooth loops (with marked points).
In conclusion, we obtain the following data
for every
\displaystyle k\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
,
\displaystyle m\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
and
\displaystyle P\in\{\{m\},[m,m+1]\}
.

(i):

Compact admissible K-spaces

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\qquad(\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m+1-k)),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\qquad(\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-1-k)),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)\qquad(\omega_{n}(\beta)<\varepsilon(m-k-U)),

and admissible CF-perturbations on these K-spaces.

(ii):

Admissible maps

(48)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}}:\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\beta:P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(\partial\beta)

(49)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}^{0}_{k+1}}:\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}^{0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(\partial\beta)

(50)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,^{\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}}:\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}^{\geq 0}_{k+1}(\beta:P)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\to P\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}(\partial\beta)

such that compositions with
\displaystyle\text{\rm id}_{P}\times\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}

(which are admissible maps to
\displaystyle P\times L
)
are strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to the CF-perturbations in (i).

(iii):

Isomorphisms of admissible K-spaces,
which are obtained from isomorphisms (
28
)–(
33
)
by replacing each moduli space
\displaystyle X
with
\displaystyle\bar{X}
.
For example, we obtain

(51)

\partial\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\beta:m)\cong\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\\
\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\varepsilon_{0}}\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k_{1}+1}(\beta_{1}:m)\,_{i}\!\times_{0}\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k_{2}+1}(\beta_{2}:m)

from (
28
).
We require that these isomorphisms are compatible with
CF-perturbations in (i) and evaluation maps (ii).

Applying results in Section 7.1, one can define

(52)

\displaystyle\displaystyle x_{m}(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m+1-k)}(-1)^{n+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\bar{a},\{m\}))

(53)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{x}_{m}(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m+1-k)}(-1)^{n+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\bar{a},[m,m+1])),

(54)

\displaystyle\displaystyle y_{m}(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m-U-k)}(-1)^{n+k+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}_{k+1}^{\geq 0}(\bar{a},\{m\})),

(55)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{y}_{m}(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m-U-k)}(-1)^{n+k+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}_{k+1}^{\geq 0}(\bar{a},[m,m+1])),

(56)

\displaystyle\displaystyle z_{m}(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m-1-k)}(-1)^{n+k+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}_{k+1}^{0}(\bar{a},\{m\})),

(57)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bar{z}_{m}(a,k)

\displaystyle\displaystyle:=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m-1-k)}(-1)^{n+k+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{N}}}_{k+1}^{0}(\bar{a},[m,m+1])).

Remark 7.34
.

In the above formulas we abbreviate differential form
\displaystyle 1
(see Remark
7.3
) and
CF-perturbations. We also abbreviate superscripts of
ev
for simplicity.

Remark 7.35
.

Here we explain one issue which can be overlooked by abbreviating CF-perturbations in the above formulas.
As is clear from Section 7.1, to define a de Rham chain
one has to fix a parameter of CF-perturbation
\displaystyle e\in(0,1]

(here we use a letter
\displaystyle e
, not to be confused with
\displaystyle\varepsilon
used in the above formulas).
Hence we take a strictly decreasing sequence of positive real numbers
\displaystyle(e_{m})_{m\geq 1}
satisfying

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}e_{m}=0
,
and define
\displaystyle x_{m}(a,k)
by

x_{m}(a,k):=\sum_{\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m+1-k)}(-1)^{n+1}\text{\rm ev}\,_{*}(\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\bar{a},\{m\}),\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{e_{m}}).

\displaystyle y_{m}(a,k)
and
\displaystyle z_{m}(a,k)
are defined by similar formulas, using
\displaystyle e_{m}
.
Then

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{m}(a,k)
is defined by interpolating
\displaystyle e_{m}
and
\displaystyle e_{m+1}
on the moduli space
\displaystyle\bar{{\mathcal{M}}}_{k+1}(\bar{a},[m,m+1])
.

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{m}(a,k)
and
\displaystyle\bar{z}_{m}(a,k)
are defined in a similar way.

Let us check that the requirements in Theorem
6.1
are satisfied.

\displaystyle x_{m}=e_{-}(\bar{x}_{m})
,
\displaystyle y_{m}=e_{-}(\bar{y}_{m})
,
\displaystyle z_{m}=e_{-}(\bar{z}_{m})
follow from the above definition
and (
21
).
Moreover

(x_{m+1}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{m}))(a,k)\neq 0\implies\omega_{n}(\bar{a})\geq\varepsilon(m+1-k)

show
\displaystyle x_{m+1}-e_{+}(\bar{x}_{m})\in F^{m}
.
By similar arguments one can show

\displaystyle y_{m+1}-e_{+}(\bar{y}_{m})\in F^{m-U-1}
and

\displaystyle z_{m+1}-e_{+}(\bar{z}_{m})\in F^{m-2}
.

The isomorphism (
51
) shows

\partial\bar{x}_{m}(a,k)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k+1\\
a_{1}+a_{2}=a\\
1\leq i\leq k_{1}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{(k_{1}-m)(k_{2}-1)+(k_{1}-1)}\bar{x}_{m}(a_{1},k_{1})\circ_{i}\bar{x}_{m}(a_{2},k_{2})

for every
\displaystyle(a,k)
such that
\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\bar{a})<\varepsilon(m+1-k)
,
thus
\displaystyle\partial\bar{x}_{m}-\frac{1}{2}[\bar{x}_{m},\bar{x}_{m}]\in F^{m}
.
By similar arguments one can show

\displaystyle\partial\bar{y}_{m}-[\bar{x}_{m},\bar{y}_{m}]-\bar{z}_{m}\in F^{m-U-1}
and

\displaystyle\partial\bar{z}_{m}-[\bar{x}_{m},\bar{z}_{m}]\in F^{m-2}
.

Finally
\displaystyle x_{m}(\bar{a},k)\neq 0\implies{\mathcal{M}}_{k+1}(\bar{a})\neq\emptyset
,
thus
\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
,
\displaystyle k\geq 2
.
Moreover

\displaystyle[x_{m}(0,2)]=(-1)^{n+1}[{\mathcal{M}}_{3}(0)]=(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle z_{m}(\bar{a},k)\neq 0\implies{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{k+1}(\bar{a})\neq\emptyset
,
thus
\displaystyle\omega_{n}(\bar{a})\geq 2\varepsilon
or
\displaystyle a=0
.
Moreover

\displaystyle[z_{m}(0,0)]=(-1)^{n+1}[{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{1}(0)]=(-1)^{n+1}[L]
.
∎

8.
Strongly smooth map from a K-space with a CF-perturbation gives a de Rham chain

The goal of this section is to explain proofs of results presented in Section 7.1.
Namely, given a strongly smooth map from a K-space (equipped with a differential form and a CF-perturbation) to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
(the space of loops with
\displaystyle k+1
marked points),
we define a de Rham chain on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
and prove Stokes’ formula and fiber product formula.
Here we imitate arguments in
[
10
]
Sections 7 and 9 in our setting.
In Section 8.1 we consider smooth maps from single K-charts.
In Section 8.2 we consider strongly smooth maps from spaces with good coordinate system (GCS), and in Section 8.3 strongly smooth maps from K-spaces.
In Sections 8.2 and 8.3, we only consider spaces without boundaries (and corners),
since generalizations to spaces with boundaries are straightforward.

Throughout this section,
\displaystyle X
denotes a separable, metrizable topolgoical space.

8.1.
Single K-chart

In this subsection,
given a smooth map from a K-chart (equipped with a CF-perturbation and a differential form)
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
,
we define a de Rham chain on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.
We also prove Stokes’s formula and fiber product formula.
We first consider K-charts without boundary, and then proceed to the case of K-charts with boundaries.

8.1.1.
K-chart without boundary

Suppose we are given the following data:

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}=(U,{\mathcal{E}},s,\psi)
is a K-chart of
\displaystyle X
.

•

\displaystyle f:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is a smooth map in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

•

\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(U)
.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}=({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
is a CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega
such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f:U\to L
is strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}

(see Definition 7.24 and Definition-Lemma 7.25 in
[
10
]
).

We are going to define
\displaystyle f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})
for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.
Let
\displaystyle({\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
be a representative of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}

(see Definitions 7.15, 7.16 and 7.19 in
[
10
]
), such that

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}=(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}},E_{{\mathfrak{r}}},\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}},\widehat{\phi}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
is a manifold chart
(we do not consider group actions: see Remark
10.1
) of
\displaystyle(U,{\mathcal{E}})
such that

\displaystyle(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}))_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
covers
\displaystyle U
.
Let
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\to E_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
denote the pull back of
\displaystyle s
by
\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
.

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}=(W_{{\mathfrak{r}}},\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}},\{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\}_{\varepsilon})
is a CF-perturbation of

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}
on
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
. Namely

–

\displaystyle W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
is an open neighborhood of
\displaystyle 0
of a finite-dimensional oriented vector space
\displaystyle\widehat{W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}
.

–

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\times W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\to E_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
map transversal to
\displaystyle 0
for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

–

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(y,\xi)=s_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(y)
in compact
\displaystyle C^{1}
topology on
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\times W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
.

–

\displaystyle\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\text{\rm dim}\,W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}_{c}(W_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
such that
\displaystyle\int_{W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}}=1
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V_{\mathfrak{r}}}:({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)\to L
is a submersion for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
and
\displaystyle E_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
are oriented so that
\displaystyle\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
preserve orientations.

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)
is oriented so that the isomorphism

E_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\oplus T({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)\cong TV_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\oplus TW_{{\mathfrak{r}}}

preserves orientations, following Convention 8.2.1 in
[
8
]
.

We take a partition of unity
\displaystyle\{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
subordinate to
\displaystyle(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}))_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
,
i.e.,

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U,[0,1])
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\chi_{\mathfrak{r}}\subset\phi_{\tau}(V_{\mathfrak{r}})

for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
, and

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\equiv 1
on
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega
.
Then, for each
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we define

(58)

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}):=\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}),

where the RHS is defined as

(59)

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}):=(-1)^{\dagger}(({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0),f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}},(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega)\wedge(\text{\rm pr}_{W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}(\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}})),

(60)

\dagger:=\text{\rm dim}\,W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\cdot(\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}+|\omega|).

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)
is an oriented
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold,
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}

is a submersion, thus
the RHS in (
59
) makes sense.

Remark 8.1
.

Strictly speaking, one has to fix an embedding of
\displaystyle({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)
to Euclidean space to define a de Rham chain.
However it is easy to check that the de Rham chain does not depend on choice of embedding.

A priori, the above definition may depend on choices of representative and partition of unity.
In Lemma
8.2
below we prove well-definedness and Stokes’ formula.

Lemma 8.2
.

(i):

For any
\displaystyle\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(U)
,
\displaystyle a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},a_{1}\omega_{1}+a_{2}\omega_{2},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})=a_{1}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega_{1},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})+a_{2}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega_{2},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}).

(ii):

The above definition does not depend on choices of representative of the CF-perturbation
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
and partition of unity.

(iii):

\displaystyle\partial f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},d\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})
for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

Proof
.

(i) is obvious as far as we fix a representative of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
and a partition of unity.
Thus to prove (ii), it is sufficient to prove the following claim:

Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{i}=(W_{i},\eta_{i},\{{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{i}\}_{\varepsilon})\,(i=1,2)
be two equivalent CF-perturbations
of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}
on a manifold chart
\displaystyle(V,E,\phi,\widehat{\phi})
and

\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(U)
such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega\subset\phi(V)
.
Then

\displaystyle\displaystyle(({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})^{-1}(0),f\circ\phi\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V},(\phi\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V})^{*}\omega\wedge(\text{\rm pr}_{W_{1}})^{*}\eta_{1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dagger}(({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})^{-1}(0),f\circ\phi\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V},(\phi\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V})^{*}\omega\wedge(\text{\rm pr}_{W_{2}})^{*}\eta_{2}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle\dagger:=(\text{\rm dim}\,W_{1}-\text{\rm dim}\,W_{2})(\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}+|\omega|)

By definition of equivalence (see Definition 7.5 in
[
10
]
),
we may assume that there exists a linear projection

\displaystyle\Pi:\widehat{W}_{2}\to\widehat{W}_{1}
such that
\displaystyle(\Pi)_{!}(\eta_{2})=\eta_{1}
and

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{2}=(\text{\rm id}_{V}\times\Pi)^{*}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})
.
Then, pushout of

\displaystyle\text{\rm id}_{V}\times\Pi:({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})^{-1}(0)\to({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})^{-1}(0)

sends

\displaystyle(\phi\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V})^{*}\omega\wedge(\text{\rm pr}_{W_{2}})^{*}\eta_{2}

to
\displaystyle(-1)^{\dagger}(\phi\circ\text{\rm pr}_{V})^{*}\omega\wedge(\text{\rm pr}_{W_{1}})^{*}\eta_{1}
,
which completes the proof.

To prove (iii), by (i) and (ii) we may assume that
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega
is sufficiently small and
\displaystyle\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\equiv 1
on
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega
for some
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
.
In this case (iii) is obvious except for signs, which can be checked by simple computations.
∎

Now we can state the fiber product formula.
Suppose, for each
\displaystyle i\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\displaystyle X_{i}
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}=(U_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},s_{i},\psi_{i})
,
\displaystyle f_{i}:U_{i}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1}
,

\displaystyle\omega_{i}
,
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{i}
as before.
Then, for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
one can define

(f_{i})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{i},\omega_{i},{\mathfrak{S}}_{i}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k_{i}+1})

for each
\displaystyle i\in\{1,2\}
.
On the other hand, for each
\displaystyle j\in\{1,\ldots,k_{1}\}
,
one can take a fiber product of K-charts

{\mathcal{U}}_{12}={\mathcal{U}}_{1}\,{}_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f_{2}}{\mathcal{U}}_{2}.

One can also define a fiber product of CF-perturbations
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{1}\times{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{12}
.
Finally we define a differential form
\displaystyle\omega_{12}
on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{12}
by

\omega_{12}:=(-1)^{(\text{\rm dim}\,U_{1}-\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}_{1}-|\omega_{1}|-n)|\omega_{2}|}\cdot\omega_{1}\times\omega_{2},

and a smooth map

f_{12}:U_{1}\,{}_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{1}}\!\times_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f_{2}}U_{2}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k_{1}+k_{2}};\quad(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto\text{\rm con}\,_{j}(f_{1}(x_{1}),f_{2}(x_{2})).

Then one can state the fiber product formula as follows.
The proof is obvious except for signs,
which can be checked by direct computations.

Lemma 8.3
.

In the situation described above, there holds

(f_{12})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{12},\omega_{12},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{12})=(f_{1})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{1},\omega_{1},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})\circ_{j}(f_{2})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{2},\omega_{2},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})

for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

8.1.2.
K-chart with boundary

Suppose we are given the following data:

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}=(U,{\mathcal{E}},s,\psi)
is an admissible K-chart on
\displaystyle X
,
i.e.
\displaystyle U
is an admissible manifold with boundaries (and corners),
the vector bundle
\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}
and the section
\displaystyle s
are also admissible.

•

\displaystyle f:U\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is an admissible map in the sense of Definition
7.7
(i).

•

\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(U)
is admissible.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}=({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
is an admissible CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega

such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f:U\to L
is strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Under these assumptions,
our goal is to define
\displaystyle f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.

Let
\displaystyle({\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
be a representative of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
,
and
\displaystyle(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}})_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
be a partition of unity subordinate to

\displaystyle(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}))_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\equiv 1

on
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega
.
Then we define

(61)

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}):=\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})

where each term in the RHS is defined below.
The proof of well-definedness (the RHS does not depend on choices of representatives of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
and partition of unity)
is straightforward and omitted.

Let
\displaystyle D:=\text{\rm dim}\,U
.
We may assume that
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
is an open neighborhood of
\displaystyle(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})\in({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{D}
.
We define

{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{D}\to({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{D};\,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})\mapsto(t^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,t^{\prime}_{D})

by
\displaystyle t^{\prime}_{i}:=\begin{cases}t_{i}&(t_{i}\geq 0)\\
0&(t_{i}<0)\\
\end{cases}
for every
\displaystyle 1\leq i\leq D
.
We take
\displaystyle\kappa\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},[0,1])

such that

\displaystyle\kappa\equiv 1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
,
and
\displaystyle\kappa\equiv 0
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}
.
Now let us define the following data:

•

\displaystyle\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:={\mathcal{R}}^{-1}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
,
\displaystyle\bar{E}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:={\mathcal{R}}^{*}E_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
.

•

\displaystyle\bar{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:=({\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}\times\text{\rm id}_{W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
.

•

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:=f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}
.

•

\displaystyle\overline{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}):=\kappa(t_{1})\cdots\kappa(t_{D})\cdot(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega)
.

Finally, we define

(62)

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}):=(-1)^{\dagger}((\bar{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0),\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}},\text{\rm pr}^{*}_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}(\overline{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega})\wedge\text{\rm pr}^{*}_{W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}(\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}})),

where the sign
\displaystyle\dagger
is defined by (
60
).
Note that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}:(\bar{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)\to L

is a submersion, thus the map

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}:(\bar{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0)\to L

is smooth.

Remark 8.4
.

The RHS of (
62
) may depend on the choice of the cutoff function
\displaystyle\kappa
.
Here we fix such
\displaystyle\kappa
and drop it from our notation in the following arguments.

The fiber product formula holds in the obvious manner and omitted.
Stokes’ formula is formulated as follows.

Proposition 8.5
.

\partial(f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}))=(-1)^{|\omega|}(f|_{\partial{\mathcal{U}}})_{*}(\partial{\mathcal{U}},\omega|_{\partial{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}|_{\partial{\mathcal{U}}})+(-1)^{|\omega|+1}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},d\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}).

Proof
.

This follows from

\displaystyle\displaystyle d(\overline{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega})(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\kappa(t_{1})\cdots\kappa(t_{D})(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}(d(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega))

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\sum_{i=1}^{D}\kappa(t_{1})\cdots d\kappa(t_{i})\cdots\kappa(t_{D})(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega)

and
\displaystyle\partial U
is oriented so that
\displaystyle TU\cong{\mathbb{R}}_{\text{out}}\oplus T(\partial U)
preserves orientations.
∎

8.1.3.
K-chart with boundary over an interval

Suppose we are given the following data:

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}=(U,{\mathcal{E}},s,\psi)
is an admissible K-chart on
\displaystyle X
.

•

\displaystyle f:U\to[a,b]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is an admissible map in the sense of Definition
7.13
(i).

•

\displaystyle\omega\in{\mathcal{A}}^{*}_{c}(U)
is admissible.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}=({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}
is an admissible CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega

such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ f:U\to[a,b]\times L
is strata-wise strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Under these assumptions,
our goal is to define

\displaystyle f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})\in\bar{C}^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})
for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
.
For simplicity of notations, in the following we assume that
\displaystyle a=-1
,
\displaystyle b=1
.

Let
\displaystyle({\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
be a representative of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}
,
and
\displaystyle(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}})_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
be a partition of unity subordinate to

\displaystyle(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}))_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}
such that

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\equiv 1

on
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\omega
.
Then we define

(63)

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}):=\sum_{{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})

where each term in the RHS is defined below.
The proof of well-definedness is omitted.

To define

\displaystyle f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
,
it is sufficient to consider the following three cases:

(i):

\displaystyle f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
is contained in
\displaystyle(-1,1)\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.

(ii):

\displaystyle f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
is contained in
\displaystyle[-1,1)\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
and intersects
\displaystyle\{-1\}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.

(iii):

\displaystyle f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
is contained in
\displaystyle(-1,1]\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
and intersects
\displaystyle\{1\}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.

The case (i) is similar to the case in the previous subsubsection and omitted.
In the following we only consider the case (ii), since the case (iii) is completely parallel.

Let
\displaystyle D:=\text{\rm dim}\,U
.
We may assume that
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
is an open neighborhood of

\displaystyle(0,t_{2},\ldots,t_{D})
in
\displaystyle({\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0})^{D}
and

\displaystyle f_{\mathbb{R}}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D})=t_{1}-1
.
Here
\displaystyle f_{\mathbb{R}}
denotes
\displaystyle\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\circ f
. Similarly, we set
\displaystyle f_{{\mathcal{L}}}:=\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}\circ f
.

We define
\displaystyle\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
,
\displaystyle\bar{E}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
and
\displaystyle\bar{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
in the same way as in the previous subsubsection.
We also define

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
and
\displaystyle\overline{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega}
as follows:

•

\displaystyle\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is defined by

\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}}\circ\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}:=f_{{\mathcal{L}}}\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}},\qquad\text{\rm pr}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\circ\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}):=t_{1}-1.

•

\displaystyle\overline{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D}):=\kappa(t_{2})\cdots\kappa(t_{D})\cdot(\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}|_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}})^{*}(\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega)
.

Here
\displaystyle\kappa\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},[0,1])
is taken in the previous subsubsection.
Then we define

f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}):=(-1)^{\dagger}((\bar{{\mathfrak{s}}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})^{-1}(0),\bar{f}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\circ\text{\rm pr}_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}},\tau_{+},\tau_{-},\text{\rm pr}^{*}_{\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}(\overline{\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega})\wedge\text{\rm pr}^{*}_{W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}}(\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}})),

where the sign
\displaystyle\dagger
is defined as before and

\displaystyle\tau_{-}
,
\displaystyle\tau_{+}
are defined as follows.

\displaystyle\tau_{+}
is defined in the obvious way, since
\displaystyle f\circ\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(V_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
does not intersect
\displaystyle\{1\}\times{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.
On the other hand,
\displaystyle\tau_{-}
is defined as a restriction of

(\bar{V}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\cap\{t_{1}\leq 0\})\times W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\leq-1}\times((V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\cap\{t_{1}=0\})\times W_{{\mathfrak{r}}});\,(t_{1},\ldots,t_{D},w)\mapsto(t_{1}-1,t_{2},\ldots,t_{D},w).

This completes the definition of

\displaystyle f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}\omega,{\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})
,
thus the definition of

\displaystyle f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})
.

The fiber product formula is stated and proved in the obvious way.
Stokes’ formula

\partial f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})=(-1)^{|\omega|}(f|_{\partial_{h}{\mathcal{U}}})*(\partial_{h}{\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon})+(-1)^{|\omega|+1}f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},d\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}),

where
\displaystyle\partial_{h}{\mathcal{U}}
is the restriction of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}
to
\displaystyle\partial_{h}U
,
and

\displaystyle\displaystyle e_{+}(f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}))=(f_{{\mathcal{L}}}|_{U_{1}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}|_{U_{1}},\omega|_{U_{1}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}|_{U_{1}}),

\displaystyle\displaystyle e_{-}(f_{*}({\mathcal{U}},\omega,{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}))=(f_{{\mathcal{L}}}|_{U_{-1}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}|_{U_{-1}},\omega|_{U_{-1}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}|_{U_{-1}})

can be checked directly and proofs are omitted.

8.2.
Space with GCS

In this subsection, we assume that
\displaystyle X
is compact.
Let
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}=(\{{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}},\{\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\geq{\mathfrak{q}}})

be a GCS (good coordinate system) without boundary on
\displaystyle X
(see Section 10).
We start from the following definition.

Definition 8.6
.

A strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}
from
\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}})
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is a family

\displaystyle(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}

which satisfies the following conditions:

•

For every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
,
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a smooth map from
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
.

•

For every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}
, there holds
\displaystyle f_{\mathfrak{p}}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}=f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}|_{U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}}
.

Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}
be a support system of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}

and
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}
be a CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle(\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{K}})

(see Definitions 5.6 and 7.47 in
[
10
]
).
Here we recall the definition of support system
(in the case
\displaystyle Z=X
):

Definition 8.7
.

A support system of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
is
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}=({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}

where
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a compact set of
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}

which is a closure of an open subset
\displaystyle{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
,
and
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\psi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap s^{-1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(0))=X
.

We assume that
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}
is transversal to
\displaystyle 0
,
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widetriangle{f}:(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}
.
Also, let
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\omega}=(\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}
be a differential form on
\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}})
.
Given these data, we are going to define

(64)

\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
.
Note that the support system
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}
is a part of the data to define (
64
),
though it is implicit in the above formula.

Remark 8.8
.

In contrast to the case of single K-charts, where de Rham chain is defined for all
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
the de Rham chain (
64
) is defined only when
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small.

In Section 8.2.1 we state and prove some technical lemmas.
In Section 8.2.2 we define (
64
) and check its well-definedness, invariance under GG-embedding, and Stokes’ formula.
We only consider spaces with GCS
without
boundaries, since generalization to spaces with boundaries (and corners) is straightforward.
The arguments of this subsection partly follow arguments in Sections 7.5–7.7 in
[
10
]
with some modifications.

8.2.1.
Technical lemmas

Recall that
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}|:=\bigg(\bigsqcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\bigg)/\sim
equipped with the
topology from
\displaystyle|\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}|
is metrizable; see Definition 5.6 (3) in
[
10
]
.
We fix a metric
\displaystyle d
on
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}|
which is compatible with this topology.

For any
\displaystyle S\subset|{\mathcal{K}}|
and
\displaystyle\delta>0
we set

B_{\delta}(S):=\{x\in|{\mathcal{K}}|\mid d(S,x)<\delta\},\qquad\bar{B}_{\delta}(S):=\{x\in|{\mathcal{K}}|\mid d(S,x)\leq\delta\}.

Recall the notion of “support set” of CF-perturbations from Definition 7.72 in
[
10
]
:

For each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
, let
\displaystyle\{({\mathfrak{V}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}},{\mathcal{S}}_{{\mathfrak{r}}})\mid{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}\}
be a representative of the CF-perturbation
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.
Then for each
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we define
\displaystyle\Pi(({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})^{-1}(0)))
to be the set consisting of all
\displaystyle x\in U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
such that
there exists
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{r}}\in{\mathfrak{R}}
,
\displaystyle y\in V_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
,
\displaystyle\xi\in W_{{\mathfrak{r}}}
such that

\phi_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(y)=x,\qquad s^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{r}}}(y,\xi)=0,\qquad\xi\in\text{\rm supp}\,\eta_{{\mathfrak{r}}}.

This definition is independent of the choice of representative.
Then we define

\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0)):=\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap\Pi(({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})^{-1}(0)))\subset|{\mathcal{K}}|

and call it the
support set
of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 8.9
.

For any neighborhood
\displaystyle U
of
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap s_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}(0))
in
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}|
,
there exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle 0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}\implies\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0))\subset U
.

Proof
.

If this is not the case, there exists a sequence
\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{m})_{m\geq 1}
of positive real numbers converging to
\displaystyle 0
,
and a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m\geq 1}
such that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon_{m}})^{-1}(0))\setminus U
for every
\displaystyle m\geq 1
.
Up to subsequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m}
has a limit in
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}|
which we denote by
\displaystyle x
.

There exists
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for infinitely many
\displaystyle m
, thus we may assume that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for all
\displaystyle m
,
then
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
. It is sufficient to show that
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)=0
,
which implies
\displaystyle x_{m}\in U
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
, a contradiction.

Take a manifold chart
\displaystyle(V,E,\phi,\widehat{\phi})
of
\displaystyle(U_{{\mathfrak{q}}},{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
such that
\displaystyle x\in\phi(V)

and
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
is locally represented by
\displaystyle(W,\eta,({\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon})

where
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}:V\times W\to E
and
\displaystyle\eta
is a compactly supported form on
\displaystyle W
.
Let
\displaystyle s:V\to E
denote the pullback of
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
by
\displaystyle\phi
.
Then
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon}(y,\xi)\to s(y)\,(y\in V,\,\xi\in W)

as
\displaystyle\varepsilon\to 0
in the compact
\displaystyle C^{1}
topology on
\displaystyle V\times W
.

Take
\displaystyle y\in V
so that
\displaystyle\phi(y)=x
and

\displaystyle y_{m}\in V
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
so that
\displaystyle\phi(y_{m})=x_{m}
.
Then
\displaystyle y_{m}\to y
and
there exists
\displaystyle\xi_{m}\in\text{\rm supp}\,\eta
such that
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{\varepsilon_{m}}(y_{m},\xi_{m})=0
.
Since
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\eta
is compact, this shows that
\displaystyle s(y)=0
,
which implies
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)=0
.
∎

Lemma 8.10
.

For any support system
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K^{\prime}}}<{\mathcal{K}}

(see Definition 5.6 (2) in
[
10
]
),
there exist
\displaystyle\delta>0
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}\implies B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0))\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\,(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}).

Proof
.

We take a support system
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}<{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}<{\mathcal{K}}
.

Step 1.
We first show that there exists
\displaystyle\delta>0
such that

\bar{B}_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0))\subset{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\,(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}).

Suppose that this is not the case.
Then there exist
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
and a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m\geq 1}
on
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0))\setminus{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

such that
\displaystyle d(x_{m},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\to 0
as
\displaystyle m\to\infty
.

Since
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}
is a finite set, there exists
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0)
for infinitely many
\displaystyle m
.
We may assume that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0)
for all
\displaystyle m
, and
there exists
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
such that
\displaystyle d(x_{m},x)\to 0
.
Then
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)=0
, since
\displaystyle x_{m}\to x
in
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
and
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x_{m})=0
for all
\displaystyle m
.

Since
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\neq\emptyset
in
\displaystyle|\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}|
,
at least one of the following three cases holds:

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\geq{\mathfrak{q}}
: This case
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is compact, thus
\displaystyle d({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})>0
.
Since
\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}=x
and
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
, we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\in U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
.
Since
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
(this follows from
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}<{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}
; see Definition 5.6 (2) in
[
10
]
),
we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
, contradicting our assumption.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
: This case
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is compact
since
\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}}
is an open embedding, and we obtain a contradiction as in the previous case.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}<{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}<\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
:
Since
\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}=x\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\displaystyle x_{m}\notin{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for every
\displaystyle m
,
we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\notin\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}}(U_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}})
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
.
On the other hand
\displaystyle x=\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}
satisfies
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)=0
and

\displaystyle D_{x}s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}:T_{x}U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}/T_{x}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\to({\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{x}/({\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{x}

is an isomorphism,
which implies that
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x_{m})\neq 0

for sufficiently large every
\displaystyle m
,
contradicting our assumption.

Step 2.

Taking
\displaystyle\delta
as in Step 1, we prove that there exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}\implies B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0))\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\quad(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}).

If this is not the case, there exist
a sequence
\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{m})_{m\geq 1}
of positive real numbers converging to
\displaystyle 0
,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
, and
a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m\geq 1}
such that

x_{m}\in(B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon_{m}})^{-1}(0)))\setminus{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

for every
\displaystyle m
.
Up to subsequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m}
has a limit
\displaystyle x
in
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}|
.
By Lemma
8.9
,
\displaystyle x\in\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0))
.
Also
\displaystyle x\in\bar{B}_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
, thus by Step 1 we get
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

There exists
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for infinitely many
\displaystyle m
,
then we may assume that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for all
\displaystyle m
, in particular
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
Then
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\neq\emptyset
,
thus at least one of the following three cases holds:

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\geq{\mathfrak{q}}
:
This case
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is compact, thus
\displaystyle d({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})>0
.
Since
\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}=x\in{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
,
we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\in U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
.
By
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
,
we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
, contradicting our assumption.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
:
Again
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is compact, and we obtain a contradiction as in the previous case.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}<{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}<\text{\rm dim}\,U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
:
Since
\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}x_{m}=x\in{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
and

\displaystyle x_{m}\notin{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for every
\displaystyle m\geq 1
,
we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\notin\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}}(U_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}})
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
.
Since the CF-perturbation
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon_{m}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
converges to
\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
in compact
\displaystyle C^{1}
-topology,

\displaystyle s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)=0
thus

\displaystyle D_{x}s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}:T_{x}U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}/T_{x}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\to({\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{x}/({\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{x}
is an isomorphism,
we obtain
\displaystyle x_{m}\notin\Pi(({\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{\varepsilon_{m}})^{-1}(0))
for sufficiently large
\displaystyle m
,
contradicting our assumption.

∎

Lemma 8.11
.

For sufficiently small
\displaystyle\delta>0
there holds

{\mathfrak{q}}<{\mathfrak{p}}\implies B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\subset U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}.

Proof
.

Suppose that this is not the case.
Then there exist
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}
,
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}
such that

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}<{\mathfrak{p}}
and a sequence
\displaystyle(\delta_{m})_{m}
converging to
\displaystyle 0

and a sequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m}
such that
\displaystyle x_{m}\in(B_{\delta_{m}}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}

for every
\displaystyle m
.
Then the sequence
\displaystyle(x_{m})_{m}
has a limit point
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\setminus U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}
,
then
\displaystyle d(x,{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})>0
,
contradicting
\displaystyle x_{m}\in B_{\delta_{m}}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\,(\forall m)
.
∎

8.2.2.
Definition and well-definedness

We start from data
\displaystyle X
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\omega}
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}
and
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}
.
We take the following choices
(our definition of partition of unity is slightly different from that of
[
10
]
, Definition 7.64).

•

A support system
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}<{\mathcal{K}}
.

•

A positive real number
\displaystyle\delta
such that

–

There exists
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
such that

0<\varepsilon^{\prime}<\varepsilon\implies B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon^{\prime}})^{-1}(0))\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\quad(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}).

–

\displaystyle 2\delta<d({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
such that
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\emptyset
.

–

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}<{\mathfrak{p}}\implies B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\subset U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

–

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\delta):=\{x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\mid d({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},x)\leq\delta\}\subset{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}}
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
.

•

Partition of unity
\displaystyle\chi=(\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}
of
\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},\delta)
.
Namely, the following conditions are satisfied:

–

\displaystyle\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:|{\mathcal{K}}|\to[0,1]
is a strongly smooth function for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Namely, for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}
can be extended to a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-function defined on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
in
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

–

\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
.

–

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}s_{{\mathfrak{p}}}^{-1}(0)\subset|{\mathcal{K}}|
.

Then we define

\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}):=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}).

\displaystyle(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

in the RHS makes sense, since for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
there holds

\text{\rm supp}\,\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\cap U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\delta)\subset{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

and
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
.
We need to recall the following definition (Definition 7.79 in
[
10
]
):

Definition 8.12
.

Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{X}}
be sets, and

\displaystyle(F_{a})_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}
be a family of maps such that

\displaystyle F_{a}:(0,\varepsilon_{a})\to{\mathcal{X}}
for each
\displaystyle a\in{\mathcal{A}}
.
We say that
\displaystyle F_{a}
is independent on choices of
\displaystyle a
in the sense of
\displaystyle\spadesuit
if the following holds:

\displaystyle\spadesuit
: For any
\displaystyle a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{A}}
, there exists
\displaystyle 0<\varepsilon_{0}<\min\{\varepsilon_{a_{1}},\varepsilon_{a_{2}}\}
such that

\displaystyle F_{a_{1}}(\varepsilon)=F_{a_{2}}(\varepsilon)
for every
\displaystyle\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
.

Lemma 8.13
.

The above definition of

\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})

is independent on choices of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}
,
\displaystyle\delta
,
\displaystyle\chi

in the sense of
\displaystyle\spadesuit
.

Proof
.

Let us take two choices
\displaystyle(({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime})^{i},\delta^{i},\chi^{i})_{i=1,2}
.
We are going to prove

\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

when
\displaystyle\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.

By our assumption,
\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}s^{-1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(0)
.
Thus, Lemma
8.9
implies that,
for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
and every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
,
there holds
\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=1

on
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap\Pi(({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})^{-1}(0))
.
Then we obtain

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}},\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}).

Then it is sufficient to show

(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})=(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}},\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}})

for any
