\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{1},{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
.
We may assume either
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}
or
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}\geq{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}
,
since otherwise
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\cap U_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}=\emptyset
, thus

\displaystyle\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\equiv 0
by
\displaystyle 2\delta<d({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}})
.
Also this equality is clear when
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}={\mathfrak{p}}_{2}
.
Thus we may assume that
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}<{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}
.

In the argument below,
we abbreviate

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}
by
\displaystyle i
. For example
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}
is abbreviated by
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
.
Let us introduce the following notations:

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{21}
denotes
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{1}|_{U_{21}}=(\varphi_{21})^{*}{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
.

•

\displaystyle f_{21}
denotes
\displaystyle f_{1}|_{U_{21}}=f_{2}\circ\varphi_{21}
.

•

\displaystyle\omega_{21}
denotes
\displaystyle\omega_{1}|_{U_{21}}=(\varphi_{21})^{*}\omega_{2}
.

•

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{21}
denotes
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{1}|_{U_{21}}=(\varphi_{21})^{*}{\mathfrak{S}}_{2}
.

Then there holds

(f_{1})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{1},\chi^{1}_{1}\chi^{2}_{2}\omega_{1},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})=(f_{21})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{21},\chi^{1}_{1}\chi^{2}_{2}\omega_{21},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{21})

since
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\chi^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\subset B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}})
and

\displaystyle B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}})\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\subset U_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}
.
On the other hand

(f_{2})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{2},\chi^{1}_{1}\chi^{2}_{2}\omega_{2},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})=(f_{21})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{21},\chi^{1}_{1}\chi^{2}_{2}\omega_{21},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{21})

when
\displaystyle\varepsilon
is sufficiently small,
since
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\chi^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\subset B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})
and

\displaystyle B_{\delta}({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})\cap\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0))\subset{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}

by Lemma
8.10
.
∎

Next we prove the invariance by GG-embedding
(for the definition of GG-embedding, see Definition 3.24 in
[
10
]
).
Lemma
8.14
below is an analogue of Proposition 9.16 in
[
10
]
.

Lemma 8.14
.

Let us consider
\displaystyle(\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i},{\mathcal{K}}_{i},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i},\widetriangle{f}_{i},\widetriangle{\omega}_{i})_{i=1,2}

and
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\Phi}
such that

•

For each
\displaystyle i\in\{1,2\}
, the tuple
\displaystyle(\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i},{\mathcal{K}}_{i},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i},\widetriangle{f}_{i},\widetriangle{\omega}_{i})

satisfies the conditions to define
\displaystyle(\widetriangle{f}_{i})_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i},\widetriangle{\omega}_{i},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{i})
for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
.

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{\Phi}:\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{1}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{2}}
is a GG-embedding.
Namely,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\Phi}=({\mathfrak{i}},(\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}})
where
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{i}}:{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}\to{\mathfrak{P}}_{2}
is an order preserving map,
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\widehat{\varphi}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}):({\mathcal{U}}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\to({\mathcal{U}}_{2})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})}
is an embedding of K-charts for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}
,
such that compatibilities in Definition 3.24 in
[
10
]
are satisfied.

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
and
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}
are compatible with
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\Phi}
(see Definition 9.3 (3), (4) in
[
10
]
).
Moreover
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\omega}_{1}=\widetriangle{\Phi}^{*}\widetriangle{\omega}_{2}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}_{1}=\widetriangle{f}_{2}\circ\widetriangle{\Phi}
.

Then, for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, there holds

(\widetriangle{f}_{1})_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},\widetriangle{\omega}_{1},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})=(\widetriangle{f}_{2})_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},\widetriangle{\omega}_{2},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{2}).

Proof
.

Let us take a support system
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{i}
of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{i}}
for
\displaystyle i=1,2
such that:

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{i}<{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
for
\displaystyle i=1,2
.

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{2}
are compatible with
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\Phi}
.
Namely,
\displaystyle\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\subset{\mathring{({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{2})}}_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})}
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}
.

First we need Lemma
8.15
below,
whose proof is almost the same as the proof of Proposition 7.67 in
[
10
]
.
In the statement, we take a metric on
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
and
its pullback to
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{1}|
via a natural embedding map

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{1}|\to|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
.
This embedding map exists since

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
are compatible with
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\Phi}
.

Lemma 8.15
.

For any
\displaystyle\delta>0
, there exists

\displaystyle\chi^{+}_{1}=((\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}

which satisfies the following conditions:

•

\displaystyle(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is a strongly smooth map from
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
to
\displaystyle[0,1]
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\subset B_{\delta}(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}))
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}
.
Here
\displaystyle B_{\delta}
denotes the
\displaystyle\delta
-neighborhood in
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
.

•

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{2}}s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0)\subset|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
.

Proof
.

There exists a natural homeomorphism
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{2}}s_{{\mathfrak{q}}}^{-1}(0)\to X
.
In the following argument we identify these two spaces, and in particular consider
\displaystyle X
as a subspace of
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
.
First we need the following fact, which is essentially the same as Lemma 7.66 in
[
10
]
:

(
\displaystyle\star
): For any open set
\displaystyle W
of
\displaystyle|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|
containing a compact subset
\displaystyle K
of
\displaystyle X
,
there exists a strongly smooth function
\displaystyle g:|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|\to[0,1]
that has a compact support
contained in
\displaystyle W
and
\displaystyle 1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle K
.

Applying the claim (
\displaystyle\star
) to
\displaystyle K:=\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
and
\displaystyle W:=B_{\delta}(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}))
,
we obtain
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|\to[0,1]
.
Applying the claim (
\displaystyle\star
) to
\displaystyle K:=X
and

\displaystyle W:=\bigg\{x\in|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|\,\bigg|\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)>1/2\bigg\}
,
we obtain
\displaystyle g:|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|\to[0,1]
.
Finally, for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}

we define
\displaystyle(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:|{\mathcal{K}}_{2}|\to[0,1]
by

(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(x):=\begin{cases}g(x)f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(x)\biggl(\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}f_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(x)\biggr)^{-1}&(x\in W)\\
0&(x\notin W).\end{cases}

∎

For each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}
, we define
\displaystyle(\chi_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:|{\mathcal{K}}_{1}|\to[0,1]
by

(\chi_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}:=(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}|_{({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{q}}}}.

Then
\displaystyle\chi_{1}=((\chi_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}
is a partition of unity of

\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1},\delta)
.
We also take a partition of unitiy of

\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{2},\delta)
, which we denote by
\displaystyle\chi_{2}=((\chi_{2})_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{2}}
.

Now we can complete the proof as follows:

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\widetriangle{f}_{1})_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},\widetriangle{\omega}_{1},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}((f_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}(({\mathcal{U}}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}},(\chi_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(\omega_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}},({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}}((f_{2})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})})_{*}(({\mathcal{U}}_{2})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})},(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})}(\omega_{2})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})},({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{2}}((f_{2})_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{*}(({\mathcal{U}}_{2})_{{\mathfrak{q}}},(\chi_{2})_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\omega_{2})_{{\mathfrak{q}}},({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{{\mathfrak{q}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=(\widetriangle{f}_{2})_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},\widetriangle{\omega}_{2},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{2}).

The first and fourth equality follows from the definition.
The second equality holds since

\displaystyle(\omega_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})^{*}((\omega_{2})_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})})\,(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1})
and

\text{\rm supp}\,(\chi^{+}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap\Pi(({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})^{-1}_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})}(0))\subset B_{\delta}(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(({\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}))\cap\Pi(({\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})^{-1}_{{\mathfrak{i}}({\mathfrak{p}})}(0))\subset\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(({\mathcal{K}}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

when
\displaystyle\delta
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon
are sufficiently small
(this can be proved by arguments similar to the proof of Lemma
8.10
).
Proof of the third equality is similar to the proof of Lemma
8.13
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
8.14
.
∎

Let us state and prove Stokes’ formula.

Proposition 8.16
.

For sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, there holds

\partial(\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}))=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},d\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}).

Proof
.

Take
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime}<{\mathcal{K}}
,
\displaystyle\delta>0
and let
\displaystyle(\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}
be a partition of unity of
\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{K}}^{\prime},\delta)
.
When
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small,

\displaystyle\displaystyle\partial(\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}))-(-1)^{|\omega|+1}\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},d\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},d\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}d\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}d\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\omega_{{\mathfrak{q}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})

\displaystyle\displaystyle\quad=(-1)^{|\omega|+1}\sum_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}(f_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{*}({\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}},\chi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}(\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}d\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\omega_{{\mathfrak{q}}},{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{q}}})=0.

The first equality follows from Stokes’ formula for K-charts:
Lemma
8.2
(iii).
The second and fifth equality holds since
\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\chi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=1
on

\displaystyle\Pi((\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}(0))
when
\displaystyle\varepsilon
is sufficiently small.
The third equality holds by the argument similar to the proof of Lemma
8.13
.
The fourth equality is obvious.
∎

8.3.
K-space

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a compact, oriented K-space with a strongly smooth map

\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
,
a differential form
\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}
on
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
and a CF-perturbation

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}=(\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}
on
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})

which is transversal to
\displaystyle 0
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}
.
Under these assumptions we define a de Rham chain

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\text{\rm dR}}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{k+1})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, and check Stokes’ formula and fiber product formula.
We only consider K-spaces
without
boundaries,
since generalization to admissible K-spaces with boundaries (and corners)
will be straightforward.

By Lemma 9.10 in
[
10
]
,
there exist

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
: GCS of
\displaystyle X
,

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{\omega}
: differential form on
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}
: strongly smooth map from
\displaystyle(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}})
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
,

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}
: support system of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}
: CF-perturbation of
\displaystyle(\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},{\mathcal{K}})
which is transversal to
\displaystyle 0
, and

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}\circ\widetriangle{f}:(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strongly submersive with respect to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}
,

•

strict KG-embedding
\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}:\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{0}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
, where
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
is an open substructure of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
,

satisfying the following compatibilities:

•

\displaystyle\widehat{\omega}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}=\widehat{\Phi}^{*}(\widetriangle{\omega})
,

•

\displaystyle\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}=\widetriangle{f}\circ\widehat{\Phi}
,

•

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}=\widehat{\Phi}^{*}(\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}})
.

Then we define

(65)

\widehat{f}_{*}(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}},\widehat{\omega},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}}):=\widetriangle{f}_{*}(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}},\widetriangle{\omega},\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}^{\varepsilon}})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
.
Well-definedness
(i.e. the RHS of (
65
) does not depend on choices of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\omega}
, etc. in the sense of
\displaystyle\spadesuit
)
follows from invariance by GG-embedding (Lemma
8.14
)
and arguments in
[
10
]
Section 9.2
(proof of Proposition 9.16
\displaystyle\implies
Theorem 9.14).
Stokes’ formula in this setting (Theorem
7.5
)
follows from Stokes’ formula for GCS (Theorem
8.16
).

Finally we give a sketch of the proof
of the fiber product formula (Theorem
7.6
),
imitating the proof of Proposition 10.23 in
[
10
]
.
Namely, we prove

(66)

(\widehat{f}_{12})_{*}(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12},\widehat{\omega}_{12},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{12}^{\varepsilon})=(\widehat{f}_{1})_{*}(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1},\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{1}^{\varepsilon})\circ_{j}(\widehat{f}_{2})_{*}(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2},\widehat{\omega}_{2},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{2}^{\varepsilon})

for sufficiently small
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
, where
\displaystyle(X_{12},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})
denotes the fiber product of

\displaystyle(X_{1},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})
and
\displaystyle(X_{2},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2})
.
For each
\displaystyle i\in\{1,2\}
, there exist a GCS
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}
on
\displaystyle X_{i}
and a KG-embedding

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}
,
namely a strict KG-embedding

\displaystyle(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})_{0}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}
,
where
\displaystyle(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})_{0}
is an open substructure of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i}
.
Let
\displaystyle(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})_{0}
denote the fiber product of

\displaystyle(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})_{0}
and
\displaystyle(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2})_{0}
.
One may assume that, for each
\displaystyle i\in\{1,2\}

there exist
\displaystyle\widetriangle{\omega}_{i}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}_{i}
,
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{i}
satisfying compatibilities.

Let
\displaystyle\chi^{i}=(\chi^{i}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{i}}
be a partition of unity of
\displaystyle(X_{i},\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})
.
For each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{i}
,

\displaystyle\chi^{i}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}
induces a strongly smooth map

\displaystyle\widehat{\chi}^{i}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}:(X_{i},\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{i})\to[0,1]
,
and there holds

\displaystyle\sum_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{i}}\widehat{\chi}^{i}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{i}}=1
.
Then (
66
) reduces to proving

(67)

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\widehat{f}_{12})_{*}(X_{12},(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})_{0},\widehat{\chi}^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\widehat{\chi}^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\widehat{\omega}_{12},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{12})=

\displaystyle\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(\widehat{f}_{1})_{*}(X_{1},(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})_{0},\widehat{\chi}^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})\circ_{j}(\widehat{f}_{2})_{*}(X_{2},(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2})_{0},\widehat{\chi}^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\widehat{\omega}_{2},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})

for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{1}
and
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{2}
.
Now there holds

(\widehat{f}_{1})_{*}(X_{1},(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{1})_{0},\widehat{\chi}^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\widehat{\omega}_{1},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{1})=((f_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}})_{*}(({\mathcal{U}}_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},(\chi_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}(\omega_{1})_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}},({\mathfrak{S}}_{1})^{\varepsilon}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}),

and similar formulas hold for
\displaystyle(\widehat{f}_{12})_{*}(X_{12},(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{12})_{0},\widehat{\chi}^{1}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{1}}\widehat{\chi}^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\widehat{\omega}_{12},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{12})

and
\displaystyle(\widehat{f}_{2})_{*}(X_{2},(\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}_{2})_{0},\widehat{\chi}^{2}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{2}}\widehat{\omega}_{2},\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}^{\varepsilon}_{2})
.
Then (
67
) follows from the fiber product formula for single K-charts (Lemma
8.3
).
This completes a sketch of the proof of Theorem
7.6
.

9.
Proof of
\displaystyle C^{0}
-approximation lemma (Theorem
7.31
)

Recall that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k+1}
is a subspace of
\displaystyle\Pi^{k+1}
,
which consists of
\displaystyle(\Gamma_{0},\ldots,\Gamma_{k})\in\Pi^{k+1}
such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(\Gamma_{i})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(\Gamma_{i+1})\,(0\leq i\leq k-1)
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{1}(\Gamma_{k})=\text{\rm ev}\,_{0}(\Gamma_{0})
.
Then, Theorem
7.31
is reduced to Lemma
9.1
below.
We first give a proof of Lemma
9.1
assuming
Lemma
9.2
(which is stated in the proof),
and proceed to a proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a compact K-space and

\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
be a strongly continuous map such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to L
is strongly smooth for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

Let
\displaystyle Z
be a closed subset of
\displaystyle X
and
\displaystyle\widehat{g}:(Z,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{Z})\to\Pi

be a strongly smooth map
(the notion of smooth map from a K-space to
\displaystyle\Pi
is defined in the same way as Definition
7.1
)
such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{g}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{f}|_{Z}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
and

\displaystyle\widehat{g}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widehat{f}|_{Z}
(with respect to
\displaystyle d_{\Pi}
).

If
\displaystyle\varepsilon<\rho_{L}
, there exist an open substructure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}

and a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}})\to\Pi

such that the following conditions hold:

•

\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{g^{\prime}}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

•

\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}=\widehat{g}
on
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}|_{Z}
.

Proof
.

Step 1.

There exist

•

a GCS
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
of
\displaystyle Z
,

•

a KG-embedding from
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{Z}
to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{Z}
,
namely an open substructure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}
of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}|_{Z}
and a strict KG-embedding

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}:\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
,

•

a strongly continuous map
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f_{Z}}:(Z,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{Z})\to\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
,

•

a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widetriangle{g}:(Z,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}_{Z})\to\Pi
,

such that

•

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{*}\widetriangle{f}_{Z}=\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{*}\widetriangle{g}=\widehat{g}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}}
,

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{g}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}_{Z}
,

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widetriangle{g}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widetriangle{f}_{Z}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

The GCS
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
exists by Theorem 3.30 in
[
10
]
.
By inspecting its proof (Section 11.1 in
[
10
]
),
one can take
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
so that each chart of

\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
is an open subchart of a certain K-chart of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
,
thus one can define
\displaystyle\widetriangle{g}
and
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}_{Z}
by pulling back
\displaystyle\widehat{f}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{g}
.

Step 2.

By Proposition 7.52 and Lemma 7.53 in
[
10
]
, there exist

•

a GCS
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
of
\displaystyle X
.

•

a KG-embedding from
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,
namely and open substructure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0,+}}
of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}

and a strict KG-embedding
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}:\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0,+}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,

•

an extension from
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
(see Definition 7.50 of
[
10
]
),
namely an open substructure
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}
of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}

and a strict extension
\displaystyle\Phi_{3}:\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,

•

a strongly continuous map
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}:(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}})\to\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,

such that
\displaystyle\Phi_{2}^{*}\widetriangle{f}=\widehat{f}|_{\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0,+}}}

and
\displaystyle\Phi_{3}^{*}\widetriangle{f}=\widetriangle{f_{Z}}|_{\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}}
.

Now we can state a
\displaystyle C^{0}
-approximation result for GCS
in Lemma
9.2
below,
which we assume for the moment.

Lemma 9.2
.

There exist

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,00}}
: an open substructure of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}
,

•

\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
: an open substructure of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,

•

a strict extension from
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,00}}
to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
,

•

a strongly smooth map
\displaystyle\widetriangle{g^{\prime}}:(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}})\to\Pi
such that

–

\displaystyle\widetriangle{g^{\prime}}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{f}|_{\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}}
,

–

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widetriangle{g^{\prime}}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ\widetriangle{f}\,(j=0,1)
on
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
,

–

\displaystyle\widetriangle{g^{\prime}}=\widetriangle{g}
on
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,00}}
.

Step 3.

Take an open substructure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
of
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
with a strict KG-embedding
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}

such that
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}|_{Z}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
factors through a strict KG-embedding
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}|_{Z}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,00}}
.
Finally, we can define
\displaystyle\widehat{g^{\prime}}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}})\to\Pi
by pulling back
\displaystyle\widetriangle{g^{\prime}}:(X,\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}})\to\Pi

by
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{0}}
.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Lemma
9.2
.
First we need to prove Lemmas
9.3
,
9.4
and
9.5
.

Lemma 9.3
.

For any nonempty finite set
\displaystyle I
, there exists a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-map

G:\{(x_{i},t_{i})_{i\in I}\mid x_{i}\in L,\,t_{i}\in[0,1],\,\max_{i,j\in I}d_{L}(x_{i},x_{j})<\rho_{L},\,\sum_{i\in I}t_{i}=1\}\to L

such that the following properties hold:

(i):

If
\displaystyle t_{i}=\begin{cases}1&(i=i_{0})\\
0&(i\neq i_{0})\end{cases}
for some
\displaystyle i_{0}\in I
, then
\displaystyle G(x_{i},t_{i})_{i}=x_{i_{0}}
.

(ii):

If there exists
\displaystyle y\in L
and
\displaystyle r\in(0,\rho_{L}]
such that
\displaystyle d_{L}(y,x_{i})<r\,(\forall i\in I)
, then

\displaystyle d_{L}(y,G(x_{i},t_{i})_{i})<r
.

Proof
.

We fix an arbitrary total order on
\displaystyle I
.
For each
\displaystyle t=(t_{i})_{i\in I}
, let
\displaystyle i_{0}:=\max\{i\mid t_{i}\neq 0\}
,
and for every
\displaystyle\theta\in[0,1]
let

(t^{\theta})_{i}:=\begin{cases}t_{i}(1-\theta t_{i_{0}})/(1-t_{i_{0}})&(i<i_{0})\\
\theta t_{i_{0}}&(i=i_{0})\\
0&(i>i_{0}).\end{cases}

Now we define
\displaystyle G
so that it satisfies (i),
and for any
\displaystyle x=(x_{i})_{i}
, the map

[0,1]\to L;\,\theta\mapsto G(x,t^{\theta})

is the shortest geodesic connecting the end points.
Now
\displaystyle G
satisfies (ii) since
any geodesic ball with radius
\displaystyle r\in(0,\rho_{L}]
is geodesically convex,
by the definition of
\displaystyle\rho_{L}
(see Section 7.3).
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

For any
\displaystyle(T,\gamma)\in\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon>0
,
there exists
\displaystyle(T^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\in\Pi
such that

\displaystyle d_{\Pi}((T,\gamma),(T^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}))<\varepsilon
.

Proof
.

Take
\displaystyle T^{\prime}:=T+\varepsilon/2
, and

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}:[0,T^{\prime}]\to L
so that
\displaystyle d_{L}(\gamma(sT),\gamma^{\prime}(sT^{\prime}))<\varepsilon/2
for any
\displaystyle s\in[0,1]
,
which is possible since
\displaystyle C^{\infty}([0,1],L)
is dense in
\displaystyle C^{0}([0,1],L)
with respect to the
\displaystyle C^{0}
-topology.
∎

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
\displaystyle U
be a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold,

\displaystyle f:U\to\Pi^{\text{\rm con}}\,
be a continuous map,
such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f:U\to L
is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

Let
\displaystyle V
be a submanifold of
\displaystyle U
,

\displaystyle g:V\to\Pi
be a smooth map such that

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ g=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f|_{V}\,(j=0,1)
and

\displaystyle g
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f|_{V}
.

Then for any
\displaystyle x\in V
, there exists an open neighborhood
\displaystyle W
of
\displaystyle x
in
\displaystyle U

and a smooth map
\displaystyle g^{\prime}:W\to\Pi
such that

\displaystyle g^{\prime}=g
on
\displaystyle W\cap V
,

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ g^{\prime}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f|_{W}\,(j=0,1)
,
and
\displaystyle g^{\prime}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f|_{W}
.

Proof
.

The last condition “
\displaystyle g^{\prime}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f|_{W}
” can be achieved by taking
\displaystyle W
sufficiently small,
since
\displaystyle f
is continuous and
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-closeness is an open condition.
Thus it is sufficient to define
\displaystyle W
and a smooth map
\displaystyle g^{\prime}:W\to\Pi
such that

\displaystyle g^{\prime}=g
on
\displaystyle W\cap V
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ g^{\prime}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f|_{W}\,(j=0,1)
.

Let
\displaystyle W
be a sufficiently small neighborhood of
\displaystyle x

, so that there exists a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-map
\displaystyle r:W\to W\cap V
satisfying
\displaystyle r|_{W\cap V}=\text{\rm id}_{W\cap V}
, and

d_{L}(\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}(f(y)),\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}(g\circ r(y)))<r_{\text{inj}}(L)

for every
\displaystyle y\in W
and
\displaystyle j\in\{0,1\}
, where
\displaystyle r_{\text{inj}}(L)
denotes the injectivity radius of
\displaystyle L
.
We define
\displaystyle\xi_{j}(y)\in T_{\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}(g\circ r(y))}L
by

\displaystyle\exp(\xi_{j}(y))=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}(f(y))
.

We set
\displaystyle g(z):=(T(z),\gamma(z))
for any
\displaystyle z\in W\cap V
.
For every
\displaystyle y\in W
and
\displaystyle j\in\{0,1\}
,
we define
\displaystyle\xi^{y}_{j}(\theta)\in T_{\gamma(r(y))(\theta)}L\,(0\leq\theta\leq T(r(y)))
so that

\xi^{y}_{0}(0)=\xi_{0}(y),\quad\nabla_{\theta}\xi^{y}_{0}\equiv 0,\quad\xi^{y}_{1}(T(r(y))=\xi_{1}(y),\quad\nabla_{\theta}\xi^{y}_{1}\equiv 0.

Taking a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-function
\displaystyle\chi:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that

\displaystyle\chi\equiv 0
near
\displaystyle 0
and

\displaystyle\chi\equiv 1
near
\displaystyle 1
,
we define

\displaystyle\xi^{\prime}(y)\in C^{\infty}(\gamma(r(y))^{*}TL)
by

\xi^{\prime}(y)(\theta):=\chi(\theta/T(r(y)))\cdot\xi^{y}_{1}(\theta)+(1-\chi(\theta/T(r(y))))\cdot\xi^{y}_{0}(\theta)\qquad(0\leq\theta\leq T(r(y))).

Finally, we define

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}(y):[0,T(r(y))]\to L
by

\gamma^{\prime}(y)(\theta):=\exp(\gamma(r(y))(\theta),\xi^{\prime}(y)(\theta))

and

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(y):=(T(r(y)),\gamma^{\prime}(y))
.
It is easy to check that this map
\displaystyle g^{\prime}
is smooth and satisfies required conditions.
∎

Now let us start the proof of Lemma
9.2
.
Let
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}
denote the index set of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
,
and
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\subset{\mathfrak{P}}
denote the index set of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z}}
.
Let
\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}
be K-charts of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
, and we denote
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=(U_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\ldots)
for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
.
Let
\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{U}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}}
be K-charts of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}
and we denote
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=(U^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\ldots)
for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}
.
We also take support systems
\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}
of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}

and
\displaystyle\{{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}}
of
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}
,
such that

\displaystyle(\varphi_{3})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}({\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\subset{\mathring{{\mathcal{K}}}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}

for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}
,
where
\displaystyle\Phi_{3}=(\varphi_{3},\widehat{\varphi}_{3})
is a strict extension from
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0}}
to
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
.

A subset
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}\subset{\mathfrak{P}}
is called a
filter

if
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
,
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\geq{\mathfrak{q}}
,
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
imply
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
.
In particular the empty set is a filter (see Section 12.3 in
[
10
]
).
Now Lemma
9.2
is proved by applying Lemma
9.6
below for
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}={\mathfrak{P}}
,
and setting

({\mathcal{U}}_{0})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:={\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\,(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}),\quad({\mathcal{U}}_{Z,00})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:=({\mathcal{U}}_{Z,0})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{W_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}\,(\forall{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}).

Lemma 9.6
.

For any filter
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}
of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{P}}
,
there exist

\displaystyle(V_{{\mathfrak{p}}},g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}}
and
\displaystyle(W_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}}

such that the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is an open neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
in
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
.

•

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}:V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\to\Pi
is a smooth map.
Moreover,
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
and
\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

•

\displaystyle(g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}}
is compatible with coordinate changes.
Specifically, for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}},{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
such that
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\geq{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}
,
there holds
\displaystyle g_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}=g_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}
on
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}\cap\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{p}}^{\prime}}^{-1}(V_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
.

•

\displaystyle W_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
is an open neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
in
\displaystyle U^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}
.

•

\displaystyle(g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}}
is compatible with
\displaystyle\widetriangle{g}
. Specifically:

–

For any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\cap{\mathfrak{F}}
, there holds
\displaystyle(\varphi_{3})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}(W_{{\mathfrak{p}}})\subset V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
and
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=g_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\circ(\varphi_{3})_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
\displaystyle W_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
.

–

For any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus{\mathfrak{F}}
and
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
satisfying
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}<{\mathfrak{q}}
, there holds

\displaystyle g_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}}=g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
on
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}})^{-1}(W_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
.

Proof
.

The proof is by induction on the cardinality of
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}
.
There is nothing to prove when
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}=\emptyset
.
To discuss the induction step, let
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{F}}
be a filter,
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}
be its maximal element
(namely
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}},{\mathfrak{p}}\geq{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\implies{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{p}}_{0}
),
and suppose that there exist
\displaystyle(V_{{\mathfrak{p}}},g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}})_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}}
and
\displaystyle(W_{{\mathfrak{q}}})_{{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}}

satisfying the conditions in the lemma.

For each
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
, we take an open neighborhood
\displaystyle W_{x}
of
\displaystyle x
in
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}

and a smooth map
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}:W_{x}\to\Pi
in the way described below.
We consider three cases (here we consider
\displaystyle x
as a point in
\displaystyle|\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}|=\bigsqcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}/\sim
, and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
,
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
as subspaces of
\displaystyle|\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}|
).

(i):

There exists
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
such that
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

(ii):

\displaystyle x\notin{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
, but there exists
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}

such that
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

(iii):

\displaystyle x\notin{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
, and
\displaystyle x\notin{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}
.

In case (i), take maximal
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
such that
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
This condition implies
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}>{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}
since
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap U_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}\neq\emptyset
and
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}
.
Then take
\displaystyle W_{x}
such that:

•

\displaystyle\overline{W_{x}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
which does
not
satisfy
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}

(note this condition implies that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
),

•

\displaystyle W_{x}\subset(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}})^{-1}(W_{{\mathfrak{q}}})
.

Then we define
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}:W_{x}\to\Pi
by

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}:=g_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}|_{W_{x}}
.

In case (ii), take maximal
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}
such that
\displaystyle x\in{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.
This condition implies
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}<{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}
.
Then take
\displaystyle W_{x}
such that:

•

\displaystyle\overline{W_{x}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}
which does
not
satisfy
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}

(note this condition implies that
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
),

•

\displaystyle\overline{W_{x}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}}=\emptyset
for any
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
,

•

\displaystyle(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}{\mathfrak{q}}})^{-1}(W_{x})\subset V_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
.

When
\displaystyle W_{x}
is sufficiently small, Lemma
9.5
shows that there exists a smooth map
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}:W_{x}\to\Pi
such that

•

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}|_{W_{x}}
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ g^{\prime}_{x}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}|_{W_{x}}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

•

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}\circ\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}{\mathfrak{q}}}=g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
on
\displaystyle(\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}{\mathfrak{q}}})^{-1}(W_{x})
.

In case (iii), take
\displaystyle W_{x}
so that

\displaystyle\overline{W_{x}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\emptyset
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}
, and

\displaystyle\overline{W_{x}}\cap{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}=\emptyset
for every
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}\setminus({\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\})
.
When
\displaystyle W_{x}
is sufficiently small,
Lemmas
9.4
and
9.5
(applied to
\displaystyle V=\{x\}
)
show that there exists a smooth map
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}:W_{x}\to\Pi
such that

•

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x}
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}|_{W_{x}}
.

•

\displaystyle\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ g^{\prime}_{x}=\text{\rm ev}\,_{j}\circ f_{{\mathfrak{p}}}|_{W_{x}}
for
\displaystyle j=0,1
.

Since
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
is compact,
one can take finitely many points
\displaystyle\{x_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that

\displaystyle\{W_{x_{i}}\}_{i\in I}
covers
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
.
For each
\displaystyle i\in I
, we take

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x_{i}}:W_{x_{i}}\to\Pi
and denote it as

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x_{i}}=(\gamma_{i},T_{i})
.
Let us take a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-function
\displaystyle\chi_{i}:U_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{\geq 0}
for each
\displaystyle i\in I
,
such that
\displaystyle\text{\rm supp}\,\chi_{i}\subset W_{x_{i}}

and
\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{i}=1
on a neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
, which we denote by
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}
.
Then we define
\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}:V_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}\to\Pi
by

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}:=(T,\gamma)
, such that

\displaystyle T:=\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{i}T_{i}
,
and for every
\displaystyle y\in V_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}

\gamma(y):[0,T(y)]\to L;\quad\theta\mapsto G(\gamma_{i}(y)(T_{i}(y)\theta/T(y)),\chi_{i}(y))_{i\in I}

where
\displaystyle G
is defined in Lemma
9.3
.
If
\displaystyle i\in I
satisfies
\displaystyle y\in V_{x_{i}}
,
then

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{x_{i}}(y)
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}(y)
,
and
\displaystyle\varepsilon<\rho_{L}
, thus

\displaystyle g^{\prime}_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}(y)
is
\displaystyle\varepsilon
-close to
\displaystyle f_{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}}(y)
.

Now we can finish the proof by replacing
\displaystyle V_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
with a smaller neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{F}}\setminus\{{\mathfrak{p}}_{0}\}
,
and
\displaystyle W_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
with a smaller neighborhood of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{K}}^{Z}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}
for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}_{Z}
.
∎

10.
Some basic notions in the theory of Kuranishi structures

Here we recall some basic notions
in the theory of Kuranishi structures (abbreviated as K-structures),
mainly to fix notations used throughout this paper.
When we use notions which are not recalled here,
we directly refer to
[
10
]
.
Throughout this section,

\displaystyle X
denotes a separable, metrizable topological space.

Kuranishi chart (K-chart)

A
K-chart
of
\displaystyle X
is a tuple

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}=(U,{\mathcal{E}},s,\psi)
such that:

•

\displaystyle U
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold,

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-vector bundle on
\displaystyle U
,

•

\displaystyle s
is a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-section of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}
,

•

\displaystyle\psi:s^{-1}(0)\to X
is a homeomorphism onto an open set of
\displaystyle X
.

\displaystyle\text{\rm dim}\,{\mathcal{U}}:=\text{\rm dim}\,U-\text{\rm rk}\,{\mathcal{E}}
is called the dimension of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}
.
An orientation of
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}
is a pair of orientations of
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}
.
A K-chart at
\displaystyle p\in X
is a K-chart
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}=(U,{\mathcal{E}},s,\psi)
such that

\displaystyle p\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi
. We denote
\displaystyle o_{p}:=\psi^{-1}(p)\in s^{-1}(0)
.

Remark 10.1
.

In the standard definition (see Definition 3.1 in
[
10
]
),
one assumes that

\displaystyle U
is an orbifold and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}
is an orbibundle.
However,
in the present paper we are working with pseudo-holomorphic disks
without
sphere bubbles,
thus we do not need to take quotients by finite group actions on moduli spaces,
hence it is sufficient to work with vector bundles on manifolds.

Embedding of K-charts

Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}=(U_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},s_{i},\psi_{i})\,(i=1,2)
be K-charts of
\displaystyle X
.
An
embedding
of K-charts
\displaystyle\Phi:{\mathcal{U}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{2}

is a pair
\displaystyle\Phi=(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi})
such that:

•

\displaystyle\varphi:U_{1}\to U_{2}
is an embedding of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifolds,

•

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}:{\mathcal{E}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{E}}_{2}
is an embedding of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-vector bundles over
\displaystyle\varphi
,

•

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi}\circ s_{1}=s_{2}\circ\varphi
,

•

\displaystyle\psi_{2}\circ\varphi=\psi_{1}
on
\displaystyle s_{1}^{-1}(0)
,

•

for any
\displaystyle x\in s_{1}^{-1}(0)
, the covariant derivative

(68)

D_{\varphi(x)}s_{2}:\frac{T_{\varphi(x)}U_{2}}{(D_{x}\varphi)(T_{x}U_{1})}\to\frac{({\mathcal{E}}_{2})_{\varphi(x)}}{\widehat{\varphi}(({\mathcal{E}}_{1})_{x})}

is an isomorphism.

When K-charts
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
and
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
are oriented,
we say that
\displaystyle\Phi=(\varphi,\widehat{\varphi})
is orientation preserving,
if an isomorphism

\det TU_{2}\otimes(\det TU_{1})^{\vee}\cong\det{\mathcal{E}}_{2}\otimes(\det{\mathcal{E}}_{1})^{\vee}

induced by (
68
) preserves orientations.

Coordinate changes

Let
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{i}=(U_{i},{\mathcal{E}}_{i},s_{i},\psi_{i})\,(i=1,2)
be K-charts of
\displaystyle X
.
A
coordinate change
in weak sense (resp. strong sense)
from
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{1}
to
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
is a triple

\displaystyle\Phi_{21}=(U_{21},\varphi_{21},\widehat{\varphi}_{21})

satisfying (i) and (ii) (resp. (i), (ii) and (iii)):

(i):

\displaystyle U_{21}
is an open subset of
\displaystyle U_{1}
,

(ii):

\displaystyle(\varphi_{21},\widehat{\varphi}_{21})
is an embedding of K-charts
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{1}|_{U_{21}}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{2}
.

(iii):

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(s_{1}^{-1}(0)\cap U_{21})=\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{1}\cap\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{2}
.

Kuranishi structure (K-structure)

A
K-structure

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
of
\displaystyle X
(of dimension
\displaystyle d
) consists of

•

a K-chart (of dimension
\displaystyle d
)
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{p}=(U_{p},{\mathcal{E}}_{p},s_{p},\psi_{p})
at
\displaystyle p
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
,

•

a coordinate change in weak sense

\displaystyle\Phi_{pq}=(U_{pq},\varphi_{pq},\widehat{\varphi}_{pq}):{\mathcal{U}}_{q}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{p}

for every
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,(\psi_{p})
,

such that

•

\displaystyle o_{q}\in U_{pq}
for every
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
,

•

for every
\displaystyle p\in X
,
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
and
\displaystyle r\in\psi_{q}(s_{q}^{-1}(0)\cap U_{pq})
,
there holds

\displaystyle\Phi_{pr}|_{U_{pqr}}=\Phi_{pq}\circ\Phi_{qr}|_{U_{pqr}}

where
\displaystyle U_{pqr}:=\varphi^{-1}_{qr}(U_{pq})\cap U_{pr}
.

The pair
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
is called a space with Kuranishi structure
(abbreviated as a
K-space
)
of dimension
\displaystyle d
.
We say
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
is oriented if each K-chart
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{p}
is oriented and

\displaystyle\varphi_{pq}
preserves orientations for every
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
.

Remark 10.2
.

In Definition 3.11
[
10
]
the notion of a relative K-space
\displaystyle(X,Z;\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
is introduced,
however in this paper we only need the absolute case (
\displaystyle X=Z
).

Strongly continuous/smooth maps from K-spaces

Let
\displaystyle(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})
be a K-space and
\displaystyle Y
be a topological space.
A
strongly continuous map

\displaystyle\widehat{f}:(X,\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}})\to Y

assigns a continuous map

\displaystyle f_{p}:U_{p}\to Y
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
such that

\displaystyle f_{p}\circ\varphi_{pq}=f_{q}
on
\displaystyle U_{pq}
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
and
\displaystyle q\in\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{p}
.
When
\displaystyle Y
has a structure of a
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
-manifold,

\displaystyle\widehat{f}
is called

strongly smooth

if
\displaystyle f_{p}:U_{p}\to Y
is of
\displaystyle C^{\infty}
for every
\displaystyle p\in X
.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\widehat{f}
is called

weakly submersive
,
if
\displaystyle f_{p}
is a submersion for every
\displaystyle p\in X
.

Good coordinate system (GCS)

Finally we recall the definition of a

good coordinate system
(GCS).
A GCS
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
of
\displaystyle X
consists of

(({\mathfrak{P}},\leq),\{{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}},\{\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}\}_{{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}})

such that:

•

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{P}},\leq)
is a finite partially ordered set.

•

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=(U_{{\mathfrak{p}}},{\mathcal{E}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}},s_{{\mathfrak{p}}},\psi_{{\mathfrak{p}}})
is a K-chart for each
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}
, and
\displaystyle\bigcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}=X
.

•

\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}
is a coordinate change
\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\to{\mathcal{U}}_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
in strong sense.

•

If
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{r}}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}
,
there holds
\displaystyle\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{r}}}|_{U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{r}}}}=\Phi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}\circ\Phi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{r}}}|_{U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{r}}}}
,
where
\displaystyle U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{r}}}:=\varphi^{-1}_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{r}}}(U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}})\cap U_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{r}}}
.

•

If
\displaystyle\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\cap\text{\rm Im}\,\psi_{{\mathfrak{q}}}\neq\emptyset
, then either
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
or
\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}
holds.

•

Let us define a relation
\displaystyle\sim
on
\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}
as follows:

\displaystyle x\sim y
if and only if one of the following holds:

–

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{q}}
and
\displaystyle x=y
.

–

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{p}}\leq{\mathfrak{q}}
and
\displaystyle y=\varphi_{{\mathfrak{q}}{\mathfrak{p}}}(x)
.

–

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{q}}\leq{\mathfrak{p}}
and
\displaystyle x=\varphi_{{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{q}}}(y)
.

Then the relation
\displaystyle\sim
is an equivalence relation, and the quotient

\displaystyle\bigg(\bigsqcup_{{\mathfrak{p}}\in{\mathfrak{P}}}U_{{\mathfrak{p}}}\bigg)/\sim
,
equipped with the quotient topology, is Hausdorff.

The definitions of strongly continuous/smooth maps naturally extend to spaces with GCS.
Finally, there exists a natural notion of embeddings from a K-structure to a GCS (KG-embedding; see Definition 3.29 in
[
10
]
).
For any K-structure
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}
on a compact space
\displaystyle X
,
there exist a GCS
\displaystyle\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
and a KG-embedding
\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{U}}}\to\widetriangle{{\mathcal{U}}}
;
see Theorem 3.30 in
[
10
]
.
